\times U_{jk}(\vec{}x_{\perp})\conj{\psi_{kl}}(p^{+},k^{+},\vec{}r_{\perp})\biggl(-\frac{2ig_{\text{s}}}{2\pi}T_{lm}^{b}\frac{\vec{\epsilon}_{\lambda}\cdot\vec{}t_{\perp}}{t_{\perp}^{2}}\biggr)\herm{U_{mn}}(\vec{}y_{\perp})W^{ab}(\vec{}b_{\perp})\end{multline}\int^{k^{+}}\frac{\udc q^{+}}{q^{+}}\int\uddc\vec{}b_{\perp}\psi_{ni}(p^{+},k^{+},\vec{}r_{\perp})\biggl(\frac{2ig_{\text{s}}}{2\pi}T_{ij}^{a}\frac{\vec{\epsilon}_{\lambda}\cdot\vec{}s_{\perp}}{s_{\perp}^{2}}\biggr)\\
\times U_{jk}(\vec{}x_{\perp})\conj{\psi_{kl}}(p^{+},k^{+},\vec{}r_{\perp})\biggl(-\frac{2ig_{\text{s}}}{2\pi}T_{lm}^{b}\frac{\vec{\epsilon}_{\lambda}\cdot\vec{}t_{\perp}}{t_{\perp}^{2}}\biggr)\herm{U_{mn}}(\vec{}y_{\perp})W^{ab}(\vec{}b_{\perp})which reduces to
\begin{equation}-\frac{4\alpha_{\text{s}}}{\pi}\ln\frac{1}{x_{g}}\abs{\psi(\vec{}r_{\perp})}^{2}\trace[T^{a}U(\vec{}x_{\perp})T^{b}\herm{U}(\vec{}y_{\perp})]\int\frac{\uddc\vec{}b_{\perp}}{(2\pi)^{2}}\frac{\vec{}s_{\perp}\cdot\vec{}t_{\perp}}{s_{\perp}^{2}t_{\perp}^{2}}W^{ab}(\vec{}b_{\perp})\end{equation}\par When all seven diagrams in figure~\ref {fig:nlophotonxsec} are taken into account, the total NLO contribution, from explicit gluon emission, works out to
\begin{multline}-\frac{\alpha_{\text{s}}}{2\pi^{2}}\ln\frac{1}{x_{g}}\abs{\psi(\vec{}r_{\perp})}^{2}\int\uddc\vec{}b_{\perp}\frac{r_{\perp}^{2}}{s_{\perp}^{2}t_{\perp}^{2}}\Bigl\{N_{c}\trace[U(\vec{}x_{\perp})\herm U(\vec{}y_{\perp})]\\
-\trace[U(\vec{}x_{\perp})\herm U(\vec{}b_{\perp})]\trace[U(\vec{}b_{\perp})\herm U(\vec{}y_{\perp})]\Bigr\}\end{multline}-\frac{\alpha_{\text{s}}}{2\pi^{2}}\ln\frac{1}{x_{g}}\abs{\psi(\vec{}r_{\perp})}^{2}\int\uddc\vec{}b_{\perp}\frac{r_{\perp}^{2}}{s_{\perp}^{2}t_{\perp}^{2}}\Bigl\{N_{c}\trace[U(\vec{}x_{\perp})\herm U(\vec{}y_{\perp})]\\
-\trace[U(\vec{}x_{\perp})\herm U(\vec{}b_{\perp})]\trace[U(\vec{}b_{\perp})\herm U(\vec{}y_{\perp})]\Bigr\}\par Recall that the leading order contribution~\eqref{eq:lodipolexsec} was
\begin{equation*}\abs{\psi(p^{+},k^{+},\vec{}r_{\perp})}^{2}\trace[U(\vec{}x_{\perp})\herm U(\vec{}y_{\perp})]\end{equation*}This expression includes the contribution from emission of gluons with momentum fractions \emph{less} than $x_{g}$, which are resummed by the Wilson lines.
Therefore, the incremental contribution from emission of gluons with momentum fraction \emph{equal} to $x_{g}$, within a range $\Delta\bigl[\ln\frac{1}{x_{g}}\bigr]$, will be
\begin{equation}\abs{\psi(p^{+},k^{+},\vec{}r_{\perp})}^{2}\pd{\trace[U(\vec{}x_{\perp})\herm U(\vec{}y_{\perp})]}{\ln(1/x_{g})}\Delta\biggl[\ln\frac{1}{x_{g}}\biggr]\end{equation}However, this is precisely the same quantity represented by the single gluon emission contributions~\eqref{eq:nlodipolexsec}, if we take the difference between two closely spaced values of $x_{g}$:
\begin{multline}-\frac{\alpha_{\text{s}}}{2\pi^{2}}\Delta\biggl[\ln\frac{1}{x_{g}}\biggr]\abs{\psi(\vec{}r_{\perp})}^{2}\int\uddc\vec{}b_{\perp}\frac{r_{\perp}^{2}}{s_{\perp}^{2}t_{\perp}^{2}}\Bigl\{N_{c}\trace[U(\vec{}x_{\perp})\herm U(\vec{}y_{\perp})]\\
-\trace[U(\vec{}x_{\perp})\herm U(\vec{}b_{\perp})]\trace[U(\vec{}b_{\perp})\herm U(\vec{}y_{\perp})]\Bigr\}\end{multline}-\frac{\alpha_{\text{s}}}{2\pi^{2}}\Delta\biggl[\ln\frac{1}{x_{g}}\biggr]\abs{\psi(\vec{}r_{\perp})}^{2}\int\uddc\vec{}b_{\perp}\frac{r_{\perp}^{2}}{s_{\perp}^{2}t_{\perp}^{2}}\Bigl\{N_{c}\trace[U(\vec{}x_{\perp})\herm U(\vec{}y_{\perp})]\\
-\trace[U(\vec{}x_{\perp})\herm U(\vec{}b_{\perp})]\trace[U(\vec{}b_{\perp})\herm U(\vec{}y_{\perp})]\Bigr\}Equating expressions~\eqref{eq:bkleftside} and~\eqref{eq:bkrightside}, we find the following evolution equation for the Wilson loop:
\begin{multline}\pd{\trace[U(\vec{}x_{\perp})\herm U(\vec{}y_{\perp})]}{\ln(1/x_{g})}=-\frac{\alpha_{\text{s}}}{2\pi^{2}}\int\uddc\vec{}b_{\perp}\frac{r_{\perp}^{2}}{s_{\perp}^{2}t_{\perp}^{2}}\Bigl\{N_{c}\trace[U(\vec{}x_{\perp})\herm U(\vec{}y_{\perp})]\\
-\trace[U(\vec{}x_{\perp})\herm U(\vec{}b_{\perp})]\trace[U(\vec{}b_{\perp})\herm U(\vec{}y_{\perp})]\Bigr\}\end{multline}\pd{\trace[U(\vec{}x_{\perp})\herm U(\vec{}y_{\perp})]}{\ln(1/x_{g})}=-\frac{\alpha_{\text{s}}}{2\pi^{2}}\int\uddc\vec{}b_{\perp}\frac{r_{\perp}^{2}}{s_{\perp}^{2}t_{\perp}^{2}}\Bigl\{N_{c}\trace[U(\vec{}x_{\perp})\herm U(\vec{}y_{\perp})]\\
-\trace[U(\vec{}x_{\perp})\herm U(\vec{}b_{\perp})]\trace[U(\vec{}b_{\perp})\herm U(\vec{}y_{\perp})]\Bigr\}\par\par\lx@frontmatter@fallback\@@unnumbered@section{subsubsection}{toc}{CGC Target Averaging}
\par Technically, this equation only applies to one specific quantum state of the gluon field of the nucleus.
But we can't prepare a nucleus in a specific state; in a collider we get an average over all possible states.
So to get an equation that describes physically measurable results, we need to use the target averaging procedure $\braket{\cdots}_{x_{g}}$ to get
\begin{multline}\pd{\braket{\trace[U(\vec{}x_{\perp})\herm U(\vec{}y_{\perp})]}_{x_{g}}}{\ln(1/x_{g})}=-\frac{\alpha_{\text{s}}}{2\pi^{2}}\int\uddc\vec{}b_{\perp}\frac{r_{\perp}^{2}}{s_{\perp}^{2}t_{\perp}^{2}}\Bigl\{N_{c}\braket{\trace[U(\vec{}x_{\perp})\herm U(\vec{}y_{\perp})]}_{x_{g}}\\
-\braket{\trace[U(\vec{}x_{\perp})\herm U(\vec{}b_{\perp})]\trace[U(\vec{}b_{\perp})\herm U(\vec{}y_{\perp})]}_{x_{g}}\Bigr\}\end{multline}\pd{\braket{\trace[U(\vec{}x_{\perp})\herm U(\vec{}y_{\perp})]}_{x_{g}}}{\ln(1/x_{g})}=-\frac{\alpha_{\text{s}}}{2\pi^{2}}\int\uddc\vec{}b_{\perp}\frac{r_{\perp}^{2}}{s_{\perp}^{2}t_{\perp}^{2}}\Bigl\{N_{c}\braket{\trace[U(\vec{}x_{\perp})\herm U(\vec{}y_{\perp})]}_{x_{g}}\\
-\braket{\trace[U(\vec{}x_{\perp})\herm U(\vec{}b_{\perp})]\trace[U(\vec{}b_{\perp})\herm U(\vec{}y_{\perp})]}_{x_{g}}\Bigr\}or, in terms of the dipole~\eqref{eq:dipoleSdefinition} and quadrupole~\eqref{eq:quadrupoleSdefinition} gluon distributions,
\begin{equation}\pd{S_{x_{g}}^{(2)}(\vec{}r_{\perp})}{\ln(1/x_{g})}=-\frac{\alpha_{\text{s}}N_{c}}{2\pi^{2}}\int\uddc\vec{}b_{\perp}\frac{r_{\perp}^{2}}{s_{\perp}^{2}t_{\perp}^{2}}\Bigl[S_{x_{g}}^{(2)}(\vec{}r_{\perp})-S_{x_{g}}^{(4)}(\vec{}s_{\perp},\vec{}t_{\perp})\Bigr]\end{equation}This differential equation will allow us to compute the evolution of $S_{x_{g}}^{(2)}$ in $x_{g}$, provided that we can compute $S_{x_{g}}^{(4)}$.
So we might be inspired to look for a corresponding equation for $S_{x_{g}}^{(4)}$.
This equation does exist, but it expresses the evolution of $S_{x_{g}}^{(4)}$ in terms of $S_{x_{g}}^{(6)}$, a correlator of two fundamental and \emph{two} adjoint Wilson lines, $\trace[U\herm{U}W\herm{W}]$.
And the evolution equation for $S_{x_{g}}^{(6)}$ would be expressed in terms of $\trace[U\herm{U}WW\herm{W}]$, and so on.
This series of equations involving progressively larger Wilson loops is called the Balitsky-JIMWLK hierarchy~\cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{Balitsky:1995ub,JalilianMarian:1997jx,JalilianMarian:1997gr,Iancu:2000hn,Iancu:2001ad,Weigert:2000gi}{}{}]}.
\par In practice, to avoid the full complexity of the Balitsky-JIMWLK hierarchy, we can use the {\it mean field approximation}, which is simply the assumption that the two Wilson loops in the quadrupole gluon distribution are uncorrelated, and thus the target average of the product, $\braket{\trace[U\herm{U}]\trace[U\herm{U}]}_{x_{g}}$ factors into the product of two averages, $\braket{\trace[U\herm{U}]}_{x_{g}}\braket{\trace[U\herm{U}]}_{x_{g}}$.
The validity of this assumption in the limit of large $N_{c}$ is supported by numerical studies~\cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{Rummukainen:2003ns,Kovchegov:2008mk,Alvioli:2012ba}{}{}]}.
This allows us to reduce the quadrupole gluon distribution as follows:
\begin{equation}S_{x_{g}}^{(4)}(\vec{}x_{\perp},\vec{}b_{\perp},\vec{}y_{\perp})=S_{x_{g}}^{(2)}(\vec{}x_{\perp},\vec{}b_{\perp})S_{x_{g}}^{(2)}(\vec{}b_{\perp},\vec{}y_{\perp})\end{equation}so that the evolution equation is a self-contained differential equation for the dipole distribution
\begin{equation}\pd{S_{x_{g}}^{(2)}(\vec{}r_{\perp})}{\ln(1/x_{g})}=-\frac{\alpha_{\text{s}}N_{c}}{\pi}\int\uddc\vec{}b_{\perp}\frac{r_{\perp}^{2}}{s_{\perp}^{2}t_{\perp}^{2}}\Bigl[S_{x_{g}}^{(2)}(\vec{}r_{\perp})-S_{x_{g}}^{(2)}(\vec{}s_{\perp})S_{x_{g}}^{(2)}(\vec{}t_{\perp})\Bigr]\end{equation}This is called the Balitsky-Kovchegov (BK) equation~\cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{Balitsky:1995ub,Balitsky:1998kc,Balitsky:1998ya,Balitsky:2001re,Kovchegov:1999yj,Kovchegov:1999ua}{}{}]} in the leading logarithmic (LL) approximation.
\par The designation as LL reflects the fact that there is a single factor of $\alpha_{\text{s}}$ for each factor of $\ln\frac{1}{x_{g}}$.
One can go beyond this and incorporate subleading terms which are proportional to $\alpha_{\text{s}}\biggl(\alpha_{\text{s}}\ln\frac{1}{x_{g}}\biggr)^{n}$, producing the next-to-leading logarithmic (NLL) BK equation~\cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{Fadin:1998py,Ciafaloni:1998gs,Balitsky:2008zza}{}{}]}, though the numerical implementation of the full NLL BK evolution is quite complex.
Some progress has been made toward such an implementation but it is not yet ready for use in phenomenology.~\cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{HeikkiPrivate2014}{}{}]}
\par It turns out that incorporating selected NLL corrections, specifically those which result from the running of the QCD coupling, is expected to give a reasonable approximation to the full NLL evolution.
Many numerical studies, including those to be discussed in the remainder of this dissertation, use this rcBK (BK with running coupling) gluon distribution.
A widely used sample implementation is available in e.g. reference~\cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{Albacete:2009fh}{}{}]}.
\par\par\lx@frontmatter@fallback\@@unnumbered@section{subsection}{toc}{Numerical Analysis of BK Evolution}
\par\begin{figure}\hbox{\includegraphics[]{generated-figures/dipoleFplot}}
\@@toccaption{{\lx@tag[ ]{{13}}{GBW, BK, and rcBK gluon distributions, solved in momentum space}}}\@@caption{{\lx@tag[: ]{{\small Figure 13}}{\small The gluon distribution from the GBW model (top), and the output of the numerical BK evolution with fixed coupling (middle) and running coupling (bottom), for central ($c=0.85$) $\mathrm{pPb}${} ($A=208$) collisions. Each curve shows $\mathcal{F}_{x_{g}}(k_{\perp})$ for $x_{g}=x_{\text{init}}\exp(-n\delta Y)$, where $n$ is the number that labels the curve and $\delta Y=\frac{1}{400}\ln(10^{8})\approx 0.04605$ is the step size in rapidity used by the numerical integrator. The curve labeled $0$ is the initial condition. Notable features include the transient ``spike'' at the low $k_{\perp}^{2}$ region of the solution to the BK evolution (which is a numerical artifact), and the significant enhancement at large $k_{\perp}^{2}$ relative to the GBW model. This figure is adapted from reference~\cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{Stasto:2012ru}{}{}]}.}}}
\end{figure}\par\begin{figure}\hbox{\includegraphics[]{generated-figures/dipoleNmomentumspace}}
\@@toccaption{{\lx@tag[ ]{{14}}{GBW, BK, and rcBK gluon distributions, solved in position space and Fourier transformed}}}\@@caption{{\lx@tag[: ]{{\small Figure 14}}{\small Analogous to figure~\ref {fig:dipoleFplot}, these plots show the dipole gluon distribution in momentum space, $\mathcal{F}_{x_{g}}$, computed by solving the equation in position space~\eqref{eq:BKN} and numerically Fourier transforming it into momentum space. The top plot shows the analytic result from the GBW model, the middle one shows the result from the BK equation with fixed coupling $\alpha_{\text{s}}=0.1$~\eqref{eq:BKN}, and the bottom one shows the result with running coupling corrections incorporated. Each curve in this plot shows $\mathcal{F}_{x_{g}}(k_{\perp})$ for $x_{g}=x_{\text{init}}\exp(-n)$, where $n$ is the number that labels the curve, so the numbers labeling the curves are not directly comparable to those in figure~\ref {fig:dipoleFplot}. In addition, these curves are for minimum bias ($c=0.56$) $\mathrm{pPb}${} ($A=208$) collisions, so the normalization is not the same as in figure~\ref {fig:dipoleFplot}, though the trend of the small-$x$ evolution is clear. The most notable feature of these plots is the numerical instability at high $k_{\perp}$ which results from the Fourier transform.}}}
\end{figure}\par Given the importance of small-$x$ evolution in calculating cross sections, it should come as no surprise that there are many, many numerical studies of the solutions to both the BFKL and BK equation (as well as the Balitsky-JIMWLK hierarchy, but that goes beyond the scope of this dissertation).
One can either solve the equation in position space, using the form~\eqref{eq:BKN} directly, or convert it to momentum space, in terms of $\phi$, and solve that.
The latter approach is taken in reference~\cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{GolecBiernat:2001if}{}{}]}, where the momentum space BK equation is written as
\begin{equation}\pd{\phi(k_{\perp},x_{g})}{\ln(1/x_{g})}=\frac{\alpha_{\text{s}}N_{c}}{\pi}\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{\udc k_{\perp}^{\prime 2}}{k_{\perp}^{\prime 2}}\biggl[\frac{k_{\perp}^{\prime 2}\phi(k_{\perp}^{\prime},x_{g})-k_{\perp}^{2}\phi(k_{\perp},x_{g})}{\abs{k_{\perp}^{2}-k_{\perp}^{\prime 2}}}+\frac{k_{\perp}^{2}\phi(k_{\perp},x_{g})}{\sqrt{4k_{\perp}^{\prime 4}+k_{\perp}^{4}}}\biggr]-\frac{\alpha_{\text{s}}N_{c}}{\pi}\phi(k_{\perp})^{2}\end{equation}Then a numerical Fourier transform allows us to convert either solution to the other.
\par Figures~\ref {fig:dipoleFplot} and~\ref {fig:dipoleNmomentumspace} show two computations of $\mathcal{F}_{x_{g}}$: in figure~\ref {fig:dipoleFplot}, the solution is computed from the momentum space BK equation~\eqref{eq:BKphi} and then $\mathcal{F}_{x_{g}}$ is calculated using equation~\eqref{eq:dipoleFfromphi}, whereas the plots in figure~\ref {fig:dipoleNmomentumspace} come from the solution to the position space BK equation~\eqref{eq:BKN} which is then Fourier transformed.
These two specific sets of plots have different normalization, so they are not directly comparable, but looking at the mid-$k_{\perp}$ region we can see some slight differences in how they depart from the initial condition as $x_{g}$ gets smaller.
\par One important difference between the two solutions is the present of numerical instabilities at large $k_{\perp}$ in the Fourier transform from the position space solution (figure~\ref {fig:dipoleNmomentumspace}).
The instability results from floating-point roundoff error in the Fourier transform.
This limits the validity of this solution to moderate values of $k_{\perp}$, though this will not be a problem for the calculations we will use this solution for in chapter~\ref {ch:crosssection}.
Conversely, in the direct momentum-space solution (figure~\ref {fig:dipoleFplot}), we see a spike at low $k_{\perp}$, which is a numerical artifact that owes its existence to the lower boundary condition on $k_{\perp}$.
In this case, the solution can only be used above a certain momentum, though again, this is not a problem for the calculations in chapter~\ref {ch:correlation} because it only samples the gluon distribution in the moderate $k_{\perp}$ range.
\par We can characterize the speed of the BK evolution by finding the saturation scale $Q_{s}$ as a function of $\ln\frac{1}{x_{g}}$.
In the GBW model, of course, this is purely an exponential dependence,
\begin{equation}Q_{s}^{2}\biggl(\ln\frac{1}{x_{g}}\biggr)=Q_{0}^{2}x_{0}^{\lambda}\exp\biggl(\lambda\ln\frac{1}{x_{g}}\biggr)\end{equation}The analytic solution to the BK equation predicts~\cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{Munier:2003vc,Munier:2003sj,Mueller:2002zm}{}{}]} a more complicated form,
\begin{equation}Q_{s}^{2}\biggl(\ln\frac{1}{x_{g}}\biggr)=Q_{0}^{2}\biggl(\ln\frac{1}{x_{g}}\biggr)^{\frac{3}{2(1-\gamma_{0})}}\exp\biggl(-\frac{\alpha_{\text{s}}N_{c}}{\pi}\frac{\chi(\gamma_{0})}{1-\gamma_{0}}\ln\frac{1}{x_{g}}\biggr)\end{equation}\par\begin{figure}\hbox{\includegraphics[]{generated-figures/satscale}}
\@@toccaption{{\lx@tag[ ]{{15}}{GBW and rcBK saturation scales}}}\@@caption{{\lx@tag[: ]{{\small Figure 15}}{\small Saturation scales for the GBW model and the solution to the BK equation with running coupling, solved in position space and Fourier transformed into momentum space, extracted by finding $k_{\perp}$ at which $\mathcal{F}_{x_{g}}(k_{\perp})=0.5\mathcal{F}_{x_{g}}(0)$. The flat portion at the left end of the plot is where the initial condition of the BK evolution is still strongly influencing the result. We can see that the evolution is slower in the BK solution.}}}
\end{figure}\par\begin{figure}\hbox{\includegraphics[]{generated-figures/kmax}}
\@@toccaption{{\lx@tag[ ]{{16}}{Saturation scale as a function of $x_{g}$ for GBW and several BK solutions}}}\@@caption{{\lx@tag[: ]{{\small Figure 16}}{\small This plot, reproduced from reference~\cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{Stasto:2012ru}{}{}]}, shows how adjustment of the parameters of the BK evolution alters the $x_{g}$ dependence of the saturation scale. Each curve shows the peak of the momentum distribution, $k_{\text{max}}=\max k\phi(k^{2},Y)$, computed from the analytic formula for the GBW model, from the fixed coupling BK evolution for selected values of $\alpha_{s}$, and from the running coupling BK evolution with selected values of $\Lambda_{\text{QCD}}^{2}$. For the BK evolution curves, the slope in the upper range of rapidities decreases as $\alpha_{s}$ or $\Lambda_{\text{QCD}}^{2}$ decreases, and the closest match to the slope of the GBW model curve at $Y>15$ is achieved with $\alpha_{s}=0.062$ for fixed coupling or $\Lambda_{\text{QCD}}^{2}=0.001$ for running coupling. The jagged ``steps'' in the curves reflect the finite spacing of the momentum grid used in the evolution.}}}
\end{figure}\par To do this calculation we need a way to extract the saturation scale from the solution to the BK equation.
There are a couple of complementary prescriptions we can use, depending on which form of the equation we're working from.
A simple method is to define the saturation scale as the momentum at which the solution crosses some particular threshold; specifically, in position space,
\begin{equation}S_{x_{g}}^{(2)}(1/Q_{s})=S_{0}\end{equation}or in momentum space,
\begin{equation}\mathcal{F}_{x_{g}}(Q_{s})=F_{0}\mathcal{F}_{x_{g}}(0)\end{equation}Alternatively, one can define the saturation scale as the momentum which maximizes the product $k_{\perp}\phi(k_{\perp})$, as in reference~\cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{GolecBiernat:2001if}{}{}]}.
The results from the various prescriptions differ slightly in their overall normalization, but they all predict essentially the same trend.
\par\lx@frontmatter@fallback\@@unnumbered@section{chapter}{toc}{Drell-Yan Correlations}
\par In the previous chapters, we saw how the color glass condensate provides a theoretical model of the effective structure of protons and nuclei.
Of course, the model is useless without the ability to connect it to measurable quantities, namely cross sections.
With that goal in mind, this chapter shows the basic process of computing the cross section for the Drell-Yan process $\mathup{{{p}}}\mathit{{A}{}_{\mspace{-3mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{-1mu}\scriptstyle{}}}\HepTo\mathit{{\ell}{}_{\mspace{-3mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{-1mu}\scriptstyle{}}}\mathit{{\mkern 4.0mu\overline{\mkern-4.0mu{\ell}}}{}_{\mspace{-2mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{0mu}\scriptstyle{}}}\mathit{{h}{}_{\mspace{-3mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{-1mu}\scriptstyle{}}}\mathit{{X}{}_{\mspace{-3mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{-1mu}\scriptstyle{}}}$, from the unintegrated gluon distribution.
\par Drell-Yan processes, first predicted in 1970~\cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{Drell:1970wh}{}{}]}, are those in which the decay of a virtual photon or Z boson produces a lepton and antilepton. Lepton pair production, as it's called, is particularly appealing as a probe of hadron structure for a few reasons:
\begin{itemize}\par\itemize@item@Electrons or muons and their antiparticles make very distinctive signatures in the detector, so they're easy to identify
\par\itemize@item@Not much else produces lepton-antilepton pairs, so background contamination is low
\par\itemize@item@Photons and Z bosons are uncharged, so they are not subject to strong interactions on their way out of the collision and therefore final state interactions with the hadron remnants are absent
\par\itemize@item@The decay of the photon or Z into the lepton-antilepton pair is represented by a factor which can be exactly calculated from electroweak theory
\par\end{itemize}Accordingly, lepton pair production gives us a fairly direct window on the unintegrated dipole gluon distribution.
The correlation, as opposed to a simple cross section, is particularly sensitive to the transverse momentum dependence of the distribution, making it especially suitable as a probe of saturation.
\par\par\lx@frontmatter@fallback\@@unnumbered@section{section}{toc}{Kinematics of \texorpdfstring{$\pA$}{pA} collisions}
\par\begin{figure}\hbox{\includegraphics[]{generated-figures/correlationdiagram}}
\@@toccaption{{\lx@tag[ ]{{17}}{Feynman diagram for $\mathup{{{p}}}\mathit{{A}{}_{\mspace{-3mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{-1mu}\scriptstyle{}}}\HepTo\mathit{{\ell}{}_{\mspace{-3mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{-1mu}\scriptstyle{}}}\mathit{{\mkern 4.0mu\overline{\mkern-4.0mu{\ell}}}{}_{\mspace{-2mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{0mu}\scriptstyle{}}}\mathup{{{\pi}}}\mathit{{X}{}_{\mspace{-3mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{-1mu}\scriptstyle{}}}$ at leading order}}}\@@caption{{\lx@tag[: ]{{\small Figure 17}}{\small Feynman diagram for $\mathup{{{p}}}\mathit{{A}{}_{\mspace{-3mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{-1mu}\scriptstyle{}}}\HepTo\mathit{{\ell}{}_{\mspace{-3mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{-1mu}\scriptstyle{}}}\mathit{{\mkern 4.0mu\overline{\mkern-4.0mu{\ell}}}{}_{\mspace{-2mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{0mu}\scriptstyle{}}}\mathup{{{\pi}}}\mathit{{X}{}_{\mspace{-3mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{-1mu}\scriptstyle{}}}$ at leading order}}}
\end{figure}\par In a proton-nucleus collision, we assume the proton comes in from the $-z$ direction and the nucleon (which is part of a nucleus) comes in from the $+z$ direction, with purely longitudinal momenta
\@@amsalign
respectively. Here $p_{\textmd{$\mathup{{{p}}}$ }}$ and $p_{\textmd{$\mathit{{A}{}_{\mspace{-3mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{-1mu}\scriptstyle{}}}$ }}$ represent the magnitudes of the proton's 3-momentum $\vec{p}_{\textmd{$\mathup{{{p}}}$ }}$ and the nucleon's 3-momentum $\vec{p}_{\textmd{$\mathit{{A}{}_{\mspace{-3mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{-1mu}\scriptstyle{}}}$ }}$, respectively --- note that $p_{\textmd{$\mathit{{A}{}_{\mspace{-3mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{-1mu}\scriptstyle{}}}$ }}$ refers to the momentum of \emph{one} nucleon, not the entire nucleus.
\par It's easiest to analyze the collision in the longitudinal center of mass frame, in which the center of mass of the proton and nucleon (not nucleus) is at rest: $p_{\textmd{$\mathup{{{p}}}$ }z}+p_{\textmd{$\mathit{{A}{}_{\mspace{-3mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{-1mu}\scriptstyle{}}}$ }z}=0$. Since the incoming proton and nucleon have zero transverse momentum and are traveling at near light speed, we can neglect the masses of the proton and nucleon and \emph{approximately} say
\lx@equationgroup@subnumbering@begin
\@@amsalign\lx@equationgroup@subnumbering@end according to the light-cone coordinate definitions in appendix~\ref {ap:lightcone}. Given our assumption that $\vec{p}_{p\perp}=\vec{p}_{A\perp}=0$, this implies that $E_{p}=E_{A}=p_{\textmd{$\mathup{{{p}}}$ }}=p_{\textmd{$\mathit{{A}{}_{\mspace{-3mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{-1mu}\scriptstyle{}}}$ }}$. This enables us to write the Mandelstam variable $s$ for the proton and nucleon (also denoted $s_{NN}$ in many sources) as
\begin{equation}s=(p_{\textmd{$\mathup{{{p}}}$ }}^{\mu}+p_{\textmd{$\mathit{{A}{}_{\mspace{-3mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{-1mu}\scriptstyle{}}}$ }}^{\mu})^{2}=p_{\textmd{$\mathup{{{p}}}$ }}^{+}p_{\textmd{$\mathit{{A}{}_{\mspace{-3mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{-1mu}\scriptstyle{}}}$ }}^{-}\end{equation}which in combination with equations~\eqref{eq:pamomenta} means that
\begin{equation}p_{\textmd{$\mathup{{{p}}}$ }}^{+}=p_{\textmd{$\mathit{{A}{}_{\mspace{-3mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{-1mu}\scriptstyle{}}}$ }}^{-}=\sqrt{s}\end{equation}\par Our eventual goal is to determine the momentum fractions $x_{p}$, $x_{g}$, and $z_{2}$, which we need for the parton distribution and gluon distribution, in terms of observable quantities.
For this reaction, those are $p_{\mathup{{{\pi}}}\perp}$, $\sqrt{s}$, $\eta_{\mathup{{{\pi}}}}$, $\eta_{\mathup{{{\gamma}}}}$, and $M$, where $\eta$ is the pseudorapidity, defined as
\begin{equation}\eta=\frac{1}{2}\ln\tan\frac{\theta}{2}\end{equation}Pseudorapidity and rapidity are related by the formula
\begin{equation}Y=\ln\frac{\sqrt{m^{2}+p_{\perp}^{2}\cosh^{2}\eta}+p_{\perp}\sinh\eta}{\sqrt{m^{2}+p_{\perp}^{2}}}\end{equation}In situations where $m\ll p_{\perp}\cosh\eta$, such as those considered in this dissertation, this reduces to $Y=\eta$, so we can consider them essentially interchangeable for the processes considered here.
\par Calculating the momentum fractions proceeds from the law of conservation of momentum, with the assumption that the plus component of the total momentum is contributed by the initial quark and the minus and transverse components of the total momentum are contributed by the gluon:
\lx@equationgroup@subnumbering@begin
\@@amsalign
\lx@equationgroup@subnumbering@end
We'll also need the final state momentum fractions, defined as $z_{\textmd{$\mathup{{{\gamma}}}$ }}\coloneqq p_{\textmd{$\mathup{{{\gamma}}}$ }}^{+}/p_{\textmd{$\mathup{{{p}}}$ }}^{+}$ and $z_{\textmd{$\mathup{{{\pi}}}$ }}\coloneqq p_{\textmd{$\mathup{{{\pi}}}$ }}^{+}/p_{\textmd{$\mathup{{{p}}}$ }}^{+}$.
Using equations~\eqref{eq:momentumpm} and~\eqref{eq:equaltosqrts}, these can be written as
\@@amsalign
assuming that the pion mass is negligible.
Now, we define $z_{2}\coloneqq\frac{p_{\textmd{$\mathup{{{\pi}}}$ }}^{+}}{k_{\textmd{$\mathit{{q}{}_{\mspace{-3mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{-1mu}\scriptstyle{}}}$ }}^{+}}$, the momentum fraction of the pion relative to the quark it fragments from, and take the pion to be collinear to the quark, so that all components of their momenta obey this proportionality: $p_{\textmd{$\mathup{{{\pi}}}$ }}^{\mu}=z_{2}k_{\textmd{$\mathit{{q}{}_{\mspace{-3mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{-1mu}\scriptstyle{}}}$ }}^{\mu}$.
Then in terms of these variables, the plus component of the conservation equation~\eqref{eq:plusconservation} leads to
\begin{equation}x_{p}=z_{\textmd{$\mathup{{{\gamma}}}$ }}+\frac{z_{\textmd{$\mathup{{{\pi}}}$ }}}{z_{2}}=\sqrt{\frac{p_{\textmd{$\mathup{{{\gamma}}}$ }\perp}^{2}+M^{2}}{s}}e^{Y_{\mathup{{{\gamma}}}}}+\frac{p_{\mathup{{{\pi}}}\perp}}{z_{2}\sqrt{s}}e^{Y_{\mathup{{{\pi}}}}}\end{equation}Similarly, we know that $p^{-}=p^{+}e^{-2Y}$~\eqref{eq:lightconerapidityrelation}, and using that on the right side of equation~\eqref{eq:minusconservation}, we get
\begin{equation}x_{g}=z_{\textmd{$\mathup{{{\gamma}}}$ }}e^{-2Y_{\mathup{{{\gamma}}}}}+\frac{z_{\textmd{$\mathup{{{\pi}}}$ }}}{z_{2}}e^{-2Y_{\mathup{{{\pi}}}}}=\sqrt{\frac{p_{\textmd{$\mathup{{{\gamma}}}$ }\perp}^{2}+M^{2}}{s}}e^{-Y_{\mathup{{{\gamma}}}}}+\frac{p_{\mathup{{{\pi}}}\perp}}{z_{2}\sqrt{s}}e^{-Y_{\mathup{{{\pi}}}}}\end{equation}\par For the inclusive cross section, we'll need to integrate over the entire allowed phase space of the final state particles emerging from fragmentation.
Our code parametrizes that space with the ``reduced'' photon momentum fraction $z\coloneqq\frac{p_{\textmd{$\mathup{{{\gamma}}}$ }}^{+}}{q^{+}}=\frac{z_{\textmd{$\mathup{{{\gamma}}}$ }}}{x_{p}}$, which represents the fraction of the total plus momentum carried by the photon. and the total transverse momentum $\vec{q}_{\perp}$, instead of the transverse pion momentum $p_{\textmd{$\mathup{{{\pi}}}$ }\perp}$ and rapidity $Y_{\mathup{{{\pi}}}}$ and the momentum fraction $z_{2}$, so we'll need to remove the latter variables from the formulas.
\par To achieve this parametrization, we can use the fact that
\@@amsalign
Combining the equation on the left with~\eqref{eq:plusconservation} and the definition of $z_{\textmd{$\mathup{{{\gamma}}}$ }}$~\eqref{eq:defnzh}, we find that
\begin{equation}x_{p}=\frac{q^{+}}{p_{\textmd{$\mathup{{{p}}}$ }}^{+}}=\frac{z_{\textmd{$\mathup{{{\gamma}}}$ }}}{z}=\frac{1}{z}\sqrt{\frac{p_{\textmd{$\mathup{{{\gamma}}}$ }\perp}^{2}+M^{2}}{s}}e^{Y_{\mathup{{{\gamma}}}}}\end{equation}\par For $x_{g}$, we can use the relation $e^{-Y_{\mathup{{{\pi}}}}}=\frac{p_{\textmd{$\mathup{{{\pi}}}$ }\perp}}{p_{\textmd{$\mathup{{{\pi}}}$ }}^{+}}=\frac{k_{\textmd{$\mathit{{q}{}_{\mspace{-3mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{-1mu}\scriptstyle{}}}$ }\perp}}{k_{\textmd{$\mathit{{q}{}_{\mspace{-3mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{-1mu}\scriptstyle{}}}$ }}^{+}}$, which follows from equation~\eqref{eq:momentumpm} and the proportionality of $p_{\textmd{$\mathup{{{\pi}}}$ }}^{\mu}$ and $k_{\textmd{$\mathit{{q}{}_{\mspace{-3mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{-1mu}\scriptstyle{}}}$ }}^{\mu}$, to express equation~\eqref{eq:xgexclusive} as
\begin{equation}x_{g}=\sqrt{\frac{p_{\textmd{$\mathup{{{\gamma}}}$ }\perp}^{2}+M^{2}}{s}}e^{-Y_{\mathup{{{\gamma}}}}}+\frac{k_{\textmd{$\mathit{{q}{}_{\mspace{-3mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{-1mu}\scriptstyle{}}}$ }\perp}^{2}}{k_{\textmd{$\mathit{{q}{}_{\mspace{-3mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{-1mu}\scriptstyle{}}}$ }}^{+}\sqrt{s}}\end{equation}We know from equation~\eqref{eq:zmomentumdivision} on the right that $k_{\textmd{$\mathit{{q}{}_{\mspace{-3mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{-1mu}\scriptstyle{}}}$ }}^{+}=(1-z)q^{+}$, and from~\eqref{eq:plusconservation} that $q^{+}=x_{p}p_{\textmd{$\mathup{{{p}}}$ }}^{+}$.
Putting these together with the assumption that $p_{\textmd{$\mathup{{{p}}}$ }}^{+}=\sqrt{s}$, we find that
\begin{equation}x_{g}=\sqrt{\frac{p_{\textmd{$\mathup{{{\gamma}}}$ }\perp}^{2}+M^{2}}{s}}e^{-Y_{\mathup{{{\gamma}}}}}+\frac{k_{\textmd{$\mathit{{q}{}_{\mspace{-3mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{-1mu}\scriptstyle{}}}$ }\perp}^{2}}{(1-z)x_{p}s}\end{equation}Adding in momentum conservation~\eqref{eq:perpconservation} gets us to the final form of the expression,
\begin{equation}x_{g}=\sqrt{\frac{p_{\textmd{$\mathup{{{\gamma}}}$ }\perp}^{2}+M^{2}}{s}}e^{-Y_{\mathup{{{\gamma}}}}}+\frac{(\vec{q}_{\perp}-\vec{p}_{\textmd{$\mathup{{{\gamma}}}$ }\perp})^{2}}{(1-z)x_{p}s}\end{equation}\par\par\lx@frontmatter@fallback\@@unnumbered@section{section}{toc}{Hybrid Factorization}
\par The final ingredient we will need for the calculation of the Drell-Yan process is the hybrid factorization formalism.
Earlier, in section~\ref {sec:partonmodel}, I briefly mentioned that our ability to split a calculation into the hard scattering process (the low-level interaction), which is calculable from perturbative QCD, and the parton distribution, which is not, relies on the existence of factorization theorems.
In fact, factorization can be implemented in several different ways.
The traditional approach~\cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{Collins:1982wa,Collins:1983ju,Collins:1988ig}{}{}]}, the {\it collinear factorization} framework, treats both projectile and target on an equal footing, representing them both with integrated parton distributions.
However, the collinear factorization approach with integrated PDFs is not well suited to describe dense systems which are within the saturation regime.
\par An alternative approach, called hybrid factorization~\cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{Dumitru:2005gt}{}{}]}, was developed specifically for asymmetric proton-nucleus collisions.
In hybrid factorization, the target is treated as a color glass condensate and represented using an unintegrated parton distribution.
This framework is designed for the kinematic region where $Y\gtrsim 2.5$, the larger the better --- this is called the ``forward rapidity'' region because large values of $Y$, defined as
\begin{equation}Y=\frac{1}{2}\ln\frac{E+p_{z}}{E-p_{z}}\end{equation}correspond to emission of particles near the direction of the projectile.
At these forward rapidities, the interaction is heavily weighted toward small-$x$ gluons from the target, $x_{g}\sim\num{e-4}$ or even lower, and relatively large-$x$ partons from the projectile, with $x_{p}\sim\num{e-1}$ or $\num{e-2}$.
\par In either approach, factorization necessitates ignoring the dynamics of the interacting fields above a certain momentum scale, in the same sense as renormalization group calculations.
Physical quantities are, of course, independent of this {\it factorization scale}, designated $\mu$, but when we calculate a perturbative approximation to fixed order (leading order or next-to-leading order etc.), we will find that the resulting formula does depend on the factorization scale.
At leading order, the dependence arises entirely through nonperturbative quantities, the parton distributions and fragmentation functions, but beyond leading order, the perturbative part of the calculation can have its own dependence on $\mu$.
We'll see this borne out in the calculations of chapter~\ref {ch:crosssection}.
\par There is some loose physical motivation for choosing a particular value for the factorization scale in any given calculation~\cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{Collins:1990bu,Maltoni:2007tc}{}{}]}, but in general, the dependence on the arbitrary scale $\mu$ should be viewed as a theoretical source of uncertainty in the final result.
Incorporating more terms in the perturbative series tends to reduce the dependence on $\mu$, as we'll see in chapter~\ref {ch:results}.
This provides a powerful motivation to extend perturbative quantities beyond leading order.
\par\par\lx@frontmatter@fallback\@@unnumbered@section{section}{toc}{Calculation of Drell-Yan Correlation}
Let's now turn to the procedure for CGC calculations described in chapter~\ref {ch:cgc}, which we can use to calculate the cross section for the Drell-Yan process.
The calculation begins with the process $\mathit{{q}{}_{\mspace{-3mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{-1mu}\scriptstyle{}}}\mathit{{A}{}_{\mspace{-3mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{-1mu}\scriptstyle{}}}\HepTo\mathit{{q}{}_{\mspace{-3mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{-1mu}\scriptstyle{}}}\mathup{{{\gamma}}}\mathit{{A}{}_{\mspace{-3mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{-1mu}\scriptstyle{}}}$, whose cross section is calculated in momentum space in reference~\cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{Gelis:2002ki}{}{}]} and in transverse coordinate space in~\cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{Dominguez:2011wm}{}{}]}.
However, these calculations only treat the case of a real photon, $M=0$, whereas we need the result for photon virtuality $M^{2}>0$.
The modifications to the calculation of~\cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{Dominguez:2011wm}{}{}]} required to account for nonzero virtuality are described in reference~\cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{Dominguez:2011br}{}{}]}.
\par\begin{figure}\hbox{\includegraphics[]{generated-figures/photonemissiondiagram}}
\@@toccaption{{\lx@tag[ ]{{18}}{Compound diagrams for a quark emitting a photon and interacting with a nucleus}}}\@@caption{{\lx@tag[: ]{{\small Figure 18}}{\small Compound diagrams for a quark emitting a photon and interacting with a nucleus}}}
\end{figure}Following the general procedure described in chapter~\ref {ch:saturation}, we start with the four diagrams contributing to this process at leading order, as shown in figure~\ref {fig:photonemissiondiagram}.
They differ only in whether the interaction with the nucleus occurs before or after the emission of the photon.
For the first diagram, putting together the splitting wavefunction and the Wilson line on each side of the cut, we get
\begin{multline}\frac{\udc^{6}\sigma}{\udddc\vec{p}_{\mathup{{{\gamma}}}}\udddc\vec{k}_{\mathit{{q}{}_{\mspace{-3mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{-1mu}\scriptstyle{}}}}}=\iint\frac{\uddc\vec{}x_{\perp}}{(2\pi)^{2}}\iint\frac{\uddc\vec{}x_{\perp}^{\prime}}{(2\pi)^{2}}\iint\frac{\uddc\vec{}b_{\perp}}{(2\pi)^{2}}\iint\frac{\uddc\vec{}b_{\perp}^{\prime}}{(2\pi)^{2}}e^{-i\vec{p}_{\textmd{$\mathup{{{\gamma}}}$ }\perp}\cdot(\vec{}x_{\perp}-\vec{}x_{\perp}^{\prime})}e^{-i\vec{k}_{\textmd{$\mathit{{q}{}_{\mspace{-3mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{-1mu}\scriptstyle{}}}$ }\perp}\cdot(\vec{}b_{\perp}-\vec{}b_{\perp}^{\prime})}\\
\times\sum_{\lambda\alpha\beta}\conj{(\psi_{\alpha\beta}^{\lambda})}(\vec{}x_{\perp}^{\prime}-\vec{}b_{\perp}^{\prime})\psi_{\alpha\beta}^{\lambda}(\vec{}x_{\perp}-\vec{}b_{\perp})\braket{\trace U\bigl(z\vec{}x_{\perp}+(1-z)\vec{}b_{\perp}\bigr)\herm{U}\bigl(z\vec{}x_{\perp}^{\prime}+(1-z)\vec{}b_{\perp}^{\prime}\bigr)}_{x_{g}}\end{multline}\frac{\udc^{6}\sigma}{\udddc\vec{p}_{\mathup{{{\gamma}}}}\udddc\vec{k}_{\mathit{{q}{}_{\mspace{-3mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{-1mu}\scriptstyle{}}}}}=\iint\frac{\uddc\vec{}x_{\perp}}{(2\pi)^{2}}\iint\frac{\uddc\vec{}x_{\perp}^{\prime}}{(2\pi)^{2}}\iint\frac{\uddc\vec{}b_{\perp}}{(2\pi)^{2}}\iint\frac{\uddc\vec{}b_{\perp}^{\prime}}{(2\pi)^{2}}e^{-i\vec{p}_{\textmd{$\mathup{{{\gamma}}}$ }\perp}\cdot(\vec{}x_{\perp}-\vec{}x_{\perp}^{\prime})}e^{-i\vec{k}_{\textmd{$\mathit{{q}{}_{\mspace{-3mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{-1mu}\scriptstyle{}}}$ }\perp}\cdot(\vec{}b_{\perp}-\vec{}b_{\perp}^{\prime})}\\
\times\sum_{\lambda\alpha\beta}\conj{(\psi_{\alpha\beta}^{\lambda})}(\vec{}x_{\perp}^{\prime}-\vec{}b_{\perp}^{\prime})\psi_{\alpha\beta}^{\lambda}(\vec{}x_{\perp}-\vec{}b_{\perp})\braket{\trace U\bigl(z\vec{}x_{\perp}+(1-z)\vec{}b_{\perp}\bigr)\herm{U}\bigl(z\vec{}x_{\perp}^{\prime}+(1-z)\vec{}b_{\perp}^{\prime}\bigr)}_{x_{g}}where $\lambda$ represents the polarization of the photon, and $\alpha$ and $\beta$ are the helicities of the initial and final quarks, respectively.
The averaging operation $\braket{\cdots}_{x_{g}}$ averages over the unknown color charge distribution of the nucleus.
The exponential factors have their origin in the LSZ reduction formula.
At the end of this expression, we see the dipole gluon distribution in the form
\begin{equation}\braket{\trace U(\vec{}x_{\perp})\herm{U}(\vec{}y_{\perp}))}_{x_{g}}=S_{x_{g}}^{(2)}(\vec{}x_{\perp},\vec{}y_{\perp})\end{equation}\par Adding in the other diagrams from figure~\ref {fig:photonemissiondiagram} gives the expression
\begin{multline}\frac{\udc^{6}\sigma}{\udddc\vec{p}_{\mathup{{{\gamma}}}}\udddc\vec{k}_{\mathit{{q}{}_{\mspace{-3mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{-1mu}\scriptstyle{}}}}}=\iint\frac{\uddc\vec{}x_{\perp}}{(2\pi)^{2}}\iint\frac{\uddc\vec{}x_{\perp}^{\prime}}{(2\pi)^{2}}\iint\frac{\uddc\vec{}b_{\perp}}{(2\pi)^{2}}\iint\frac{\uddc\vec{}b_{\perp}^{\prime}}{(2\pi)^{2}}e^{-i\vec{p}_{\textmd{$\mathup{{{\gamma}}}$ }\perp}\cdot(\vec{}x_{\perp}-\vec{}x_{\perp}^{\prime})}e^{-ik_{\textmd{$\mathit{{q}{}_{\mspace{-3mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{-1mu}\scriptstyle{}}}$ }\perp}\cdot(\vec{}b_{\perp}-\vec{}b_{\perp}^{\prime})}\\
\times\sum_{\lambda\alpha\beta}\conj{(\psi_{\alpha\beta}^{\lambda})}(\vec{}x_{\perp}^{\prime}-\vec{}b_{\perp}^{\prime})\psi_{\alpha\beta}^{\lambda}(\vec{}x_{\perp}-\vec{}b_{\perp})\bigl[S_{x_{g}}^{(2)}\bigl(z\vec{}x_{\perp}+(1-z)\vec{}b_{\perp},z\vec{}x_{\perp}^{\prime}+(1-z)\vec{}b_{\perp}^{\prime}\bigr)+S_{x_{g}}^{(2)}(\vec{}b_{\perp},\vec{}b_{\perp}^{\prime})\\
-S_{x_{g}}^{(2)}\bigl(\vec{}b_{\perp},z\vec{}x_{\perp}^{\prime}+(1-z)\vec{}b_{\perp}^{\prime}\bigr)-S_{x_{g}}^{(2)}\bigl(z\vec{}x_{\perp}+(1-z)\vec{}b_{\perp},\vec{}b_{\perp}^{\prime}\bigr)\bigr]\end{multline}\frac{\udc^{6}\sigma}{\udddc\vec{p}_{\mathup{{{\gamma}}}}\udddc\vec{k}_{\mathit{{q}{}_{\mspace{-3mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{-1mu}\scriptstyle{}}}}}=\iint\frac{\uddc\vec{}x_{\perp}}{(2\pi)^{2}}\iint\frac{\uddc\vec{}x_{\perp}^{\prime}}{(2\pi)^{2}}\iint\frac{\uddc\vec{}b_{\perp}}{(2\pi)^{2}}\iint\frac{\uddc\vec{}b_{\perp}^{\prime}}{(2\pi)^{2}}e^{-i\vec{p}_{\textmd{$\mathup{{{\gamma}}}$ }\perp}\cdot(\vec{}x_{\perp}-\vec{}x_{\perp}^{\prime})}e^{-ik_{\textmd{$\mathit{{q}{}_{\mspace{-3mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{-1mu}\scriptstyle{}}}$ }\perp}\cdot(\vec{}b_{\perp}-\vec{}b_{\perp}^{\prime})}\\
\times\sum_{\lambda\alpha\beta}\conj{(\psi_{\alpha\beta}^{\lambda})}(\vec{}x_{\perp}^{\prime}-\vec{}b_{\perp}^{\prime})\psi_{\alpha\beta}^{\lambda}(\vec{}x_{\perp}-\vec{}b_{\perp})\bigl[S_{x_{g}}^{(2)}\bigl(z\vec{}x_{\perp}+(1-z)\vec{}b_{\perp},z\vec{}x_{\perp}^{\prime}+(1-z)\vec{}b_{\perp}^{\prime}\bigr)+S_{x_{g}}^{(2)}(\vec{}b_{\perp},\vec{}b_{\perp}^{\prime})\\
-S_{x_{g}}^{(2)}\bigl(\vec{}b_{\perp},z\vec{}x_{\perp}^{\prime}+(1-z)\vec{}b_{\perp}^{\prime}\bigr)-S_{x_{g}}^{(2)}\bigl(z\vec{}x_{\perp}+(1-z)\vec{}b_{\perp},\vec{}b_{\perp}^{\prime}\bigr)\bigr]Following reference~\cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{Dominguez:2011wm}{}{}]}, we make the substitutions $\vec{u}=\vec{}x_{\perp}-\vec{}b_{\perp}$, $\vec{u}^{\prime}=\vec{}x_{\perp}^{\prime}-\vec{}b_{\perp}^{\prime}$, and
\@@amsalign
which results in
\begin{multline}\frac{\udc^{6}\sigma}{\udddc\vec{p}_{\mathup{{{\gamma}}}}\udddc\vec{k}_{\mathit{{q}{}_{\mspace{-3mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{-1mu}\scriptstyle{}}}}}=\iint\frac{\uddc\vec{u}}{(2\pi)^{2}}\iint\frac{\uddc\vec{u}^{\prime}}{(2\pi)^{2}}\iint\frac{\uddc\vec{v}}{(2\pi)^{2}}\iint\frac{\uddc\vec{v}^{\prime}}{(2\pi)^{2}}e^{-i\vec{q}_{\perp}\cdot(\vec{v}-\vec{v}^{\prime})}S_{x_{g}}^{(2)}(\vec{v},\vec{v}^{\prime})\\
\times\sum_{\lambda\alpha\beta}\conj{(\psi_{\alpha\beta}^{\lambda})}(\vec{u}^{\prime})\psi_{\alpha\beta}^{\lambda}(\vec{u})\bigl[e^{-i(\vec{p}_{\textmd{$\mathup{{{\gamma}}}$ }\perp}-z\vec{q}_{\perp})\cdot\vec{u}}e^{i(\vec{p}_{\textmd{$\mathup{{{\gamma}}}$ }\perp}-z\vec{q}_{\perp})\cdot\vec{u}^{\prime}}+e^{-i\vec{p}_{\textmd{$\mathup{{{\gamma}}}$ }\perp}\cdot\vec{u}}e^{i\vec{p}_{\textmd{$\mathup{{{\gamma}}}$ }\perp}\cdot\vec{u}^{\prime}}\\
-e^{-i\vec{p}_{\textmd{$\mathup{{{\gamma}}}$ }\perp}\cdot\vec{u}}e^{i(\vec{p}_{\textmd{$\mathup{{{\gamma}}}$ }\perp}-z\vec{q}_{\perp})\cdot\vec{u}^{\prime}}-e^{-i(\vec{p}_{\textmd{$\mathup{{{\gamma}}}$ }\perp}-z\vec{q}_{\perp})\cdot\vec{u}}e^{i\vec{p}_{\textmd{$\mathup{{{\gamma}}}$ }\perp}\cdot\vec{u}^{\prime}}\bigr]\end{multline}\frac{\udc^{6}\sigma}{\udddc\vec{p}_{\mathup{{{\gamma}}}}\udddc\vec{k}_{\mathit{{q}{}_{\mspace{-3mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{-1mu}\scriptstyle{}}}}}=\iint\frac{\uddc\vec{u}}{(2\pi)^{2}}\iint\frac{\uddc\vec{u}^{\prime}}{(2\pi)^{2}}\iint\frac{\uddc\vec{v}}{(2\pi)^{2}}\iint\frac{\uddc\vec{v}^{\prime}}{(2\pi)^{2}}e^{-i\vec{q}_{\perp}\cdot(\vec{v}-\vec{v}^{\prime})}S_{x_{g}}^{(2)}(\vec{v},\vec{v}^{\prime})\\
\times\sum_{\lambda\alpha\beta}\conj{(\psi_{\alpha\beta}^{\lambda})}(\vec{u}^{\prime})\psi_{\alpha\beta}^{\lambda}(\vec{u})\bigl[e^{-i(\vec{p}_{\textmd{$\mathup{{{\gamma}}}$ }\perp}-z\vec{q}_{\perp})\cdot\vec{u}}e^{i(\vec{p}_{\textmd{$\mathup{{{\gamma}}}$ }\perp}-z\vec{q}_{\perp})\cdot\vec{u}^{\prime}}+e^{-i\vec{p}_{\textmd{$\mathup{{{\gamma}}}$ }\perp}\cdot\vec{u}}e^{i\vec{p}_{\textmd{$\mathup{{{\gamma}}}$ }\perp}\cdot\vec{u}^{\prime}}\\
-e^{-i\vec{p}_{\textmd{$\mathup{{{\gamma}}}$ }\perp}\cdot\vec{u}}e^{i(\vec{p}_{\textmd{$\mathup{{{\gamma}}}$ }\perp}-z\vec{q}_{\perp})\cdot\vec{u}^{\prime}}-e^{-i(\vec{p}_{\textmd{$\mathup{{{\gamma}}}$ }\perp}-z\vec{q}_{\perp})\cdot\vec{u}}e^{i\vec{p}_{\textmd{$\mathup{{{\gamma}}}$ }\perp}\cdot\vec{u}^{\prime}}\bigr]In this expression, the integrals over $\vec{v}$ and $\vec{v}^{\prime}$ give us the momentum space dipole gluon distribution, according to equation~\eqref{eq:dipoleFdefinition}, and the integrals over $\vec{u}$ and $\vec{u}^{\prime}$ implement the Fourier transform of the splitting wavefunction,
\begin{equation}\iint\frac{\uddc\vec{u}}{(2\pi)^{2}}\iint\frac{\uddc\vec{u}^{\prime}}{(2\pi)^{2}}\sum_{\lambda\alpha\beta}\conj{(\psi_{\alpha\beta}^{\lambda})}(\vec{u}^{\prime})\psi_{\alpha\beta}^{\lambda}(\vec{u})e^{-i\vec{}q_{1\perp}\cdot\vec{u}}e^{-i\vec{}q_{2\perp}\cdot\vec{u}^{\prime}}\end{equation}for different values of $\vec{}q_{1\perp}$ and $\vec{}q_{2\perp}$ in each term.
\par This is the first point at which the nonzero virtuality becomes relevant.
The explicit form of the splitting wavefunction for a virtual photon is given in~\cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{Dominguez:2011br}{}{}]} as
\begin{equation}\psi_{\alpha\beta}^{\lambda}(q^{+},p_{\textmd{$\mathup{{{\gamma}}}$ }}^{+},\vec{u})=2\pi\sqrt{\frac{2}{p_{\textmd{$\mathup{{{\gamma}}}$ }}^{+}}}\begin{cases}(1-z)MK_{0}(\epsilon_{M}u),&\lambda=0\quad\text{(longitudinal)}\\
i\epsilon_{M}K_{1}(\epsilon_{M}u)\frac{\vec{u}\cdot\vec{\epsilon}_{\perp}^{(1)}}{u^{2}}\bigl(\delta_{\alpha-}\delta_{\beta-}+(1-z)\delta_{\alpha+}\delta_{\beta+}\bigr),&\lambda=1\quad\text{(transverse)}\\
i\epsilon_{M}K_{1}(\epsilon_{M}u)\frac{\vec{u}\cdot\vec{\epsilon}_{\perp}^{(2)}}{u^{2}}\bigl(\delta_{\alpha+}\delta_{\beta+}+(1-z)\delta_{\alpha-}\delta_{\beta-}\bigr),&\lambda=2\quad\text{(transverse)}\end{cases}\end{equation}where $\epsilon_{M}^{2}=(1-z)M^{2}$.
We can plug that into~\eqref{eq:splittingft} and perform the sums over $\lambda$, $\alpha$, and $\beta$.
Using the identity~\eqref{eq:ident:polarization}, equation~\eqref{eq:splittingft} reduces to
\begin{multline}\iint\frac{\uddc\vec{u}}{(2\pi)^{2}}\iint\frac{\uddc\vec{u}^{\prime}}{(2\pi)^{2}}4\pi^{2}\frac{2}{p_{\textmd{$\mathup{{{\gamma}}}$ }}^{+}}K_{0}(\epsilon_{M}u)K_{0}(\epsilon_{M}u^{\prime})M^{2}(1-z)^{2}e^{-i\vec{}q_{1\perp}\cdot\vec{u}}e^{i\vec{}q_{2\perp}\cdot\vec{u}^{\prime}}\\
+\iint\frac{\uddc\vec{u}}{(2\pi)^{2}}\iint\frac{\uddc\vec{u}^{\prime}}{(2\pi)^{2}}4\pi^{2}\frac{2}{p_{\textmd{$\mathup{{{\gamma}}}$ }}^{+}}K_{1}(\epsilon_{M}u)K_{1}(\epsilon_{M}u^{\prime})\epsilon_{M}^{2}\\
\times\frac{\vec{u}^{\prime}\cdot\vec{u}-(\vec{u}^{\prime}\cdot\vec{p}_{\textmd{$\mathup{{{\gamma}}}$ }})(\vec{u}\cdot\vec{p}_{\textmd{$\mathup{{{\gamma}}}$ }})/p_{\textmd{$\mathup{{{\gamma}}}$ }}^{2}}{u^{\prime 2}u^{2}}\bigl[(1-z)^{2}+1\bigr]e^{-i\vec{}q_{1\perp}\cdot\vec{u}}e^{i\vec{}q_{2\perp}\cdot\vec{u}^{\prime}}\end{multline}\iint\frac{\uddc\vec{u}}{(2\pi)^{2}}\iint\frac{\uddc\vec{u}^{\prime}}{(2\pi)^{2}}4\pi^{2}\frac{2}{p_{\textmd{$\mathup{{{\gamma}}}$ }}^{+}}K_{0}(\epsilon_{M}u)K_{0}(\epsilon_{M}u^{\prime})M^{2}(1-z)^{2}e^{-i\vec{}q_{1\perp}\cdot\vec{u}}e^{i\vec{}q_{2\perp}\cdot\vec{u}^{\prime}}\\
+\iint\frac{\uddc\vec{u}}{(2\pi)^{2}}\iint\frac{\uddc\vec{u}^{\prime}}{(2\pi)^{2}}4\pi^{2}\frac{2}{p_{\textmd{$\mathup{{{\gamma}}}$ }}^{+}}K_{1}(\epsilon_{M}u)K_{1}(\epsilon_{M}u^{\prime})\epsilon_{M}^{2}\\
\times\frac{\vec{u}^{\prime}\cdot\vec{u}-(\vec{u}^{\prime}\cdot\vec{p}_{\textmd{$\mathup{{{\gamma}}}$ }})(\vec{u}\cdot\vec{p}_{\textmd{$\mathup{{{\gamma}}}$ }})/p_{\textmd{$\mathup{{{\gamma}}}$ }}^{2}}{u^{\prime 2}u^{2}}\bigl[(1-z)^{2}+1\bigr]e^{-i\vec{}q_{1\perp}\cdot\vec{u}}e^{i\vec{}q_{2\perp}\cdot\vec{u}^{\prime}}where the first line comes from the longitudinal component and the second and third lines from the transverse components.
\par\par\par\par\par Performing the Fourier integrals, the cross section for $\mathit{{q}{}_{\mspace{-3mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{-1mu}\scriptstyle{}}}\mathit{{A}{}_{\mspace{-3mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{-1mu}\scriptstyle{}}}\HepTo\mathit{{q}{}_{\mspace{-3mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{-1mu}\scriptstyle{}}}\mathup{{{\gamma}}}\mathit{{X}{}_{\mspace{-3mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{-1mu}\scriptstyle{}}}$ becomes~\cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{Dominguez:2011br}{}{}]}
\begin{multline}\frac{\udc^{8}\sigma}{\udc Y_{\mathup{{{\gamma}}}}\udc Y_{\mathit{{q}{}_{\mspace{-3mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{-1mu}\scriptstyle{}}}}\uddc\vec{p}_{\textmd{$\mathup{{{\gamma}}}$ }\perp}\uddc\vec{k}_{\textmd{$\mathit{{q}{}_{\mspace{-3mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{-1mu}\scriptstyle{}}}$ }\perp}\uddc\vec{b}}=\frac{\alpha_{\text{em}}e_{q}^{2}}{2\pi^{2}}(1-z)\mathcal{F}_{x_{g}}(\vec{k}_{\textmd{$\mathup{{{g}}}$ }\perp})\\
\times\Biggl\{\bigl[1+(1-z)^{2}\bigr]\frac{z^{2}k_{\textmd{$\mathup{{{g}}}$ }\perp}^{2}}{\bigl[(\vec{p}_{\textmd{$\mathup{{{\gamma}}}$ }\perp}-z\vec{k}_{\textmd{$\mathup{{{g}}}$ }\perp})^{2}+\epsilon_{M}^{2}\bigr]\bigl[p_{\textmd{$\mathup{{{\gamma}}}$ }\perp}^{2}+\epsilon_{M}^{2}\bigr]}\\
-z^{2}\epsilon_{M}^{2}\biggl[\frac{1}{p_{\textmd{$\mathup{{{\gamma}}}$ }\perp}^{2}+\epsilon_{M}^{2}}-\frac{1}{(\vec{p}_{\textmd{$\mathup{{{\gamma}}}$ }\perp}-z\vec{k}_{\textmd{$\mathup{{{g}}}$ }\perp})^{2}+\epsilon_{M}^{2}}\biggr]^{2}\Biggr\}\end{multline}\frac{\udc^{8}\sigma}{\udc Y_{\mathup{{{\gamma}}}}\udc Y_{\mathit{{q}{}_{\mspace{-3mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{-1mu}\scriptstyle{}}}}\uddc\vec{p}_{\textmd{$\mathup{{{\gamma}}}$ }\perp}\uddc\vec{k}_{\textmd{$\mathit{{q}{}_{\mspace{-3mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{-1mu}\scriptstyle{}}}$ }\perp}\uddc\vec{b}}=\frac{\alpha_{\text{em}}e_{q}^{2}}{2\pi^{2}}(1-z)\mathcal{F}_{x_{g}}(\vec{k}_{\textmd{$\mathup{{{g}}}$ }\perp})\\
\times\Biggl\{\bigl[1+(1-z)^{2}\bigr]\frac{z^{2}k_{\textmd{$\mathup{{{g}}}$ }\perp}^{2}}{\bigl[(\vec{p}_{\textmd{$\mathup{{{\gamma}}}$ }\perp}-z\vec{k}_{\textmd{$\mathup{{{g}}}$ }\perp})^{2}+\epsilon_{M}^{2}\bigr]\bigl[p_{\textmd{$\mathup{{{\gamma}}}$ }\perp}^{2}+\epsilon_{M}^{2}\bigr]}\\
-z^{2}\epsilon_{M}^{2}\biggl[\frac{1}{p_{\textmd{$\mathup{{{\gamma}}}$ }\perp}^{2}+\epsilon_{M}^{2}}-\frac{1}{(\vec{p}_{\textmd{$\mathup{{{\gamma}}}$ }\perp}-z\vec{k}_{\textmd{$\mathup{{{g}}}$ }\perp})^{2}+\epsilon_{M}^{2}}\biggr]^{2}\Biggr\}where $e_{q}$ is the charge of the initial-state quark.
\par To obtain the cross section for $\mathup{{{p}}}\mathit{{A}{}_{\mspace{-3mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{-1mu}\scriptstyle{}}}\HepTo\mathit{{\ell}{}_{\mspace{-3mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{-1mu}\scriptstyle{}}}\mathit{{\mkern 4.0mu\overline{\mkern-4.0mu{\ell}}}{}_{\mspace{-2mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{0mu}\scriptstyle{}}}\mathup{{{\pi}}^{\scriptstyle{0}}}\mathit{{X}{}_{\mspace{-3mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{-1mu}\scriptstyle{}}}$, we need to multiply~\eqref{eq:partonlevelquarkphotonxsec} by the integrated parton distribution, the fragmentation function, and the factor representing the splitting of the virtual photon into a lepton pair, which is $\frac{\alpha_{\text{em}}}{3\pi}\frac{\udc M^{2}}{M^{2}}$.
Then we integrate over all possible values of the unknown momentum fraction $z_{2}$.
\begin{multline}\frac{\udc^{9}\sigma}{\udc Y_{\mathup{{{\gamma}}}}\udc Y_{\mathup{{{\pi}}}}\uddc\vec{p}_{\textmd{$\mathup{{{\gamma}}}$ }\perp}\uddc\vec{}p_{\mathup{{{\pi}}}\perp}\uddc\vec{b}\,\udc M^{2}}=\frac{\alpha_{\text{em}}^{2}e_{q}^{2}}{6\pi^{3}M^{2}}\int_{\frac{z_{\textmd{$\mathup{{{\pi}}}$ }}}{1-z_{\textmd{$\mathup{{{\gamma}}}$ }}}}^{1}\frac{\udc z_{2}}{z_{2}^{2}}\sum_{q}x_{p}q(x_{p},\mu)D_{\mathup{{{\pi}}}/q}(z_{2},\mu)(1-z)\mathcal{F}_{x_{g}}(\vec{}k_{\mathup{{{g}}}\perp})\\
\times\Biggl\{\bigl[1+(1-z)^{2}\bigr]\frac{z^{2}k_{\mathup{{{g}}}\perp}^{2}}{\bigl[(\vec{p}_{\textmd{$\mathup{{{\gamma}}}$ }\perp}-z\vec{}k_{\mathup{{{g}}}\perp})^{2}+\epsilon_{M}^{2}\bigr]\bigl[p_{\textmd{$\mathup{{{\gamma}}}$ }\perp}^{2}+\epsilon_{M}^{2}\bigr]}\\
-z^{2}\epsilon_{M}^{2}\biggl[\frac{1}{p_{\textmd{$\mathup{{{\gamma}}}$ }\perp}^{2}+\epsilon_{M}^{2}}-\frac{1}{(\vec{p}_{\textmd{$\mathup{{{\gamma}}}$ }\perp}-z\vec{}k_{\mathup{{{g}}}\perp})^{2}+\epsilon_{M}^{2}}\biggr]^{2}\Biggr\}\end{multline}\frac{\udc^{9}\sigma}{\udc Y_{\mathup{{{\gamma}}}}\udc Y_{\mathup{{{\pi}}}}\uddc\vec{p}_{\textmd{$\mathup{{{\gamma}}}$ }\perp}\uddc\vec{}p_{\mathup{{{\pi}}}\perp}\uddc\vec{b}\,\udc M^{2}}=\frac{\alpha_{\text{em}}^{2}e_{q}^{2}}{6\pi^{3}M^{2}}\int_{\frac{z_{\textmd{$\mathup{{{\pi}}}$ }}}{1-z_{\textmd{$\mathup{{{\gamma}}}$ }}}}^{1}\frac{\udc z_{2}}{z_{2}^{2}}\sum_{q}x_{p}q(x_{p},\mu)D_{\mathup{{{\pi}}}/q}(z_{2},\mu)(1-z)\mathcal{F}_{x_{g}}(\vec{}k_{\mathup{{{g}}}\perp})\\
\times\Biggl\{\bigl[1+(1-z)^{2}\bigr]\frac{z^{2}k_{\mathup{{{g}}}\perp}^{2}}{\bigl[(\vec{p}_{\textmd{$\mathup{{{\gamma}}}$ }\perp}-z\vec{}k_{\mathup{{{g}}}\perp})^{2}+\epsilon_{M}^{2}\bigr]\bigl[p_{\textmd{$\mathup{{{\gamma}}}$ }\perp}^{2}+\epsilon_{M}^{2}\bigr]}\\
-z^{2}\epsilon_{M}^{2}\biggl[\frac{1}{p_{\textmd{$\mathup{{{\gamma}}}$ }\perp}^{2}+\epsilon_{M}^{2}}-\frac{1}{(\vec{p}_{\textmd{$\mathup{{{\gamma}}}$ }\perp}-z\vec{}k_{\mathup{{{g}}}\perp})^{2}+\epsilon_{M}^{2}}\biggr]^{2}\Biggr\}\par The other cross section we need, for the denominator of the correlation, is the inclusive cross section for $\mathup{{{p}}}\mathit{{A}{}_{\mspace{-3mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{-1mu}\scriptstyle{}}}\HepTo\mathup{{{\pi}}^{\scriptstyle{0}}}\mathit{{X}{}_{\mspace{-3mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{-1mu}\scriptstyle{}}}$, which we can get by integrating over all possible combinations of the pion momentum and everything else ($\mathit{{X}{}_{\mspace{-3mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{-1mu}\scriptstyle{}}}$) that could come out of the reaction --- or, equivalently, we can just ignore the fact that the final-state quark fragments at all, and just integrate over all possible momenta of that quark.
It's most straightforward to get this by starting again from equation~\eqref{eq:partonlevelquarkphotonxsec} and inserting the integrated parton distribution (but not the fragmentation function) and the lepton splitting factor $\frac{\alpha_{\text{em}}}{3\pi}\frac{\udc M^{2}}{M^{2}}$.
\par Then we integrate over the unknown momentum of the quark, represented by $Y_{\mathit{{q}{}_{\mspace{-3mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{-1mu}\scriptstyle{}}}}$ and $\vec{k}_{\textmd{$\mathit{{q}{}_{\mspace{-3mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{-1mu}\scriptstyle{}}}$ }}$.
We're assuming the quark to be on-shell, $k_{\textmd{$\mathit{{q}{}_{\mspace{-3mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{-1mu}\scriptstyle{}}}$ }}^{\mu}k_{\textmd{$\mathit{{q}{}_{\mspace{-3mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{-1mu}\scriptstyle{}}}$ }\mu}=m_{\mathit{{q}{}_{\mspace{-3mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{-1mu}\scriptstyle{}}}}^{2}$, which means there are only three degrees of freedom in this momentum.
Now, since the photon momentum $\vec{p}_{\textmd{$\mathup{{{\gamma}}}$ }}$ is a constant, we can change variables from $k_{\textmd{$\mathit{{q}{}_{\mspace{-3mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{-1mu}\scriptstyle{}}}$ }}$ to $\vec{q}=\vec{k}_{\textmd{$\mathit{{q}{}_{\mspace{-3mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{-1mu}\scriptstyle{}}}$ }}+\vec{p}_{\textmd{$\mathup{{{\gamma}}}$ }}$, which is a simple shift.
We can then take advantage of the transformations
\@@amsalign
to work out the inclusive cross section to
\begin{multline}\frac{\udc^{6}\sigma}{\udc Y_{\mathup{{{\gamma}}}}\uddc\vec{p}_{\textmd{$\mathup{{{\gamma}}}$ }\perp}\uddc\vec{b}\,\udc M^{2}}=\frac{\alpha_{\text{em}}^{2}e_{q}^{2}}{6\pi^{3}M^{2}}\sum_{q}\int_{z_{\textmd{$\mathup{{{\gamma}}}$ }}}^{1}\frac{\udc z}{z}x_{p}q(x_{p},\mu)\iint\uddc\vec{}k_{\mathup{{{g}}}\perp}\mathcal{F}_{x_{g}}(\vec{}k_{\mathup{{{g}}}\perp})\\
\times\Biggl\{\bigl[1+(1-z)^{2}\bigr]\frac{z^{2}k_{\mathup{{{g}}}\perp}^{2}}{\bigl[(\vec{p}_{\textmd{$\mathup{{{\gamma}}}$ }\perp}-z\vec{}k_{\mathup{{{g}}}\perp})^{2}+\epsilon_{M}^{2}\bigr]\bigl[p_{\textmd{$\mathup{{{\gamma}}}$ }\perp}^{2}+\epsilon_{M}^{2}\bigr]}\\
-z^{2}\epsilon_{M}^{2}\biggl[\frac{1}{p_{\textmd{$\mathup{{{\gamma}}}$ }\perp}^{2}+\epsilon_{M}^{2}}-\frac{1}{(\vec{p}_{\textmd{$\mathup{{{\gamma}}}$ }\perp}-z\vec{}k_{\mathup{{{g}}}\perp})^{2}+\epsilon_{M}^{2}}\biggr]^{2}\Biggr\}\end{multline}\frac{\udc^{6}\sigma}{\udc Y_{\mathup{{{\gamma}}}}\uddc\vec{p}_{\textmd{$\mathup{{{\gamma}}}$ }\perp}\uddc\vec{b}\,\udc M^{2}}=\frac{\alpha_{\text{em}}^{2}e_{q}^{2}}{6\pi^{3}M^{2}}\sum_{q}\int_{z_{\textmd{$\mathup{{{\gamma}}}$ }}}^{1}\frac{\udc z}{z}x_{p}q(x_{p},\mu)\iint\uddc\vec{}k_{\mathup{{{g}}}\perp}\mathcal{F}_{x_{g}}(\vec{}k_{\mathup{{{g}}}\perp})\\
\times\Biggl\{\bigl[1+(1-z)^{2}\bigr]\frac{z^{2}k_{\mathup{{{g}}}\perp}^{2}}{\bigl[(\vec{p}_{\textmd{$\mathup{{{\gamma}}}$ }\perp}-z\vec{}k_{\mathup{{{g}}}\perp})^{2}+\epsilon_{M}^{2}\bigr]\bigl[p_{\textmd{$\mathup{{{\gamma}}}$ }\perp}^{2}+\epsilon_{M}^{2}\bigr]}\\
-z^{2}\epsilon_{M}^{2}\biggl[\frac{1}{p_{\textmd{$\mathup{{{\gamma}}}$ }\perp}^{2}+\epsilon_{M}^{2}}-\frac{1}{(\vec{p}_{\textmd{$\mathup{{{\gamma}}}$ }\perp}-z\vec{}k_{\mathup{{{g}}}\perp})^{2}+\epsilon_{M}^{2}}\biggr]^{2}\Biggr\}which is consistent with other inclusive results derived in references~\cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{Kopeliovich:2000fb,Baier:2004tj,Gelis:2002nn,Gelis:2002fw}{}{}]}.
\par The correlation is defined as the ratio of the double inclusive to single inclusive cross sections. For this work, we integrate over transverse momentum and assume there is no impact parameter dependence, thus considering the correlation only as a function of rapidity, virtual photon mass, and angle.
\begin{equation}C^{\text{DY}}(Y_{\mathup{{{\gamma}}}},Y_{\mathup{{{\pi}}^{\scriptstyle{0}}}},M,\Delta\phi)=\frac{\displaystyle\int_{p_{\textmd{$\mathup{{{\gamma}}}$ }\perp}>p_{\perp\text{cut}}}\uddc\vec{p}_{\textmd{$\mathup{{{\gamma}}}$ }\perp}\int_{p_{\mathup{{{\pi}}}\perp}>p_{\perp\text{cut}}}\uddc\vec{}p_{\mathup{{{\pi}}}\perp}\frac{\udc^{9}\sigma}{\udc Y_{\mathup{{{\gamma}}}}\udc Y_{\mathup{{{\pi}}^{\scriptstyle{0}}}}\uddc\vec{p}_{\textmd{$\mathup{{{\gamma}}}$ }\perp}\uddc\vec{}p_{\mathup{{{\pi}}}\perp}\uddc\vec{b}\,\udc M^{2}}}{\displaystyle\int_{p_{\textmd{$\mathup{{{\gamma}}}$ }\perp}>p_{\perp\text{cut}}}\uddc\vec{p}_{\textmd{$\mathup{{{\gamma}}}$ }\perp}\frac{\udc^{6}\sigma}{\udc Y_{\mathup{{{\gamma}}}}\uddc\vec{p}_{\textmd{$\mathup{{{\gamma}}}$ }\perp}\uddc\vec{b}\,\udc M^{2}}}\end{equation}\par\par\lx@frontmatter@fallback\@@unnumbered@section{section}{toc}{Results}
\par Figures~\ref {fig:corr_rhic_2} and~\ref {fig:corr_lhc_2} show the predictions of this model for kinematic conditions characteristic of RHIC and the LHC, respectively.
The RHIC results in figure~\ref {fig:corr_rhic_2} use $\sqrt{s}=\SI{200}{GeV}$, $A=197$, and $p_{\perp\text{cut}}=\SI{1.5}{GeV}$, and the LHC results use $\sqrt{s}=\SI{8800}{GeV}$, $A=208$, and $p_{\perp\text{cut}}=\SI{1.5}{GeV}$.
\par There are two characteristics that are immediately evident: the double-peak structure surrounding back-to-back emission ($\Delta\phi\approx\pi$), and the variation in the parallel-emission behavior around $\Delta\phi\approx 0,2\pi$.
While the double peak is roughly similar for all three models of the gluon distribution, for most kinematic conditions the BK equation with either fixed or running coupling gives a large excess for parallel emission relative to the GBW model.
Since the BK solution differs from the GBW model primarily in the asymptotic behavior of $\mathcal{F}_{x_{g}}$ at large $k_{\mathup{{{g}}}\perp}$, power versus exponential respectively, we can see that the parallel emission comes primarily from large-transverse momentum gluons, whereas the back-to-back emission comes mostly from low-transverse momentum gluons.
This makes sense intuitively, since if the lepton pair and the hadron are to emerge in nearly the same direction, they'll have to have at least $2p_{\perp\text{cut}}$ of total transverse momentum to be detected, but back-to-back emission can come from a system with little or no transverse momentum.
\par Figure~\ref {fig:corr_momentum_breakdown} shows an explicit breakdown of the momenta $k_{\textmd{$\mathup{{{g}}}$ }\perp}$ that contribute to the correlation at each angle, which confirms this interpretation: the correlation for parallel emission comes from higher momentum than the central peak.
Overall, the correlation at the LHC receives contributions from higher momenta than at RHIC, due to there being more total energy $\sqrt{s}$ available in the collision.
\par\begin{figure}\hbox{\includegraphics[]{generated-figures/corr_rhic_2}}
\@@toccaption{{\lx@tag[ ]{{19}}{Azimuthal angular correlation results for RHIC}}}\@@caption{{\lx@tag[: ]{{\small Figure 19}}{\small The angular correlations between the virtual photons and pions at RHIC, at medium rapidity, $Y_{\mathup{{{\gamma}}}}=Y_{\mathup{{{\pi}}^{\scriptstyle{0}}}}=2.5$. The upper graph shows the correlation for a virtual photon mass of $M=\SI{0.5}{GeV}$, and the lower one, for $M=\SI{4}{GeV}$. In each case, the three curves for GBW, fixed coupling BK, and running coupling BK, exhibit basically the same double-peak structure around $\Delta\phi=\pi$, but they show differing behavior near $\Delta\phi=0,2\pi$, the near side correlation. This relates to the large-$k^{2}$ behavior of the corresponding gluon distributions. Reproduced from figure 5 of~\cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{Stasto:2012ru}{}{}]}.}}}
\end{figure}\par\begin{figure}\hbox{\includegraphics[]{generated-figures/corr_lhc_2}}
\@@toccaption{{\lx@tag[ ]{{20}}{Azimuthal angular correlation results for the LHC}}}\@@caption{{\lx@tag[: ]{{\small Figure 20}}{\small The virtual photon-pion angular correlations at LHC at rapidity $Y_{\gamma}=Y_{\pi}=4$. The upper and lower graphs show $M=\SI{4}{GeV}$ and $M=\SI{8}{GeV}$, respectively. As in figure~\ref {fig:corr_rhic_2}, the key difference is in the near side correlation, arising due to differences in the high-momentum region of the gluon distribution. Reproduced from figure 6 of~\cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{Stasto:2012ru}{}{}]}.}}}
\end{figure}\par\begin{figure}\hbox{\includegraphics[]{generated-figures/corr_momentum_breakdown}}
\@@toccaption{{\lx@tag[ ]{{21}}{Contributions to the correlation split by momentum}}}\@@caption{{\lx@tag[: ]{{\small Figure 21}}{\small Breakdown of the contributions to the correlation, split by the transverse momentum $k_{\textmd{$\mathup{{{g}}}$ }\perp}$ acquired by the quark as it interacts with the gluon field of the target. Momentum ranges are in $\si{GeV}$. The plot on the left corresponds to the top plot of figure~\ref {fig:corr_rhic_2}, with $M=\SI{0.5}{GeV}$ at RHIC, and the one on the right corresponds to the top plot of figure~\ref {fig:corr_lhc_2} with $M=\SI{4}{GeV}$ using LHC parameters.
In both cases, there is a fairly sharp transition between the momenta that contribute to the central back-to-back emission peak and those that contribute to the parallel emission peak. For the RHIC plot on the left, that transition is around $\SI{1}{GeV}$, and on the right, between $\SI{10}{GeV}$ and $\SI{100}{GeV}$.}}}
\end{figure}\par\par\lx@frontmatter@fallback\@@unnumbered@section{subsection}{toc}{Peak Characteristics}
The double peak of figures~\ref {fig:corr_rhic_2} and~\ref {fig:corr_lhc_2} is particularly interesting because it is unique to the Drell-Yan process; it doesn't appear in similar calculations for the correlation between produced hadrons or jets~\cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{Marquet:2007vb}{}{}]}.
We can trace the origin of the central ``dip'' to the kinematic factor which appears in equations~\eqref{eq:doubleinclusiveleptonpairxsec} and~\eqref{eq:singleinclusiveleptonpairxsec}:
\begin{multline}\bigl[1+(1-z)^{2}\bigr]\frac{z^{2}k_{\mathup{{{g}}}\perp}^{2}}{\bigl[(\vec{p}_{\textmd{$\mathup{{{\gamma}}}$ }\perp}-z\vec{}k_{\mathup{{{g}}}\perp})^{2}+\epsilon_{M}^{2}\bigr]\bigl[p_{\textmd{$\mathup{{{\gamma}}}$ }\perp}^{2}+\epsilon_{M}^{2}\bigr]}\\
-z^{2}\epsilon_{M}^{2}\biggl[\frac{1}{p_{\textmd{$\mathup{{{\gamma}}}$ }\perp}^{2}+\epsilon_{M}^{2}}-\frac{1}{(\vec{p}_{\textmd{$\mathup{{{\gamma}}}$ }\perp}-z\vec{}k_{\mathup{{{g}}}\perp})^{2}+\epsilon_{M}^{2}}\biggr]^{2}\end{multline}\bigl[1+(1-z)^{2}\bigr]\frac{z^{2}k_{\mathup{{{g}}}\perp}^{2}}{\bigl[(\vec{p}_{\textmd{$\mathup{{{\gamma}}}$ }\perp}-z\vec{}k_{\mathup{{{g}}}\perp})^{2}+\epsilon_{M}^{2}\bigr]\bigl[p_{\textmd{$\mathup{{{\gamma}}}$ }\perp}^{2}+\epsilon_{M}^{2}\bigr]}\\
-z^{2}\epsilon_{M}^{2}\biggl[\frac{1}{p_{\textmd{$\mathup{{{\gamma}}}$ }\perp}^{2}+\epsilon_{M}^{2}}-\frac{1}{(\vec{p}_{\textmd{$\mathup{{{\gamma}}}$ }\perp}-z\vec{}k_{\mathup{{{g}}}\perp})^{2}+\epsilon_{M}^{2}}\biggr]^{2}This approaches zero as $k_{\mathup{{{g}}}\perp}\to 0$.
If we were analyzing the parton-level cross section~\eqref{eq:partonlevelquarkphotonxsec} as a function of $p_{\textmd{$\mathup{{{\gamma}}}$ }\perp}$ and $k_{\mathit{{q}{}_{\mspace{-3mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{-1mu}\scriptstyle{}}}\perp}$, we would expect to see the correlation drop to zero when $\vec{p}_{\textmd{$\mathup{{{\gamma}}}$ }\perp}+\vec{}k_{\mathit{{q}{}_{\mspace{-3mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{-1mu}\scriptstyle{}}}\perp}=\vec{0}$.
But the double inclusive result~\eqref{eq:doubleinclusiveleptonpairxsec} involves an integration over $z_{2}$, so even when evaluated at specific values of $\vec{p}_{\textmd{$\mathup{{{\gamma}}}$ }\perp}$ and $\vec{}p_{\mathup{{{\pi}}}\perp}$, it incorporates a range of values of $\vec{}k_{\mathup{{{g}}}\perp}=\frac{\vec{}p_{\mathup{{{\pi}}}\perp}}{z_{2}}+\vec{p}_{\textmd{$\mathup{{{\gamma}}}$ }\perp}$.
Thus there won't be any point $(\vec{p}_{\textmd{$\mathup{{{\gamma}}}$ }\perp},\vec{}p_{\mathup{{{\pi}}}\perp})$ where the correlation is exactly zero; effectively, the fragmentation blurs the peak structure.
We also integrate over transverse momentum when calculating the correlation~\eqref{eq:leptonpaircorrelation}, which smooths out the peak even more.
\par Depending on the kinematic conditions, the central suppression may be small enough that a true double peak doesn't appear.
This appears in reference~\cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{JalilianMarian:2012bd}{}{}]}, for example, which shows the same analysis for real photons, which is the $M\to 0$ limit of this calculation, and in figure~\ref {fig:doublepeakvmass} we can see that the peak depth decreases as we move toward lower photon masses.
\par\begin{figure}\hbox{\includegraphics[]{generated-figures/doublepeakwidthvmass}}
\hbox{\includegraphics[]{generated-figures/doublepeakdepthvmass}}
\@@toccaption{{\lx@tag[ ]{{22}}{Variation of peak half-width and depth with $M$}}}\@@caption{{\lx@tag[: ]{{\small Figure 22}}{\small Plots of the half-width of the double peak, on the left, and the depth of the dip, on the right, as a function of virtual photon mass.}}}
\end{figure}\par Because the correlation at the back-to-back emission peak comes from low-momentum regions of the gluon distribution, we can reasonably expect the characteristics of the peak to reflect the presence and nature of saturation.
In particular, the results in figures~\ref {fig:corr_rhic_2} and~\ref {fig:corr_lhc_2} show that the results for the running coupling BK equation, for which the evolution of the saturation scale with $\ln\frac{1}{x_{g}}$ is slower, leads to a suppression of the peak height relative to the fixed coupling.
A larger saturation scale $Q_{s}$ seems to produce a taller peak.
\par Figure~\ref {fig:doublepeakvmass} supports the same conclusion by showing that the fixed coupling BK equation produces a peak which is both shallower and thinner than that of the GBW model.
Looking back at figure~\ref {fig:kmax}, we see that the fixed coupling BK solution has a smaller saturation scale than the GBW model, so again, we see that a large saturation scale makes the double peak larger.
\par\par\lx@frontmatter@fallback\@@unnumbered@section{section}{toc}{Conclusions}
\par There are two main ideas to take away from this chapter.
First, the calculation of the lepton pair-hadron angular correlation serves as an example of how the CGC and hybrid formalism are used in practice.
This lays the groundwork for the next-to-leading order calculation to be shown in the next chapter.
\par Secondly, the correlation is an interesting numerical result in its own right.
It's clear that it is a sensitive probe of the gluon distribution in both high- and low-momentum regions.
We see an enhancement of the correlation around parallel emission, which gives us information about the high-momentum tail of the gluon distribution, and also the unique double peak structure for near-parallel emission, whose properties can reveal the characteristics of the gluon distribution in the low-momentum saturation regime.
\par\lx@frontmatter@fallback\@@unnumbered@section{chapter}{toc}{NLO Inclusive Pion Production}
\par Leading order calculations, like that in the last chapter, typically give a decent approximation to measured cross sections.
But there are several reasons to look to the next order of perturbation theory in hopes of improving the results.
Of course, one generally expects that adding more terms to a perturbation series will give a more accurate approximation of the exact (all-order) result.
Beyond that, though, the cross section depends on the renormalization and factorization scales $\mu$ through terms which are subleading in $\alpha_{\text{s}}$.
If we are able to calculate some of those terms explicitly, the remaining dependence on $\mu$, which is unknown and thus must be considered a theoretical uncertainty, will be reduced.
\par Calculating the subleading corrections to a cross section is not a trivial task.
Beyond leading order, the number of diagrams that contribute grows drastically, often roughly exponentially with the order.
There are also additional kinematic degrees of freedom, corresponding to the momenta of emitted particles, which make the calculation of each diagram more complicated, and can introduce divergences which need to be regulated.
\par In this chapter I'll review the calculations for the inclusive process $\mathup{{{p}}}\mathit{{A}{}_{\mspace{-3mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{-1mu}\scriptstyle{}}}\HepTo\mathup{{{\pi}}}\mathit{{X}{}_{\mspace{-3mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{-1mu}\scriptstyle{}}}$ including all next-to-leading order corrections.
The leading order calculation was performed in reference~\cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{Dumitru:2002qt}{}{}]}, and~\cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{Dumitru:2005gt,Fujii:2011fh,Albacete:2012xq,Rezaeian:2012ye}{}{}]} added various subleading corrections.
However, the complete LO+NLO result was only derived in~\cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{Chirilli:2012jd}{}{}]} (summarized in~\cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{Chirilli:2011km}{}{}]}), and numerically implemented in~\cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{Stasto:2013cha}{}{}]} with the program SOLO, ``Saturation at One-Loop Order''~\cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{SOLO}{}{}]}.
\par\par\lx@frontmatter@fallback\@@unnumbered@section{section}{toc}{Kinematics}
As in section~\ref {sec:pakinematics}, we take the proton and nucleon to have momenta in the $\pm z$ directions,
\@@amsalign
leading to $p_{\textmd{$\mathup{{{p}}}$ }}^{+}=p_{\textmd{$\mathit{{A}{}_{\mspace{-3mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{-1mu}\scriptstyle{}}}$ }}^{-}=\sqrt{s}$ (equation~\eqref{eq:equaltosqrts}). And, again, the fundamental collision involves one parton from the proton with plus component momentum $x_{p}p_{\textmd{$\mathup{{{p}}}$ }}^{+}$, and one parton from the nucleus, with a minus component of momentum $x_{g}p_{\textmd{$\mathit{{A}{}_{\mspace{-3mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{-1mu}\scriptstyle{}}}$ }}^{-}$ and transverse momentum $\vec{k}_{g\perp}$. $p^{\mu}$ represents the momentum of the produced hadron.
\par\par\lx@frontmatter@fallback\@@unnumbered@section{subsection}{toc}{Leading Order}
Applying conservation of momentum at the interaction vertex of figure~\ref {fig:qqlodiagram}, assuming that $p_{p}^{-}=p_{\textmd{$\mathit{{A}{}_{\mspace{-3mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{-1mu}\scriptstyle{}}}$ }}^{+}=0$ (equations~\eqref{eq:pluscomponent} and~\eqref{eq:minuscomponent}), gives
\@@amsalign
From equation~\eqref{eq:momentumpm}, assuming the quark mass is negligible ($k^{\mu}k_{\mu}=0$), these become
\@@amsalign
and then using equation~\eqref{eq:equaltosqrts} and $k_{\perp}=\frac{p_{\perp}}{z}$ we get
\@@amsalign
Evidently, the measured momentum of the detected hadron determines everything except one unknown momentum fraction. We can choose any of $z$, $x_{p}^{\text{LO}}$, or $x_{g}^{\text{LO}}$ to parametrize this unknown degree of freedom, and consequently, the formula for the cross section will involve a single integral over the chosen variable.
\par\begin{figure}\hbox{\includegraphics[]{generated-figures/qqlodiagram}}
\@@toccaption{{\lx@tag[ ]{{23}}{Feynman diagram for $\mathup{{{p}}}\mathit{{A}{}_{\mspace{-3mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{-1mu}\scriptstyle{}}}\HepTo\mathit{{h}{}_{\mspace{-3mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{-1mu}\scriptstyle{}}}\mathit{{X}{}_{\mspace{-3mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{-1mu}\scriptstyle{}}}$ at leading order in the quark-quark channel}}}\@@caption{{\lx@tag[: ]{{\small Figure 23}}{\small Feynman diagram for $\mathup{{{p}}}\mathit{{A}{}_{\mspace{-3mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{-1mu}\scriptstyle{}}}\HepTo\mathit{{h}{}_{\mspace{-3mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{-1mu}\scriptstyle{}}}\mathit{{X}{}_{\mspace{-3mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{-1mu}\scriptstyle{}}}$ at leading order in the quark-quark channel}}}
\end{figure}\par\par\lx@frontmatter@fallback\@@unnumbered@section{subsection}{toc}{Next-to-Leading Order}
\par At next-to-leading order, the momentum of the emitted gluon is also unknown, which adds an additional degree of freedom, so the formula will involve an additional integral.
It's most convenient to express the NLO formulas in terms of a new variable, $\xi$, representing the fraction of $+$-component momentum retained by the original parton after it emits a gluon. For example, in figure~\ref {fig:qqnlodiagram},
\@@amsalign
We can then choose any two of $z$, $x_{p}^{\text{NLO}}$, $x_{g}^{\text{NLO}}$, and $\xi$ to parametrize the region of integration for this double integral.
The integration region for various choices is shown in figure~\ref {fig:integrationdomain}.
\par\begin{figure}\hbox{\includegraphics[]{generated-figures/integrationdomain1}}
\hskip 28.45274pt
\hbox{\includegraphics[]{generated-figures/integrationdomain2}}
\hskip 28.45274pt
\hbox{\includegraphics[]{generated-figures/integrationdomain3}}
\@@toccaption{{\lx@tag[ ]{{24}}{Domains of integration in terms of various kinematic variables}}}\@@caption{{\lx@tag[: ]{{\small Figure 24}}{\small Domains of integration over the kinematic variables $z$, $x_{p}^{\text{NLO}}$, and $\xi$. Any two of the variables are sufficient to parametrize the integration domain, and the others can be taken as functions of whichever two are chosen as primary. The different images show the shape of the integration region for various choices of the two primary variables.}}}
\end{figure}\par As a momentum fraction, $\xi$ clearly cannot exceed $1$. To determine the lower bound on this new integral, though, we will need to express $\xi$ in terms of the other kinematic variables. Starting with momentum conservation,
\lx@equationgroup@subnumbering@begin
\@@amsalign
\lx@equationgroup@subnumbering@end
and plugging the definition~\eqref{eq:xidef} into equation~\eqref{eq:nloconservationplus} leads to
\begin{equation}x_{p}^{\text{NLO}}p_{\textmd{$\mathup{{{p}}}$ }}^{+}=k^{+}+(1-\xi)x_{p}^{\text{NLO}}p_{\textmd{$\mathup{{{p}}}$ }}^{+}\end{equation}Solving this for $\xi$ and simplifying gives
\@@amsalign
Or, inverting the relation, we have
\begin{equation}x_{p}^{\text{NLO}}=\frac{p_{\perp}}{z\xi\sqrt{s}}e^{Y}\end{equation}\par Now looking at~\eqref{eq:nloconservationminus}, since the radiated gluon must be on shell ($k_{g}^{2}=0$) we have, for a gluon with nonzero momentum,
\begin{equation}k_{g}^{-}=\frac{k_{\mathup{{{g}}}\perp}^{2}}{k_{g}^{+}}=\frac{k_{\mathup{{{g}}}\perp}^{2}}{(1-\xi)x_{p}^{\text{NLO}}p_{\textmd{$\mathup{{{p}}}$ }}^{+}}\end{equation}and assuming that the quark is massless, $m_{k}\approx 0$, in combination with equation~\eqref{eq:firstsimplifiedxi} we have
\begin{equation}k^{-}\approx\frac{k_{\perp}^{2}}{k_{+}}=\frac{k_{\perp}^{2}}{\xi x_{p}^{\text{NLO}}p_{\textmd{$\mathup{{{p}}}$ }}^{+}}\end{equation}Both of these contain the combination $x_{p}^{\text{NLO}}p_{\textmd{$\mathup{{{p}}}$ }}^{+}$ in the denominator, which can be rewritten as
\begin{equation}\frac{1}{x_{p}^{\text{NLO}}p_{\textmd{$\mathup{{{p}}}$ }}^{+}}=\frac{z\xi\sqrt{s}}{p_{\perp}}e^{-Y}\frac{1}{\sqrt{s}}=\frac{z\xi}{p_{\perp}}e^{-Y}\end{equation}Then plugging equation~\eqref{eq:reciprocalxppplus} into~\eqref{eq:simplifykgminus} and~\eqref{eq:simplifykminus} and those into equation~\eqref{eq:nloconservationminus} gives
\begin{equation}x_{g}^{\text{NLO}}p_{\textmd{$\mathit{{A}{}_{\mspace{-3mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{-1mu}\scriptstyle{}}}$ }}^{-}=\frac{zk_{\perp}^{2}}{p_{\perp}}e^{-Y}+\frac{z\xi k_{\mathup{{{g}}}\perp}^{2}}{(1-\xi)p_{\perp}}e^{-Y}\end{equation}Since $z=\frac{p_{\perp}}{k_{\perp}}$, this simplifies to
\begin{equation}x_{g}^{\text{NLO}}p_{\textmd{$\mathit{{A}{}_{\mspace{-3mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{-1mu}\scriptstyle{}}}$ }}^{-}=k_{\perp}e^{-Y}+\frac{\xi k_{\mathup{{{g}}}\perp}^{2}}{(1-\xi)k_{\perp}}e^{-Y}\end{equation}From conservation of transverse momentum, equation~\eqref{eq:nloconservationperp}, we have $k_{\mathup{{{g}}}\perp}^{2}=(\vec{q}_{\perp}-\vec{}k_{\perp})^{2}$, so
\begin{equation}x_{g}^{\text{NLO}}p_{\textmd{$\mathit{{A}{}_{\mspace{-3mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{-1mu}\scriptstyle{}}}$ }}^{-}=k_{\perp}e^{-Y}+\frac{(\vec{q}_{\perp}-\vec{}k_{\perp})^{2}}{k_{\perp}}\frac{\xi}{1-\xi}e^{-Y}\end{equation}and finally, setting $p_{\textmd{$\mathit{{A}{}_{\mspace{-3mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{-1mu}\scriptstyle{}}}$ }}^{-}=\sqrt{s}$, we can solve for $x_{g}^{\text{NLO}}$ as
\begin{equation}x_{g}^{\text{NLO}}=\frac{k_{\perp}}{\sqrt{s}}e^{-Y}+\frac{(\vec{}q_{\perp}-\vec{}k_{\perp})^{2}}{k_{\perp}\sqrt{s}}\frac{\xi}{1-\xi}e^{-Y}=x_{g}^{\text{LO}}\biggl(1+\frac{(\vec{}q_{\perp}-\vec{}k_{\perp})^{2}}{k_{\perp}^{2}}\frac{\xi}{1-\xi}\biggr)\end{equation}\par In practice, it is sometimes beneficial to assume the gluon transverse momentum $\vec{}q_{\perp}$ is negligible or zero. This is approximately valid because the gluon distribution $\mathcal{F}_{x_{g}}$ falls off exponentially outside $q_{\perp}\lesssim Q_{s}$, so the contribution of the region $q_{\perp}\gtrsim Q_{s}$ to the cross section can be neglected. When $Q_{s}\ll k_{\perp}$ that means we drop terms subleading in $q_{\perp}/k_{\perp}$, resulting in
\begin{equation}x_{g}^{\text{NLO}}=\frac{k_{\perp}}{\sqrt{s}}e^{-Y}+\frac{k_{\perp}}{\sqrt{s}}\frac{\xi}{1-\xi}e^{-Y}=\frac{k_{\perp}}{\sqrt{s}}\frac{e^{-Y}}{1-\xi}\end{equation}\par As a momentum fraction, $x_{g}^{\text{NLO}}$ has to satisfy $x_{g}^{\text{NLO}}<1$.
Using~\eqref{eq:xgnlo}, that means the gluon momentum fraction $\xi$ is kinematically limited to
\begin{equation}\xi<\frac{1-x_{g}^{\text{LO}}}{1-x_{g}^{\text{LO}}+x_{g}^{\text{LO}}(\vec{}q_{\perp}-\vec{}k_{\perp})^{2}/k_{\perp}^{2}}\end{equation}This excludes $\xi=1$ unless $\vec{}k_{\perp}=\vec{}q_{\perp}$; physically, it corresponds to the fact that the gluon can only carry all the longitudinal momentum if the final state quark (prior to fragmentation) carries all the transverse momentum.
For simplicity, we ignore this limit for the basic computation described in chapter~\ref {ch:results} and reintroduce it in chapter~\ref {ch:beyondnlo}.
\par\par\lx@frontmatter@fallback\@@unnumbered@section{section}{toc}{NLO Cross Section Expressions}
\par As previously mentioned, several papers~\cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{Dumitru:2002qt,Dumitru:2005gt,Fujii:2011fh,Albacete:2012xq,Rezaeian:2012ye}{}{}]} spanning the decade from 2002 to 2012 calculated progressively more precise formulas for the $\mathrm{p}A\to hX$ cross section.
However, reference~\cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{Chirilli:2012jd}{}{}]} was the first to incorporate the complete NLO corrections, the formulas implemented by SOLO.
In this section I'll review the derivation of selected terms to demonstrate the process.
\par\begin{figure}\hbox{\includegraphics[]{generated-figures/qqnlodiagram}}
\@@toccaption{{\lx@tag[ ]{{25}}{A representative Feynman diagram for $\mathup{{{p}}}\mathit{{A}{}_{\mspace{-3mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{-1mu}\scriptstyle{}}}\HepTo\mathit{{h}{}_{\mspace{-3mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{-1mu}\scriptstyle{}}}\mathit{{X}{}_{\mspace{-3mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{-1mu}\scriptstyle{}}}$ at next-to-leading order in the quark-quark channel}}}\@@caption{{\lx@tag[: ]{{\small Figure 25}}{\small A representative Feynman diagram for $\mathup{{{p}}}\mathit{{A}{}_{\mspace{-3mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{-1mu}\scriptstyle{}}}\HepTo\mathit{{h}{}_{\mspace{-3mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{-1mu}\scriptstyle{}}}\mathit{{X}{}_{\mspace{-3mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{-1mu}\scriptstyle{}}}$ at next-to-leading order in the quark-quark channel}}}
\end{figure}\par\par\lx@frontmatter@fallback\@@unnumbered@section{subsection}{toc}{Leading order}
\par The leading order contributions, shown in figure~\ref {fig:locompounddiagrams}, can be calculated in much the same way as in chapter~\ref {ch:correlation}.
