We can compute the amplitude for interaction between a quark and the gluon field of the nucleus, $\mathit{{q}{}_{\mspace{-3mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{-1mu}\scriptstyle{}}}\mathit{{A}{}_{\mspace{-3mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{-1mu}\scriptstyle{}}}\HepTo\mathit{{q}{}_{\mspace{-3mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{-1mu}\scriptstyle{}}}\mathit{{X}{}_{\mspace{-3mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{-1mu}\scriptstyle{}}}$, as the Wilson line~\eqref{eq:wilsonline} in the fundamental representation (or its Fourier transform, to get it as a function of momentum):
\begin{equation}\mathcal{A}=\int\frac{\uddc\vec{}x_{\perp}}{2\pi}e^{-i\vec{}k_{\perp}\cdot\vec{}x_{\perp}}U(\vec{}x_{\perp})\end{equation}To get the cross section, we multiply this by its complex conjugate to get
\begin{equation}\abs{\mathcal{A}}^{2}=\int\frac{\uddc\vec{}x_{\perp}\uddc\vec{}y_{\perp}}{(2\pi)^{2}}e^{-i\vec{}k_{\perp}\cdot\vec{}x_{\perp}}e^{i\vec{}k_{\perp}\cdot\vec{}y_{\perp}}\trace U(\vec{}x_{\perp})\herm{U}(\vec{}y_{\perp})\end{equation}We then average over the unknown color charge distribution of the nucleus (just as we would average over initial spins in a QED calculation). The averaging operation is represented by $\frac{1}{N_{c}}\braket{\cdots}_{x_{g}}$, and the resulting parton-level cross section is the momentum space dipole gluon distribution $\mathcal{F}_{x_{g}}$ from equation~\eqref{eq:dipoleFdefinition}.
\@@amsalign
The subscript $x_{g}$ indicates the longitudinal momentum fraction of the gluons involved in the interaction. This gives the cross section for one flavor of quark interacting with the gluon field of the nucleus.
\par We also have to include the contribution for a gluon interacting with the nucleus, $\mathup{{{g}}}\mathit{{A}{}_{\mspace{-3mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{-1mu}\scriptstyle{}}}\HepTo\mathup{{{g}}}\mathit{{X}{}_{\mspace{-3mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{-1mu}\scriptstyle{}}}$.
This is the same but using the adjoint Wilson line~\eqref{eq:adjointwilsonline}.
Accordingly, the square of the amplitude is\begin{equation}\abs{\mathcal{A}}^{2}=\int\frac{\uddc\vec{}x_{\perp}\uddc\vec{}y_{\perp}}{(2\pi)^{2}}e^{-i\vec{}k_{\perp}\cdot\vec{}x_{\perp}}e^{i\vec{}k_{\perp}\cdot\vec{}y_{\perp}}\trace W^{ab}(\vec{}x_{\perp})\herm{W}^{ab}(\vec{}y_{\perp})\end{equation}Using identity~\eqref{eq:ident:wilsonlinereduce} the product becomes
\begin{equation}4\trace\Bigl\{\trace\bigl[T^{a}U(\vec{}x_{\perp})T^{b}\herm U(\vec{}x_{\perp})\bigr]\trace\bigl[\herm U(\vec{}y_{\perp})T^{b}U(\vec{}y_{\perp})T^{a}\bigr]\Bigr\}\end{equation}With the matrix indices explicit, this is
\begin{equation}4\trace\Bigl\{T_{ij}^{a}U_{jk}(\vec{}x_{\perp})T_{kl}^{b}\herm U_{li}(\vec{}x_{\perp})\herm U_{pq}(\vec{}y_{\perp})T_{qr}^{b}U_{rs}(\vec{}y_{\perp})T_{sp}^{a}\Bigr\}\end{equation}Using the identity $T_{ij}^{a}T_{kl}^{b}=\frac{1}{2}\delta_{il}\delta_{jk}-\frac{1}{2N_{c}}\delta_{ij}\delta_{kl}$, after a slightly tedious calculation and much relabeling of indices this can be reduced to
\begin{multline}\trace\Bigl[U_{jk}(\vec{}x_{\perp})\herm U_{kj}(\vec{}y_{\perp})U_{il}(\vec{}y_{\perp})\herm U_{li}(\vec{}x_{\perp})-\frac{1}{N_{c}}U_{jk}(\vec{}x_{\perp})\herm U_{ki}(\vec{}x_{\perp})U_{il}(\vec{}y_{\perp})\herm U_{lj}(\vec{}y_{\perp})\\
-\frac{1}{N_{c}}U_{il}(\vec{}x_{\perp})\herm U_{lj}(\vec{}y_{\perp})U_{jk}(\vec{}y_{\perp})\herm U_{ki}(\vec{}x_{\perp})+\frac{1}{N_{c}^{2}}\underbrace{U_{ik}(\vec{}x_{\perp})\herm U_{ki}(\vec{}x_{\perp})}_{N_{c}}\underbrace{U_{jl}(\vec{}y_{\perp})\herm U_{lj}(\vec{}y_{\perp})}_{N_{c}}\Bigr]\end{multline}\trace\Bigl[U_{jk}(\vec{}x_{\perp})\herm U_{kj}(\vec{}y_{\perp})U_{il}(\vec{}y_{\perp})\herm U_{li}(\vec{}x_{\perp})-\frac{1}{N_{c}}U_{jk}(\vec{}x_{\perp})\herm U_{ki}(\vec{}x_{\perp})U_{il}(\vec{}y_{\perp})\herm U_{lj}(\vec{}y_{\perp})\\
-\frac{1}{N_{c}}U_{il}(\vec{}x_{\perp})\herm U_{lj}(\vec{}y_{\perp})U_{jk}(\vec{}y_{\perp})\herm U_{ki}(\vec{}x_{\perp})+\frac{1}{N_{c}^{2}}\underbrace{U_{ik}(\vec{}x_{\perp})\herm U_{ki}(\vec{}x_{\perp})}_{N_{c}}\underbrace{U_{jl}(\vec{}y_{\perp})\herm U_{lj}(\vec{}y_{\perp})}_{N_{c}}\Bigr]In the large-$N_{c}$ limit, the middle two terms vanish, and we're left with
\begin{equation}\trace\bigl[U(\vec{}x_{\perp})\herm U(\vec{}y_{\perp})\bigr]\trace\bigl[U(\vec{}y_{\perp})\herm U(\vec{}x_{\perp})\bigr]-1\end{equation}which, after color-averaging with $\frac{1}{N_{c}^{2}-1}\braket{\cdots}_{x_{g}}$, leaves us with
\@@amsalign
In the last line I've used the mean-field limit to assume we can consider the two contracted Wilson line pairs to be uncorrelated.
If we separate the two terms inside parentheses, the $-1$ leads to a delta function $\delta^{(2)}(\vec{}k_{\perp})$ after integration, but in practice we always evaluate the gluon distribution at finite momentum, so we can safely drop that term.
\par To construct the final cross section for $\mathup{{{p}}}\mathit{{A}{}_{\mspace{-3mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{-1mu}\scriptstyle{}}}\HepTo\mathit{{h}{}_{\mspace{-3mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{-1mu}\scriptstyle{}}}\mathit{{X}{}_{\mspace{-3mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{-1mu}\scriptstyle{}}}$, we multiply the parton-level quark cross section by the quark distribution function $x_{p}q_{i}(x_{p})$ and the fragmentation function $D_{\mathit{{h}{}_{\mspace{-3mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{-1mu}\scriptstyle{}}}/i}(z)/z^{2}$, and similarly for the gluon cross section, and integrate over the longitudinal momentum space parametrized by $z$, $x_{p}$, or $x_{g}$ as described in section~\ref {sec:xseckinematics}:
\begin{equation}\frac{\udddc\sigma_{\text{LO}}^{\mathrm{p}A\to hX}}{\udc Y\uddc\vec{p}_{\perp}}=\int_{\tau}^{1}\frac{\udc z}{z^{2}}\Biggl[\sum_{\text{flavors }q}x_{p}q(x_{p})D_{\mathit{{h}{}_{\mspace{-3mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{-1mu}\scriptstyle{}}}/q}(z)\mathcal{F}_{x_{g}}(\vec{}k_{\perp})+x_{p}g(x_{p})D_{\mathit{{h}{}_{\mspace{-3mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{-1mu}\scriptstyle{}}}/g}(z)\tilde{\mathcal{F}}_{x_{g}}(x_{p},k_{\perp})\Biggr]\end{equation}where $\tau=\frac{p_{\perp}}{\sqrt{s}}e^{Y}$.
\par\begin{figure}\hbox{\includegraphics[]{generated-figures/locompounddiagrams}}
\@@toccaption{{\lx@tag[ ]{{26}}{Diagrams contributing to $\mathup{{{p}}}\mathit{{A}{}_{\mspace{-3mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{-1mu}\scriptstyle{}}}\HepTo\mathit{{h}{}_{\mspace{-3mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{-1mu}\scriptstyle{}}}\mathit{{X}{}_{\mspace{-3mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{-1mu}\scriptstyle{}}}$ at leading order}}}\@@caption{{\lx@tag[: ]{{\small Figure 26}}{\small Diagrams contributing to $\mathup{{{p}}}\mathit{{A}{}_{\mspace{-3mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{-1mu}\scriptstyle{}}}\HepTo\mathit{{h}{}_{\mspace{-3mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{-1mu}\scriptstyle{}}}\mathit{{X}{}_{\mspace{-3mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{-1mu}\scriptstyle{}}}$ at leading order}}}
\end{figure}\par\par\lx@frontmatter@fallback\@@unnumbered@section{subsection}{toc}{Next-to-Leading Order Example}
\par\begin{figure}\hbox{\includegraphics[]{generated-figures/nloqqcompounddiagrams}}
\@@toccaption{{\lx@tag[ ]{{27}}{Diagrams contribution to $\mathup{{{p}}}\mathit{{A}{}_{\mspace{-3mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{-1mu}\scriptstyle{}}}\HepTo\mathit{{h}{}_{\mspace{-3mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{-1mu}\scriptstyle{}}}\mathit{{X}{}_{\mspace{-3mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{-1mu}\scriptstyle{}}}$ in the quark-quark channel at next-to-leading order}}}\@@caption{{\lx@tag[: ]{{\small Figure 27}}{\small Diagrams contributing to the quark-quark channel at next-to-leading order. The left column shows real diagrams, in which the emitted gluon is an unmeasured final-state particle, and the right column shows virtual diagrams, in which the gluon is emitted and reabsorbed.}}}
\end{figure}\par The next-to-leading order contributions to the cross section arise from Feynman diagrams in which a gluon is emitted and/or absorbed by the parton coming from the proton. Naturally, this leads to a greatly increased number of Feynman diagrams; for example, figure~\ref {fig:nloqqcompounddiagrams} shows only those diagrams in which the initial- and final-state partons are both a quark, the $qq$ (or $q\to qg$ where the hadron comes from the final-state quark) channel. In addition to those, there are diagrams in which the initial- and final-state partons are both a gluon, the $gg$ channel (which comprises both $g\to gg$, and $g\to gq$ where the produced hadron comes from the gluon rather than the quark). There are also off-diagonal channels in which the initial- and final-state partons are different: the $qg$ channel ($q\to qg$ where the hadron comes from the final-state gluon) and $gq$ channel ($g\to gq$ where the hadron comes from the final-state quark).
\par The formulas for all these contributions are derived in references~\cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{Chirilli:2012jd}{}{}]}, which uses earlier results from~\cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{Marquet:2007vb,Dominguez:2011wm}{}{}]}.
Let's look at the quark-quark channel, for which the diagrams are shown in figure~\ref {fig:nloqqcompounddiagrams}, as an example.
\par The derivation proceeds in much the same way as that of the Drell-Yan process of chapter~\ref {ch:correlation}.
Using the general framework of light-cone perturbation theory and the CGC rules described in chapter~\ref {ch:saturation}, it's straightforward to see that the real diagrams, on the left side of figure~\ref {fig:nloqqcompounddiagrams}, correspond to the expression
\begin{multline}\frac{\udc^{6}\sigma}{\udddc\vec{k}_{\textmd{$\mathit{{q}{}_{\mspace{-3mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{-1mu}\scriptstyle{}}}$ }}\udddc\vec{k}_{\textmd{$\mathup{{{g}}}$ }}}=\alpha_{\text{s}}C_{F}\delta(x_{p}p_{\textmd{$\mathup{{{p}}}$ }}^{+}-k_{\textmd{$\mathit{{q}{}_{\mspace{-3mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{-1mu}\scriptstyle{}}}$ }}^{+}-k_{\textmd{$\mathup{{{g}}}$ }}^{+})\int\frac{\uddc\vec{}x_{\perp}}{(2\pi)^{2}}\frac{\uddc\vec{}x_{\perp}^{\prime}}{(2\pi)^{2}}\frac{\uddc\vec{}b_{\perp}}{(2\pi)^{2}}\frac{\uddc\vec{}b_{\perp}^{\prime}}{(2\pi)^{2}}\\
\times e^{-i\vec{k}_{\textmd{$\mathup{{{g}}}$ }\perp}\cdot(\vec{}x_{\perp}-\vec{}x_{\perp}^{\prime})}e^{-i\vec{k}_{\textmd{$\mathit{{q}{}_{\mspace{-3mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{-1mu}\scriptstyle{}}}$ }\perp}\cdot(\vec{}b_{\perp}-\vec{}b_{\perp}^{\prime})}\sum_{\lambda\alpha\beta}\conj{(\psi_{\alpha\beta}^{\lambda})}(\vec{}u_{\perp}^{\prime})\psi_{\alpha\beta}^{\lambda}(\vec{}u_{\perp})\\
\times\bigl[S_{x_{g}}^{(6)}(\vec{}b_{\perp},\vec{}x_{\perp},\vec{}b_{\perp}^{\prime},\vec{}x_{\perp}^{\prime})+S_{x_{g}}^{(2)}(\vec{}v_{\perp},\vec{}v_{\perp}^{\prime})-S_{x_{g}}^{(3)}(\vec{}b_{\perp},\vec{}x_{\perp},\vec{}v_{\perp}^{\prime})-S_{x_{g}}^{(3)}(\vec{}v_{\perp},\vec{}x_{\perp}^{\prime},\vec{}b_{\perp}^{\prime})\bigr]\end{multline}\frac{\udc^{6}\sigma}{\udddc\vec{k}_{\textmd{$\mathit{{q}{}_{\mspace{-3mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{-1mu}\scriptstyle{}}}$ }}\udddc\vec{k}_{\textmd{$\mathup{{{g}}}$ }}}=\alpha_{\text{s}}C_{F}\delta(x_{p}p_{\textmd{$\mathup{{{p}}}$ }}^{+}-k_{\textmd{$\mathit{{q}{}_{\mspace{-3mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{-1mu}\scriptstyle{}}}$ }}^{+}-k_{\textmd{$\mathup{{{g}}}$ }}^{+})\int\frac{\uddc\vec{}x_{\perp}}{(2\pi)^{2}}\frac{\uddc\vec{}x_{\perp}^{\prime}}{(2\pi)^{2}}\frac{\uddc\vec{}b_{\perp}}{(2\pi)^{2}}\frac{\uddc\vec{}b_{\perp}^{\prime}}{(2\pi)^{2}}\\
\times e^{-i\vec{k}_{\textmd{$\mathup{{{g}}}$ }\perp}\cdot(\vec{}x_{\perp}-\vec{}x_{\perp}^{\prime})}e^{-i\vec{k}_{\textmd{$\mathit{{q}{}_{\mspace{-3mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{-1mu}\scriptstyle{}}}$ }\perp}\cdot(\vec{}b_{\perp}-\vec{}b_{\perp}^{\prime})}\sum_{\lambda\alpha\beta}\conj{(\psi_{\alpha\beta}^{\lambda})}(\vec{}u_{\perp}^{\prime})\psi_{\alpha\beta}^{\lambda}(\vec{}u_{\perp})\\
\times\bigl[S_{x_{g}}^{(6)}(\vec{}b_{\perp},\vec{}x_{\perp},\vec{}b_{\perp}^{\prime},\vec{}x_{\perp}^{\prime})+S_{x_{g}}^{(2)}(\vec{}v_{\perp},\vec{}v_{\perp}^{\prime})-S_{x_{g}}^{(3)}(\vec{}b_{\perp},\vec{}x_{\perp},\vec{}v_{\perp}^{\prime})-S_{x_{g}}^{(3)}(\vec{}v_{\perp},\vec{}x_{\perp}^{\prime},\vec{}b_{\perp}^{\prime})\bigr]where
\@@amsalign
and similarly for the primed quantities, and
\@@amsalign
As explained in chapter~\ref {ch:saturation}, each product of the form $UW$ arises from the interaction of the gluon field of the nucleus with the quark and the emitted gluon, whereas ``isolated'' fundamental Wilson lines (a $U$ without an associated factor of $W$) comes from interaction of the quark with the nucleus prior to emission of the gluon.
Accordingly, we can tell that the $S_{x_{g}}^{(6)}$ term arises from the top diagram on the left in figure~\ref {fig:nloqqcompounddiagrams}, the $S_{x_{g}}^{(2)}$ term comes from the second diagram down, and each of the two $S_{x_{g}}^{(3)}$ terms comes from one of the bottom diagrams on the left side of the figure.
\par Since we are computing an inclusive cross section, we'll integrate over the phase space of the unmeasured particle, here the emitted gluon.
After integrating over $\vec{k}_{\textmd{$\mathup{{{g}}}$ }}$, we get a factor of $\delta^{(2)}(\vec{}x_{\perp}-\vec{}x_{\perp}^{\prime})$, which simplifies $S_{x_{g}}^{(6)}(\vec{}b_{\perp},\vec{}x_{\perp},\vec{}b_{\perp}^{\prime},\vec{}x_{\perp}^{\prime})\to S_{x_{g}}^{(2)}(\vec{}b_{\perp},\vec{}b_{\perp}^{\prime})$. The expression~\eqref{eq:unintegratedrealqqresult} becomes
\begin{multline}\frac{\udc^{3}\sigma}{\udddc\vec{k}_{\textmd{$\mathit{{q}{}_{\mspace{-3mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{-1mu}\scriptstyle{}}}$ }}}=\alpha_{\text{s}}C_{F}\int\frac{\uddc\vec{}x_{\perp}}{(2\pi)^{2}}\frac{\uddc\vec{}b_{\perp}}{(2\pi)^{2}}\frac{\uddc\vec{}b_{\perp}^{\prime}}{(2\pi)^{2}}e^{-i\vec{k}_{\textmd{$\mathit{{q}{}_{\mspace{-3mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{-1mu}\scriptstyle{}}}$ }\perp}\cdot(\vec{}b_{\perp}-\vec{}b_{\perp}^{\prime})}\sum_{\lambda\alpha\beta}\conj{(\psi_{\alpha\beta}^{\lambda})}(\vec{}u_{\perp}^{\prime})\psi_{\alpha\beta}^{\lambda}(\vec{}u_{\perp})\\
\times\bigl[S_{x_{g}}^{(2)}(\vec{}b_{\perp},\vec{}b_{\perp}^{\prime})+S_{x_{g}}^{(2)}(\vec{}v_{\perp},\vec{}v_{\perp}^{\prime})-S_{x_{g}}^{(3)}(\vec{}b_{\perp},\vec{}x_{\perp},\vec{}v_{\perp}^{\prime})-S_{x_{g}}^{(3)}(\vec{}v_{\perp},\vec{}x_{\perp},\vec{}b_{\perp}^{\prime})\bigr]\end{multline}\frac{\udc^{3}\sigma}{\udddc\vec{k}_{\textmd{$\mathit{{q}{}_{\mspace{-3mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{-1mu}\scriptstyle{}}}$ }}}=\alpha_{\text{s}}C_{F}\int\frac{\uddc\vec{}x_{\perp}}{(2\pi)^{2}}\frac{\uddc\vec{}b_{\perp}}{(2\pi)^{2}}\frac{\uddc\vec{}b_{\perp}^{\prime}}{(2\pi)^{2}}e^{-i\vec{k}_{\textmd{$\mathit{{q}{}_{\mspace{-3mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{-1mu}\scriptstyle{}}}$ }\perp}\cdot(\vec{}b_{\perp}-\vec{}b_{\perp}^{\prime})}\sum_{\lambda\alpha\beta}\conj{(\psi_{\alpha\beta}^{\lambda})}(\vec{}u_{\perp}^{\prime})\psi_{\alpha\beta}^{\lambda}(\vec{}u_{\perp})\\
\times\bigl[S_{x_{g}}^{(2)}(\vec{}b_{\perp},\vec{}b_{\perp}^{\prime})+S_{x_{g}}^{(2)}(\vec{}v_{\perp},\vec{}v_{\perp}^{\prime})-S_{x_{g}}^{(3)}(\vec{}b_{\perp},\vec{}x_{\perp},\vec{}v_{\perp}^{\prime})-S_{x_{g}}^{(3)}(\vec{}v_{\perp},\vec{}x_{\perp},\vec{}b_{\perp}^{\prime})\bigr]where now
\@@amsalign
\par For the virtual diagrams, in the right column of figure~\ref {fig:nloqqcompounddiagrams}, the same procedure results in the expression
\begin{multline}\frac{\udddc\sigma}{\udddc\vec{k}_{\textmd{$\mathit{{q}{}_{\mspace{-3mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{-1mu}\scriptstyle{}}}$ }}}=-2\alpha_{\text{s}}C_{F}\int\frac{\uddc\vec{}v_{\perp}}{(2\pi)^{2}}\frac{\uddc\vec{}v_{\perp}^{\prime}}{(2\pi)^{2}}\frac{\uddc\vec{}u_{\perp}}{(2\pi)^{2}}e^{-i\vec{k}_{\textmd{$\mathit{{q}{}_{\mspace{-3mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{-1mu}\scriptstyle{}}}$ }\perp}\cdot(\vec{}v_{\perp}-\vec{}v_{\perp}^{\prime})}\\
\times\sum_{\lambda\alpha\beta}\conj{(\psi_{\alpha\beta}^{\lambda})}(\vec{}u_{\perp})\psi_{\alpha\beta}^{\lambda}(\vec{}u_{\perp})\bigl[S_{x_{g}}^{(2)}(\vec{}v_{\perp},\vec{}v_{\perp}^{\prime})-S_{x_{g}}^{(3)}(\vec{}b_{\perp},\vec{}x_{\perp},\vec{}v_{\perp}^{\prime})\bigr]\end{multline}\frac{\udddc\sigma}{\udddc\vec{k}_{\textmd{$\mathit{{q}{}_{\mspace{-3mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{-1mu}\scriptstyle{}}}$ }}}=-2\alpha_{\text{s}}C_{F}\int\frac{\uddc\vec{}v_{\perp}}{(2\pi)^{2}}\frac{\uddc\vec{}v_{\perp}^{\prime}}{(2\pi)^{2}}\frac{\uddc\vec{}u_{\perp}}{(2\pi)^{2}}e^{-i\vec{k}_{\textmd{$\mathit{{q}{}_{\mspace{-3mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{-1mu}\scriptstyle{}}}$ }\perp}\cdot(\vec{}v_{\perp}-\vec{}v_{\perp}^{\prime})}\\
\times\sum_{\lambda\alpha\beta}\conj{(\psi_{\alpha\beta}^{\lambda})}(\vec{}u_{\perp})\psi_{\alpha\beta}^{\lambda}(\vec{}u_{\perp})\bigl[S_{x_{g}}^{(2)}(\vec{}v_{\perp},\vec{}v_{\perp}^{\prime})-S_{x_{g}}^{(3)}(\vec{}b_{\perp},\vec{}x_{\perp},\vec{}v_{\perp}^{\prime})\bigr]In this expression we only have one momentum, the quark momentum, because only the quark line passes through the cut, and this removes the need for the delta function because the initial quark momentum doesn't occur anywhere in the expression.
The integral over the loop momentum is already incorporated into the definition of the Wilson line.
And the factor of $2$ arises because the top two diagrams in the right column of figure~\ref {fig:nloqqcompounddiagrams} give the same contribution (specifically, the $S_{x_{g}}^{(3)}$ term), and likewise for the bottom two diagrams (the $S_{x_{g}}^{(2)}$ term).
\par Putting together both the real~\eqref{eq:realqqresult} and virtual~\eqref{eq:virtualqqresult} contributions gives the complete expression for the quark-quark channel.
\begin{multline}\frac{\udc^{3}\sigma}{\udddc\vec{k}_{\textmd{$\mathit{{q}{}_{\mspace{-3mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{-1mu}\scriptstyle{}}}$ }}}=\alpha_{\text{s}}C_{F}\int\frac{\uddc\vec{}u_{\perp}}{(2\pi)^{2}}\frac{\uddc\vec{}v_{\perp}}{(2\pi)^{2}}\frac{\uddc\vec{}v_{\perp}^{\prime}}{(2\pi)^{2}}e^{-i\vec{k}_{\textmd{$\mathit{{q}{}_{\mspace{-3mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{-1mu}\scriptstyle{}}}$ }\perp}\cdot(\vec{}b_{\perp}-\vec{}b_{\perp}^{\prime})}\sum_{\lambda\alpha\beta}\conj{(\psi_{\alpha\beta}^{\lambda})}(\vec{}u_{\perp}^{\prime})\psi_{\alpha\beta}^{\lambda}(\vec{}u_{\perp})\\
\times\bigl[S_{x_{g}}^{(2)}(\vec{}b_{\perp},\vec{}b_{\perp}^{\prime})-S_{x_{g}}^{(2)}(\vec{}v_{\perp},\vec{}v_{\perp}^{\prime})+S_{x_{g}}^{(3)}(\vec{}b_{\perp},\vec{}x_{\perp},\vec{}v_{\perp}^{\prime})-S_{x_{g}}^{(3)}(\vec{}v_{\perp},\vec{}x_{\perp},\vec{}b_{\perp}^{\prime})\bigr]\end{multline}\frac{\udc^{3}\sigma}{\udddc\vec{k}_{\textmd{$\mathit{{q}{}_{\mspace{-3mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{-1mu}\scriptstyle{}}}$ }}}=\alpha_{\text{s}}C_{F}\int\frac{\uddc\vec{}u_{\perp}}{(2\pi)^{2}}\frac{\uddc\vec{}v_{\perp}}{(2\pi)^{2}}\frac{\uddc\vec{}v_{\perp}^{\prime}}{(2\pi)^{2}}e^{-i\vec{k}_{\textmd{$\mathit{{q}{}_{\mspace{-3mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{-1mu}\scriptstyle{}}}$ }\perp}\cdot(\vec{}b_{\perp}-\vec{}b_{\perp}^{\prime})}\sum_{\lambda\alpha\beta}\conj{(\psi_{\alpha\beta}^{\lambda})}(\vec{}u_{\perp}^{\prime})\psi_{\alpha\beta}^{\lambda}(\vec{}u_{\perp})\\
\times\bigl[S_{x_{g}}^{(2)}(\vec{}b_{\perp},\vec{}b_{\perp}^{\prime})-S_{x_{g}}^{(2)}(\vec{}v_{\perp},\vec{}v_{\perp}^{\prime})+S_{x_{g}}^{(3)}(\vec{}b_{\perp},\vec{}x_{\perp},\vec{}v_{\perp}^{\prime})-S_{x_{g}}^{(3)}(\vec{}v_{\perp},\vec{}x_{\perp},\vec{}b_{\perp}^{\prime})\bigr]We can substitute in the definition of the splitting wavefunction,
\begin{equation}\psi_{\alpha\beta}^{\lambda}(x_{p}p_{\textmd{$\mathup{{{p}}}$ }}^{+},k_{\textmd{$\mathit{{q}{}_{\mspace{-3mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{-1mu}\scriptstyle{}}}$ }}^{+},\vec{}r_{\perp})=2\pi i\sqrt{\frac{2}{k_{\textmd{$\mathit{{q}{}_{\mspace{-3mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{-1mu}\scriptstyle{}}}$ }}^{+}}}\begin{cases}\frac{\vec{}r_{\perp}\cdot\epsilon_{\perp}^{(1)}}{r_{\perp}^{2}}(\delta_{\alpha-}\delta_{\beta-}+\xi\delta_{\alpha+}\delta_{\beta+}),&\lambda=1\\
\frac{\vec{}r_{\perp}\cdot\epsilon_{\perp}^{(2)}}{r_{\perp}^{2}}(\delta_{\alpha+}\delta_{\beta+}+\xi\delta_{\alpha-}\delta_{\beta-}),&\lambda=2\end{cases}\end{equation}and simplify the gluon distributions using the identity
\begin{equation}S_{x_{g}}^{(3)}(\vec{}x_{\perp},\vec{}b_{\perp},\vec{}y_{\perp})=\frac{N_{c}}{2C_{F}}\biggl[S_{x_{g}}^{(4)}(\vec{}x_{\perp},\vec{}b_{\perp},\vec{}y_{\perp})-\frac{1}{N_{c}^{2}}S_{x_{g}}^{(2)}(\vec{}x_{\perp},\vec{}y_{\perp})\biggr]\end{equation}to get
\begin{multline}\frac{\udc^{3}\sigma}{\udddc\vec{k}_{\textmd{$\mathit{{q}{}_{\mspace{-3mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{-1mu}\scriptstyle{}}}$ }}}=\alpha_{\text{s}}\int\frac{\uddc\vec{}u_{\perp}}{(2\pi)^{2}}\frac{\uddc\vec{}v_{\perp}}{(2\pi)^{2}}\frac{\uddc\vec{}v_{\perp}^{\prime}}{(2\pi)^{2}}e^{-i\vec{k}_{\textmd{$\mathit{{q}{}_{\mspace{-3mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{-1mu}\scriptstyle{}}}$ }\perp}\cdot(\vec{}b_{\perp}-\vec{}b_{\perp}^{\prime})}\sum_{\lambda\alpha\beta}\conj{(\psi_{\alpha\beta}^{\lambda})}(\vec{}u_{\perp}^{\prime})\psi_{\alpha\beta}^{\lambda}(\vec{}u_{\perp})\\
\times\Biggl(C_{F}S_{x_{g}}^{(2)}(\vec{}b_{\perp},\vec{}b_{\perp}^{\prime})-C_{F}S_{x_{g}}^{(2)}(\vec{}v_{\perp},\vec{}v_{\perp}^{\prime})-\frac{1}{2N_{c}}S_{x_{g}}^{(2)}(\vec{}b_{\perp},\vec{}v_{\perp}^{\prime})+\frac{1}{2N_{c}}S_{x_{g}}^{(2)}(\vec{}v_{\perp},\vec{}b_{\perp}^{\prime})\\
+\frac{N_{c}}{2}S_{x_{g}}^{(4)}(\vec{}b_{\perp},\vec{}x_{\perp},\vec{}v_{\perp}^{\prime})-\frac{N_{c}}{2}S_{x_{g}}^{(4)}(\vec{}v_{\perp},\vec{}x_{\perp},\vec{}b_{\perp}^{\prime})\Biggr)\end{multline}\frac{\udc^{3}\sigma}{\udddc\vec{k}_{\textmd{$\mathit{{q}{}_{\mspace{-3mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{-1mu}\scriptstyle{}}}$ }}}=\alpha_{\text{s}}\int\frac{\uddc\vec{}u_{\perp}}{(2\pi)^{2}}\frac{\uddc\vec{}v_{\perp}}{(2\pi)^{2}}\frac{\uddc\vec{}v_{\perp}^{\prime}}{(2\pi)^{2}}e^{-i\vec{k}_{\textmd{$\mathit{{q}{}_{\mspace{-3mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{-1mu}\scriptstyle{}}}$ }\perp}\cdot(\vec{}b_{\perp}-\vec{}b_{\perp}^{\prime})}\sum_{\lambda\alpha\beta}\conj{(\psi_{\alpha\beta}^{\lambda})}(\vec{}u_{\perp}^{\prime})\psi_{\alpha\beta}^{\lambda}(\vec{}u_{\perp})\\
\times\Biggl(C_{F}S_{x_{g}}^{(2)}(\vec{}b_{\perp},\vec{}b_{\perp}^{\prime})-C_{F}S_{x_{g}}^{(2)}(\vec{}v_{\perp},\vec{}v_{\perp}^{\prime})-\frac{1}{2N_{c}}S_{x_{g}}^{(2)}(\vec{}b_{\perp},\vec{}v_{\perp}^{\prime})+\frac{1}{2N_{c}}S_{x_{g}}^{(2)}(\vec{}v_{\perp},\vec{}b_{\perp}^{\prime})\\
+\frac{N_{c}}{2}S_{x_{g}}^{(4)}(\vec{}b_{\perp},\vec{}x_{\perp},\vec{}v_{\perp}^{\prime})-\frac{N_{c}}{2}S_{x_{g}}^{(4)}(\vec{}v_{\perp},\vec{}x_{\perp},\vec{}b_{\perp}^{\prime})\Biggr)which is in terms of only two gluon distributions, $S_{x_{g}}^{(2)}$ and $S_{x_{g}}^{(4)}$.
If we further use the mean field approximation~\eqref{eq:meanfieldappx}, that reduces to only $S_{x_{g}}^{(2)}$; we do this in the SOLO code, but in this document I'll keep $S_{x_{g}}^{(4)}$ explicit.
\par The last step in finding the cross section for $\mathup{{{p}}}\mathit{{A}{}_{\mspace{-3mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{-1mu}\scriptstyle{}}}\HepTo\mathit{{h}{}_{\mspace{-3mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{-1mu}\scriptstyle{}}}\mathit{{X}{}_{\mspace{-3mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{-1mu}\scriptstyle{}}}$ in the quark-quark channel is to convolve with the parton distributions of the proton and the fragmentation functions.
\begin{equation}\frac{\udddc\sigma}{\udddc\vec{p}}=\int_{\tau}^{1}\frac{\udc z}{z^{2}}\int_{\tau/z}^{1}\udc\xi x_{p}q(x_{p})D_{h/j}(z)\frac{\udddc\sigma}{\udddc\vec{k}_{\textmd{$\mathit{{q}{}_{\mspace{-3mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{-1mu}\scriptstyle{}}}$ }}}\end{equation}\par\par\par\lx@frontmatter@fallback\@@unnumbered@section{subsection}{toc}{Removal of Divergences}
\par In reference~\cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{Chirilli:2012jd}{}{}]}, the quark-quark channel cross section is given as the following two expressions:
\begin{multline}\frac{\alpha_{\text{s}}}{2\pi^{2}}\int_{\tau}^{1}\int_{\frac{\tau}{z}}^{1}\frac{\udc z\udc\xi}{z^{2}}x_{p}q(x_{p})D_{\mathit{{h}{}_{\mspace{-3mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{-1mu}\scriptstyle{}}}/q}(z)\frac{1+\xi^{2}}{1-\xi}\biggl[C_{F}\int\uddc\vec{q}_{\perp}\mathcal{I}(\vec{k}_{\textmd{$\mathit{{q}{}_{\mspace{-3mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{-1mu}\scriptstyle{}}}$ }\perp},\vec{q}_{\perp})\\
+\frac{N_{c}}{2}\int\uddc\vec{q}_{\perp}\uddc\vec{}k_{\mathup{{{g}}}1\perp}\mathcal{J}(\vec{k}_{\textmd{$\mathit{{q}{}_{\mspace{-3mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{-1mu}\scriptstyle{}}}$ }\perp},\vec{q}_{\perp},\vec{}k_{\mathup{{{g}}}1\perp})\biggr]\end{multline}\frac{\alpha_{\text{s}}}{2\pi^{2}}\int_{\tau}^{1}\int_{\frac{\tau}{z}}^{1}\frac{\udc z\udc\xi}{z^{2}}x_{p}q(x_{p})D_{\mathit{{h}{}_{\mspace{-3mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{-1mu}\scriptstyle{}}}/q}(z)\frac{1+\xi^{2}}{1-\xi}\biggl[C_{F}\int\uddc\vec{q}_{\perp}\mathcal{I}(\vec{k}_{\textmd{$\mathit{{q}{}_{\mspace{-3mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{-1mu}\scriptstyle{}}}$ }\perp},\vec{q}_{\perp})\\
+\frac{N_{c}}{2}\int\uddc\vec{q}_{\perp}\uddc\vec{}k_{\mathup{{{g}}}1\perp}\mathcal{J}(\vec{k}_{\textmd{$\mathit{{q}{}_{\mspace{-3mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{-1mu}\scriptstyle{}}}$ }\perp},\vec{q}_{\perp},\vec{}k_{\mathup{{{g}}}1\perp})\biggr]from the real diagrams and
\begin{multline}-\frac{\alpha_{\text{s}}}{2\pi^{2}}\int_{\tau}^{1}\int_{0}^{1}\frac{\udc z\udc\xi}{z^{2}}x_{p}q(x_{p})D_{\mathit{{h}{}_{\mspace{-3mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{-1mu}\scriptstyle{}}}/q}(z)\frac{1+\xi^{2}}{1-\xi}\biggl[C_{F}\int\uddc\vec{q}_{\perp}\mathcal{I}(\vec{q}_{\perp},\vec{k}_{\textmd{$\mathit{{q}{}_{\mspace{-3mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{-1mu}\scriptstyle{}}}$ }\perp})\\
+\frac{N_{c}}{2}\int\uddc\vec{q}_{\perp}\uddc\vec{}k_{\mathup{{{g}}}1\perp}\mathcal{J}(\vec{q}_{\perp},\vec{k}_{\textmd{$\mathit{{q}{}_{\mspace{-3mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{-1mu}\scriptstyle{}}}$ }\perp},\vec{}k_{\mathup{{{g}}}1\perp})\biggr]\end{multline}-\frac{\alpha_{\text{s}}}{2\pi^{2}}\int_{\tau}^{1}\int_{0}^{1}\frac{\udc z\udc\xi}{z^{2}}x_{p}q(x_{p})D_{\mathit{{h}{}_{\mspace{-3mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{-1mu}\scriptstyle{}}}/q}(z)\frac{1+\xi^{2}}{1-\xi}\biggl[C_{F}\int\uddc\vec{q}_{\perp}\mathcal{I}(\vec{q}_{\perp},\vec{k}_{\textmd{$\mathit{{q}{}_{\mspace{-3mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{-1mu}\scriptstyle{}}}$ }\perp})\\
+\frac{N_{c}}{2}\int\uddc\vec{q}_{\perp}\uddc\vec{}k_{\mathup{{{g}}}1\perp}\mathcal{J}(\vec{q}_{\perp},\vec{k}_{\textmd{$\mathit{{q}{}_{\mspace{-3mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{-1mu}\scriptstyle{}}}$ }\perp},\vec{}k_{\mathup{{{g}}}1\perp})\biggr]from the virtual diagrams, where
\@@amsalign
It's clear that these formulas are divergent in two ways: the {\it rapidity divergence}, where the integrand becomes undefined at $\xi\to 1$, and the {\it collinear divergence} when $\vec{k}_{\textmd{$\mathit{{q}{}_{\mspace{-3mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{-1mu}\scriptstyle{}}}$ }\perp}=\vec{q}_{\perp}$.
Regulating these divergences constitutes the largest obstacle in computing the full NLO corrections to the cross section.
\par Broadly speaking, here is how the process works. A simplified version of the quark-quark channel result looks like
\begin{multline}\biggl(\frac{\udddc\sigma}{\udc Y\uddc\vec{p}_{\perp}}\biggr)_{qq}=\int_{\tau}^{1}\frac{\udc z}{z^{2}}x_{p}q^{(0)}(x_{p})D_{\mathit{{h}{}_{\mspace{-3mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{-1mu}\scriptstyle{}}}/q}^{(0)}(z)\mathcal{F}_{x_{g}}^{(0)}(\vec{q}_{\perp})\\
+\frac{\alpha_{\text{s}}}{2\pi}\int_{\tau}^{1}\frac{\udc z}{z^{2}}\int_{0}^{1}\udc\xi\,x_{p}q^{(0)}(x_{p})D_{\mathit{{h}{}_{\mspace{-3mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{-1mu}\scriptstyle{}}}/q}^{(0)}(z)\frac{A\mathcal{F}_{x_{g}}^{(0)}(\vec{q}_{\perp})+B\mathcal{G}_{x_{g}}^{(0)}(\vec{q}_{\perp},\vec{k}_{\textmd{$\mathup{{{g}}}$ }\perp})}{1-\xi}\end{multline}\biggl(\frac{\udddc\sigma}{\udc Y\uddc\vec{p}_{\perp}}\biggr)_{qq}=\int_{\tau}^{1}\frac{\udc z}{z^{2}}x_{p}q^{(0)}(x_{p})D_{\mathit{{h}{}_{\mspace{-3mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{-1mu}\scriptstyle{}}}/q}^{(0)}(z)\mathcal{F}_{x_{g}}^{(0)}(\vec{q}_{\perp})\\
+\frac{\alpha_{\text{s}}}{2\pi}\int_{\tau}^{1}\frac{\udc z}{z^{2}}\int_{0}^{1}\udc\xi\,x_{p}q^{(0)}(x_{p})D_{\mathit{{h}{}_{\mspace{-3mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{-1mu}\scriptstyle{}}}/q}^{(0)}(z)\frac{A\mathcal{F}_{x_{g}}^{(0)}(\vec{q}_{\perp})+B\mathcal{G}_{x_{g}}^{(0)}(\vec{q}_{\perp},\vec{k}_{\textmd{$\mathup{{{g}}}$ }\perp})}{1-\xi}where $A$ and $B$ are nonsingular coefficients. The leading order term is finite on its own, but the next-to-leading order term is divergent at $\xi\to 1$. (In this simplified example, I'll omit the collinear divergence until later.)
\par We can rewrite this as
\begin{multline}\biggl(\frac{\udddc\sigma}{\udc Y\uddc\vec{p}_{\perp}}\biggr)_{qq}=\int_{\tau}^{1}\frac{\udc z}{z^{2}}x_{p}q^{(0)}(x_{p})D_{\mathit{{h}{}_{\mspace{-3mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{-1mu}\scriptstyle{}}}/q}^{(0)}(z)\\
\times\Biggl[\mathcal{F}_{x_{g}}^{(0)}(\vec{}k_{\perp})+\frac{\alpha_{\text{s}}}{2\pi}\int_{0}^{1}\udc\xi\frac{A\mathcal{F}_{x_{g}}^{(0)}(\vec{q}_{\perp})+B\mathcal{G}_{x_{g}}^{(0)}(\vec{q}_{\perp},\vec{k}_{\textmd{$\mathup{{{g}}}$ }\perp})}{1-\xi}\Biggr]\end{multline}\biggl(\frac{\udddc\sigma}{\udc Y\uddc\vec{p}_{\perp}}\biggr)_{qq}=\int_{\tau}^{1}\frac{\udc z}{z^{2}}x_{p}q^{(0)}(x_{p})D_{\mathit{{h}{}_{\mspace{-3mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{-1mu}\scriptstyle{}}}/q}^{(0)}(z)\\
\times\Biggl[\mathcal{F}_{x_{g}}^{(0)}(\vec{}k_{\perp})+\frac{\alpha_{\text{s}}}{2\pi}\int_{0}^{1}\udc\xi\frac{A\mathcal{F}_{x_{g}}^{(0)}(\vec{q}_{\perp})+B\mathcal{G}_{x_{g}}^{(0)}(\vec{q}_{\perp},\vec{k}_{\textmd{$\mathup{{{g}}}$ }\perp})}{1-\xi}\Biggr]$\mathcal{F}_{x_{g}}^{(0)}(\vec{q}_{\perp})$ is not actually a physical quantity, so there's no reason for it to be finite.
We can then assume that $\mathcal{F}_{x_{g}}^{(0)}(\vec{q}_{\perp})$ is divergent in a way that cancels out the rapidity divergence, so the total of the terms in square brackets is finite.
\par At this point we could define the renormalized gluon distribution $\mathcal{F}_{x_{g}}(\vec{q}_{\perp})$ to be exactly the term in brackets, so that the quark-quark cross section would just be
\begin{equation}\biggl(\frac{\udddc\sigma}{\udc Y\uddc\vec{p}_{\perp}}\biggr)_{qq}=\int_{\tau}^{1}\frac{\udc z}{z^{2}}x_{p}q^{(0)}(x_{p})D_{\mathit{{h}{}_{\mspace{-3mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{-1mu}\scriptstyle{}}}/q}^{(0)}(z)\mathcal{F}_{x_{g}}(\vec{q}_{\perp})\end{equation}but that would make $\mathcal{F}_{x_{g}}$ dependent on the exact coefficients $A$ and $B$.
If we do that, we can't generalize $\mathcal{F}_{x_{g}}$ to any other calculation.
Instead, we should define the renormalized distribution as the sum of $\mathcal{F}_{x_{g}}^{(0)}$ and some other divergent term that cancels out the singularity in the remaining part of the expression. That will leave us with
\begin{equation}\biggl(\frac{\udddc\sigma}{\udc Y\uddc\vec{p}_{\perp}}\biggr)_{qq}=\int_{\tau}^{1}\frac{\udc z}{z^{2}}x_{p}q^{(0)}(x_{p})D_{\mathit{{h}{}_{\mspace{-3mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{-1mu}\scriptstyle{}}}/q}^{(0)}(z)\bigl[\mathcal{F}_{x_{g}}(\vec{q}_{\perp})+C(z,\xi,\vec{q}_{\perp})\bigr]\end{equation}$C$ is called the {\it coefficient function}.
The procedure for how we choose to split the term in brackets between $\mathcal{F}_{x_{g}}$ and $C$ is a {\it renormalization scheme}.\par When there are multiple divergences, namely the rapidity divergence and collinear divergence, we can generalize the procedure to incorporate these divergences into the parton distribution and fragmentation function as well.
In doing so, it's worth considering the physical nature of the divergences~\cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{Chirilli:2012jd}{}{}]}:
\begin{itemize}\par\itemize@item@The rapidity divergence occurs when the quark momentum fraction $\xi$ goes to $1$, so that the emitted gluon's momentum becomes negligible in the rest frame of the target.
These extremely low-momentum gluon emissions are physically indistinguishable from the target's own gluon field.
When we renormalize the rapidity divergence using the target gluon distribution, we are effectively absorbing the low-momentum gluon emissions into the structure of the target.
\par\itemize@item@The collinear divergence, on the other hand, occurs when the quark and the emitted gluon are traveling in the same direction with nearly equal momenta.
However, we know that a propagating quark is dressed with a cloud of virtual gluons (and other particles).
An emitted gluon with nearly the same momentum as the quark will effectively be part of that cloud, so it makes sense to absorb the collinear divergence into the function that represents the nonperturbative dynamics of the propagating physical quark.
This is either the fragmentation function, if the gluon is emitted after the interaction, or the integrated parton distribution, if it's emitted before the interaction.
\par\end{itemize}\par With this in mind, let's look at a slightly less simplified version of the quark-quark channel cross section, just like~\eqref{eq:simplifiedqq} except that this contains the collinear divergence as well,
\begin{multline}\biggl(\frac{\udddc\sigma}{\udc Y\uddc\vec{p}_{\perp}}\biggr)_{qq}=\int_{\tau}^{1}\frac{\udc z}{z^{2}}x_{p}q^{(0)}(x_{p})D_{\mathit{{h}{}_{\mspace{-3mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{-1mu}\scriptstyle{}}}/q}^{(0)}(z)\mathcal{F}_{x_{g}}^{(0)}(\vec{q}_{\perp})\\
+\frac{\alpha_{\text{s}}}{2\pi}\int_{\tau}^{1}\frac{\udc z}{z^{2}}\int_{\tau/z}^{1}\udc\xi\,x_{p}q^{(0)}(x_{p})D_{\mathit{{h}{}_{\mspace{-3mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{-1mu}\scriptstyle{}}}/q}^{(0)}(z)\frac{A\mathcal{F}_{x_{g}}^{(0)}(\vec{q}_{\perp})+B\mathcal{G}_{x_{g}}^{(0)}(\vec{q}_{\perp},\vec{k}_{\textmd{$\mathup{{{g}}}$ }\perp})}{(1-\xi)(\vec{q}_{\perp}-\vec{k}_{\textmd{$\mathup{{{g}}}$ }\perp})^{2}}\end{multline}\biggl(\frac{\udddc\sigma}{\udc Y\uddc\vec{p}_{\perp}}\biggr)_{qq}=\int_{\tau}^{1}\frac{\udc z}{z^{2}}x_{p}q^{(0)}(x_{p})D_{\mathit{{h}{}_{\mspace{-3mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{-1mu}\scriptstyle{}}}/q}^{(0)}(z)\mathcal{F}_{x_{g}}^{(0)}(\vec{q}_{\perp})\\
+\frac{\alpha_{\text{s}}}{2\pi}\int_{\tau}^{1}\frac{\udc z}{z^{2}}\int_{\tau/z}^{1}\udc\xi\,x_{p}q^{(0)}(x_{p})D_{\mathit{{h}{}_{\mspace{-3mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{-1mu}\scriptstyle{}}}/q}^{(0)}(z)\frac{A\mathcal{F}_{x_{g}}^{(0)}(\vec{q}_{\perp})+B\mathcal{G}_{x_{g}}^{(0)}(\vec{q}_{\perp},\vec{k}_{\textmd{$\mathup{{{g}}}$ }\perp})}{(1-\xi)(\vec{q}_{\perp}-\vec{k}_{\textmd{$\mathup{{{g}}}$ }\perp})^{2}}With all three of the functions $x_{p}q^{(0)}(x_{p})$, $D_{\mathit{{h}{}_{\mspace{-3mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{-1mu}\scriptstyle{}}}/q}^{(0)}(z)$, and $\mathcal{F}_{x_{g}}^{(0)}(\vec{q}_{\perp})$ playing a role in removing the divergences, we should wind up with an expression of the form
\begin{multline}\biggl(\frac{\udddc\sigma}{\udc Y\uddc\vec{p}_{\perp}}\biggr)_{qq}=\int_{\tau}^{1}\frac{\udc z}{z^{2}}\Biggl[x_{p}q^{(0)}(x_{p})-\frac{\alpha_{\text{s}}}{2\pi}\int_{x_{p}}^{1}\udc\xi^{\prime}\frac{C^{\prime}x_{p}q(x_{p}/\xi^{\prime})}{(\vec{q}_{\perp}-\vec{k}_{\textmd{$\mathup{{{g}}}$ }\perp})^{2}}\Biggr]\\
\times\Biggl[D_{\mathit{{h}{}_{\mspace{-3mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{-1mu}\scriptstyle{}}}/q}^{(0)}(z)-\frac{\alpha_{\text{s}}}{2\pi}\int_{z}^{1}\udc\xi^{\prime\prime}\frac{C^{\prime\prime}D_{\mathit{{h}{}_{\mspace{-3mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{-1mu}\scriptstyle{}}}/q}(z/\xi^{\prime\prime})}{(\vec{q}_{\perp}-\vec{k}_{\textmd{$\mathup{{{g}}}$ }\perp})^{2}}\Biggr]\\
\times\Biggl[\mathcal{F}_{x_{g}}^{(0)}(\vec{}k_{\perp})+\frac{\alpha_{\text{s}}}{2\pi}\int_{0}^{1}\udc\xi\frac{A\mathcal{F}_{x_{g}}^{(0)}(\vec{}k_{\perp})+B\mathcal{G}_{x_{g}}^{(0)}(\vec{}k_{\perp},\vec{q}_{\perp})}{1-\xi}\Biggr]\end{multline}\biggl(\frac{\udddc\sigma}{\udc Y\uddc\vec{p}_{\perp}}\biggr)_{qq}=\int_{\tau}^{1}\frac{\udc z}{z^{2}}\Biggl[x_{p}q^{(0)}(x_{p})-\frac{\alpha_{\text{s}}}{2\pi}\int_{x_{p}}^{1}\udc\xi^{\prime}\frac{C^{\prime}x_{p}q(x_{p}/\xi^{\prime})}{(\vec{q}_{\perp}-\vec{k}_{\textmd{$\mathup{{{g}}}$ }\perp})^{2}}\Biggr]\\
\times\Biggl[D_{\mathit{{h}{}_{\mspace{-3mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{-1mu}\scriptstyle{}}}/q}^{(0)}(z)-\frac{\alpha_{\text{s}}}{2\pi}\int_{z}^{1}\udc\xi^{\prime\prime}\frac{C^{\prime\prime}D_{\mathit{{h}{}_{\mspace{-3mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{-1mu}\scriptstyle{}}}/q}(z/\xi^{\prime\prime})}{(\vec{q}_{\perp}-\vec{k}_{\textmd{$\mathup{{{g}}}$ }\perp})^{2}}\Biggr]\\
\times\Biggl[\mathcal{F}_{x_{g}}^{(0)}(\vec{}k_{\perp})+\frac{\alpha_{\text{s}}}{2\pi}\int_{0}^{1}\udc\xi\frac{A\mathcal{F}_{x_{g}}^{(0)}(\vec{}k_{\perp})+B\mathcal{G}_{x_{g}}^{(0)}(\vec{}k_{\perp},\vec{q}_{\perp})}{1-\xi}\Biggr]where $C^{\prime}$ and $C^{\prime\prime}$ are nonsingular coefficients (like $A$ and $B$).
For simplicity, let me express this as
\begin{equation}\biggl(\frac{\udddc\sigma}{\udc Y\uddc\vec{p}_{\perp}}\biggr)_{qq}=\int_{\tau}^{1}\frac{\udc z}{z^{2}}(q_{0}-q_{1})(D_{0}-D_{1})(F_{0}+F_{1})\end{equation}where $q_{0}$ and $q_{1}$ stand for the bare parton distribution and the subtraction, respectively, and similarly for $D$ and $F$.
Distributing we find
\begin{multline}\biggl(\frac{\udddc\sigma}{\udc Y\uddc\vec{p}_{\perp}}\biggr)_{qq}=\int_{\tau}^{1}\frac{\udc z}{z^{2}}\bigl[q_{0}D_{0}F_{0}+q_{0}D_{0}F_{1}-q_{1}D_{0}F_{0}-q_{1}D_{0}F_{1}\\
-q_{0}D_{1}F_{0}-q_{0}D_{1}F_{1}+q_{1}D_{1}F_{0}+q_{1}D_{1}F_{1}\bigr]\end{multline}\biggl(\frac{\udddc\sigma}{\udc Y\uddc\vec{p}_{\perp}}\biggr)_{qq}=\int_{\tau}^{1}\frac{\udc z}{z^{2}}\bigl[q_{0}D_{0}F_{0}+q_{0}D_{0}F_{1}-q_{1}D_{0}F_{0}-q_{1}D_{0}F_{1}\\
-q_{0}D_{1}F_{0}-q_{0}D_{1}F_{1}+q_{1}D_{1}F_{0}+q_{1}D_{1}F_{1}\bigr]Note that each term is at an order in $\alpha_{\text{s}}$ corresponding to the sum of its subscripts (e.g. the last term is $\orderof{\alpha_{\text{s}}^{3}}$).
\par Now, if we go back to~\eqref{eq:simplifiedqqtwodiv}, which is the bare result,
\@@amsalign
then we reproduce the distributed result~\eqref{eq:distributed} up to terms which are finite and subleading in $\alpha_{\text{s}}$ (specifically $\orderof{\alpha_{\text{s}}^{2}}$).
This is the basic procedure by which subtracting divergences gives a finite result.
\par Performing the full subtractions for the real and virtual contributions involves a fairly tedious calculation which is the subject of reference~\cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{Chirilli:2012jd}{}{}]}.
That work uses the $\overline{\text{MS}}$ renormalization scheme, which entails regularizing integrals by dimensional regularization and bringing a term proportional to $-\frac{1}{\epsilon}+\gamma_{E}$ into the gluon distribution; the details of $\overline{\text{MS}}$ renormalization are described in many standard references.
The result works out to
\begin{multline}\biggl(\frac{\udddc\sigma}{\udc Y\uddc\vec{p}_{\perp}}\biggr)_{nqq}^{(1)}=\frac{\alpha_{\text{s}}}{2\pi}\int_{\tau}^{1}\int_{\frac{\tau}{z}}^{1}\frac{\udc z\udc\xi}{z^{2}}x_{p}q(x_{p})D_{\mathit{{h}{}_{\mspace{-3mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{-1mu}\scriptstyle{}}}/q}(z)\biggl[\int\frac{\uddc\vec{}x_{\perp}\uddc\vec{}y_{\perp}}{(2\pi)^{2}}S_{x_{g}}^{(2)}(\vec{}r_{\perp})\mathcal{H}^{(1)}_{2qq}\\
+\int\frac{\uddc\vec{}x_{\perp}\uddc\vec{}b_{\perp}\uddc\vec{}y_{\perp}}{(2\pi)^{4}}S_{x_{g}}^{(4)}(\vec{}s_{\perp},\vec{}t_{\perp})\mathcal{H}^{(1)}_{4qq}\biggr]\end{multline}\biggl(\frac{\udddc\sigma}{\udc Y\uddc\vec{p}_{\perp}}\biggr)_{nqq}^{(1)}=\frac{\alpha_{\text{s}}}{2\pi}\int_{\tau}^{1}\int_{\frac{\tau}{z}}^{1}\frac{\udc z\udc\xi}{z^{2}}x_{p}q(x_{p})D_{\mathit{{h}{}_{\mspace{-3mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{-1mu}\scriptstyle{}}}/q}(z)\biggl[\int\frac{\uddc\vec{}x_{\perp}\uddc\vec{}y_{\perp}}{(2\pi)^{2}}S_{x_{g}}^{(2)}(\vec{}r_{\perp})\mathcal{H}^{(1)}_{2qq}\\
+\int\frac{\uddc\vec{}x_{\perp}\uddc\vec{}b_{\perp}\uddc\vec{}y_{\perp}}{(2\pi)^{4}}S_{x_{g}}^{(4)}(\vec{}s_{\perp},\vec{}t_{\perp})\mathcal{H}^{(1)}_{4qq}\biggr]where the hard factors $\mathcal{H}^{(1)}_{2qq}$ and $\mathcal{H}^{(1)}_{4qq}$ are given in equations~\eqref{eq:xsec12qq} and~\eqref{eq:xsec14qq}.
\par\par\lx@frontmatter@fallback\@@unnumbered@section{section}{toc}{Adapting Formulas for Numerical Implementation}
\par As they are given in reference~\cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{Chirilli:2012jd}{}{}]}, the formulas are integrals over various sets of position coordinates.
A computational implementation needs code to evaluate the integrands and also to perform the integration.
The integrands involve various singular terms proportional to $\delta(1-\xi)$ and the plus prescription $\frac{1}{(1-\xi)_{+}}$, which need to be rewritten in terms of well-behaved (nonsingular) functions in order to be numerically evaluated.
There are also Fourier factors like $e^{-i\vec{}k_{\perp}\cdot\vec{}r_{\perp}}$ in the integrals, which are numerically difficult to evaluate, so in the interest of accuracy it will be useful to integrate out those factors symbolically where possible.
This section describes the details of that process.
\par\par\lx@frontmatter@fallback\@@unnumbered@section{subsection}{toc}{Eliminating Singularities in \texorpdfstring{$\xsecquarkpfrac$}{xi}}
Each term in the cross section can be written as a sum of three parts: one proportional to a plus prescription-regularized fraction, one proportional to a delta function, and one ``normal'' contribution which has no singularities that will prevent its numerical evaluation.
\begin{equation}\int_{\tau}^{1}\udc z\int_{\frac{\tau}{z}}^{1}\udc\xi\biggl[\frac{F_{s}(z,\xi)}{(1-\xi)_{+}}+F_{d}(z,\xi)\delta(1-\xi)+F_{n}(z,\xi)\biggr]\end{equation}\par To make the $F_{s}$ term numerically tractable, we need to simplify it using the definition of the plus prescription,
\begin{equation}f(x)_{+}\udc x\coloneqq f(x)\udc x-\delta(1-x)\udc x\int_{0}^{1}f(y)\udc y\end{equation}When this is integrated from some $a\in[0,1]$ to $1$, that integral can be simplified as follows:
\@@amsalign
Applying that to the $F_{s}$ term from~\eqref{eq:xsectermbreakdown}, we get
\begin{equation}\int_{\tau}^{1}\udc z\int_{\frac{\tau}{z}}^{1}\udc\xi\frac{F_{s}(z,\xi)}{(1-\xi)_{+}}=\int_{\tau}^{1}\udc z\int_{\frac{\tau}{z}}^{1}\udc\xi\,\frac{F_{s}(z,\xi)-F_{s}(z,1)}{1-\xi}+\int_{\tau}^{1}\udc z\,F_{s}(z,1)\ln\biggl(1-\frac{\tau}{z}\biggr)\end{equation}\par The $F_{d}$ term is easier, as it only requires us to do the integration over $\xi$:
\begin{equation}\int_{\tau}^{1}\udc z\int_{\frac{\tau}{z}}^{1}\udc\xi F_{d}(z,\xi)\delta(1-\xi)=\int_{\tau}^{1}\udc zF_{d}(z,1)\end{equation}\par And of course, the $F_{n}$ term of~\eqref{eq:xsectermbreakdown} requires no symbolic integration.
\par Putting the three terms together, we get the following expression, which is equivalent to~\eqref{eq:xsectermbreakdown} but is suitable for numerical evaluation:
\begin{equation}\int_{\tau}^{1}\udc z\int_{\frac{\tau}{z}}^{1}\udc\xi\biggl[\frac{F_{s}(z,\xi)-F_{s}(z,1)}{1-\xi}+F_{n}(z,\xi)\biggr]+\int_{\tau}^{1}\udc z\biggl[F_{s}(z,1)\ln\biggl(1-\frac{\tau}{z}\biggr)+F_{d}(z,1)\biggr]\end{equation}It is easy to split each hard factor up into the parts $F_{s}$, $F_{n}$, and $F_{d}$ and then the integration can be done automatically with an implementation of this formula.
\par\par\lx@frontmatter@fallback\@@unnumbered@section{subsection}{toc}{Reducing Additional Degrees of Freedom}
In general, each term of the cross section depends on several other variables beyond $z$ and $\xi$. Which variables those are varies from one term to another, though, and even depends on how a given term is expressed.
\par Broadly speaking there are three different ways that a term might be expressed:
\begin{description}\par\description@item@[Cartesian position space]the term will be expressed as an integral over $z$, $\xi$, and transverse displacements
\@@amsalign
This is closest to the original form given in reference~\cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{Chirilli:2012jd}{}{}]}, but is not particularly useful for computation because it includes Fourier factors like $e^{-i\vec{}k_{\perp}\cdot\vec{}r_{\perp}}$, which are numerically unstable.
\par\description@item@[Radial position space] the term will be expressed as an integral over $z$, $\xi$, and the radial part of transverse positions $r_{\perp}$, $s_{\perp}$, and $t_{\perp}$. In order to express the term in this way, we'll assume angular independence of the gluon distribution $S_{x_{g}}^{(2)}$.
\par\description@item@[Momentum space] the term will be expressed as an integral over $z$, $\xi$, and some number of momentum variables $k_{\mathup{{{g}}}\perp}$, $k_{\mathup{{{g}}}1\perp}$, etc. This is the most accurate form for numerical computation, but it requires us to do some symbolic Fourier integrals, which are not possible for every term. Also, the details of how to convert a given term into momentum space vary significantly from one term to another, even when it is possible.
\par\end{description}\par\par\lx@frontmatter@fallback\@@unnumbered@section{subsubsection}{toc}{Dipole-Type Terms}
Dipole-type terms involve integrals over only two transverse position coordinates, $\vec{}x_{\perp}$ and $\vec{}y_{\perp}$. A generic term of this type can be written as
\begin{multline}\biggl(\frac{\udddc\sigma}{\udc Y\uddc\vec{p}_{\perp}}\biggr)_{2ji}^{(n)}=\biggl(\frac{\alpha_{s}}{2\pi}\biggr)^{n}\int\frac{\udc z\udc\xi}{z^{2}}x_{p}f_{i}(x_{p},\mu)D_{\mathit{{h}{}_{\mspace{-3mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{-1mu}\scriptstyle{}}}/j}(z,\mu)\\
\times\int\frac{\uddc\vec{}x_{\perp}\uddc\vec{}y_{\perp}}{(2\pi)^{2}}\mathcal{S}(\vec{}x_{\perp},\vec{}y_{\perp})e^{-iC_{0}\vec{}k_{\perp}\cdot(\vec{}x_{\perp}-\vec{}y_{\perp})}\hat{}\mathcal{H}^{(n)}_{2ji}(\vec{}x_{\perp},\vec{}y_{\perp})\end{multline}\biggl(\frac{\udddc\sigma}{\udc Y\uddc\vec{p}_{\perp}}\biggr)_{2ji}^{(n)}=\biggl(\frac{\alpha_{s}}{2\pi}\biggr)^{n}\int\frac{\udc z\udc\xi}{z^{2}}x_{p}f_{i}(x_{p},\mu)D_{\mathit{{h}{}_{\mspace{-3mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{-1mu}\scriptstyle{}}}/j}(z,\mu)\\
\times\int\frac{\uddc\vec{}x_{\perp}\uddc\vec{}y_{\perp}}{(2\pi)^{2}}\mathcal{S}(\vec{}x_{\perp},\vec{}y_{\perp})e^{-iC_{0}\vec{}k_{\perp}\cdot(\vec{}x_{\perp}-\vec{}y_{\perp})}\hat{}\mathcal{H}^{(n)}_{2ji}(\vec{}x_{\perp},\vec{}y_{\perp})where $n=0$ for leading order or $1$ for next-to-leading order, and $i$ and $j$ represent parton flavors (gluon, up, down, strange, and heavy quarks). $C_{0}$ is some factor independent of the position and momentum coordinates, and $\mathcal{S}$ represents one or more factors of the dipole gluon distribution $S_{x_{g}}^{(2)}$. The hard factor $\mathcal{H}^{(n)}_{2ji}$ from reference~\cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{Chirilli:2012jd}{}{}]} corresponds to the combination
\begin{equation}\mathcal{H}^{(n)}_{2ji}=e^{-iC_{0}\vec{}k_{\perp}\cdot(\vec{}x_{\perp}-\vec{}y_{\perp})}\hat{}\mathcal{H}^{(n)}_{2ji}\end{equation}\par To manipulate these terms, we first simplify the expression by changing coordinates to
\@@amsalign
In this work, we assume that $S_{x_{g}}^{(2)}$, and thus $\mathcal{S}$, is independent of $\vec{R}_{\perp}$ and the angle of $\vec{}r_{\perp}$, so we can write $\mathcal{S}(\vec{}x_{\perp},\vec{}y_{\perp})$ as $\mathcal{S}(r_{\perp})$. It's also easy to verify, by inspection of the expressions in ref.~\cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{Chirilli:2012jd}{}{}]}, that all the hard factors $\mathcal{H}^{(n)}_{2ji}$ are independent of $\vec{R}_{\perp}$. Accordingly, we can change variables
\begin{equation}\uddc\vec{}x_{\perp}\uddc\vec{}y_{\perp}=\frac{1}{4}\uddc\vec{R}_{\perp}\uddc\vec{}r_{\perp}\end{equation}and integrate out the $\vec{R}_{\perp}$ dependence, defining
\begin{equation}S_{\perp}\coloneqq\int\frac{\uddc\vec{R}_{\perp}}{4}\end{equation}$S_{\perp}$ can be interpreted as the cross-sectional area of the target. After this integration, the term~\eqref{eq:unintegrateddipolecontribution} becomes
\begin{equation}\biggl(\frac{\udddc\sigma}{\udc Y\uddc\vec{p}_{\perp}}\biggr)_{2ji}^{(n)}=S_{\perp}\biggl(\frac{\alpha_{\text{s}}}{2\pi}\biggr)^{n}\int\frac{\udc z\udc\xi}{z^{2}}x_{p}f_{i}(x_{p},\mu)D_{\mathit{{h}{}_{\mspace{-3mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{-1mu}\scriptstyle{}}}/j}(z,\mu)\int\frac{\uddc\vec{}r_{\perp}}{(2\pi)^{2}}\mathcal{S}(r_{\perp})e^{-iC_{0}\vec{}k_{\perp}\cdot\vec{}r_{\perp}}\hat{}\mathcal{H}^{(n)}_{2ji}(\vec{}r_{\perp})\end{equation}\par Recall from section~\ref {sec:xisingularities} that a term can be expressed as a combination of a plus-regularized contribution, a delta-function contribution, and a normal contribution, as in equation~\eqref{eq:xsectermbreakdown}. We can obtain the three functions $F^{(n)}_{s2ji}$, $F^{(n)}_{d2ji}$, and $F^{(n)}_{n2ji}$ for each term by breaking down the term into these three contributions and pulling out all the integrals --- not only those over $z$ and $\xi$, but also over position and momentum variables. Different terms (and different representations of those terms) will be integrated over different variables, so the third and additional arguments of $F^{(n)}_{s2ji}$ etc. will be used to indicate what is being integrated over. When the third argument is a 2D position vector, that indicates the Cartesian representation; when it is the magnitude of a 2D position vector, that indicates the radial representation; when it is a 2D momentum vector, that indicates the momentum-space representation.
\par\par\lx@frontmatter@fallback\@@unnumbered@section{paragraph}{toc}{Cartesian position space}
It's straightforward to break down equation~\eqref{eq:integrateddipolecontribution} into the three functions $F_{s}$, $F_{n}$, and $F_{d}$ from section~\ref {sec:xisingularities} as follows:
\lx@equationgroup@subnumbering@begin
\@@amsalign
\lx@equationgroup@subnumbering@end
The entire term~\eqref{eq:integrateddipolecontribution} can then be written as
\begin{equation}\biggl(\frac{\udddc\sigma}{\udc Y\uddc\vec{p}_{\perp}}\biggr)_{2ji}^{(n)}=\int\udc z\udc\xi\int\uddc\vec{}r_{\perp}\biggl[\frac{F^{(n)}_{s2ji}(z,\xi,\vec{}r_{\perp})}{(1-\xi)_{+}}+F^{(n)}_{n2ji}(z,\xi,\vec{}r_{\perp})+F^{(n)}_{d2ji}(z,\xi,\vec{}r_{\perp})\delta(1-\xi)\biggr]\end{equation}or, using equation~\eqref{eq:contributions}, as
\begin{multline}\biggl(\frac{\udddc\sigma}{\udc Y\uddc\vec{p}_{\perp}}\biggr)_{2ji}^{(n)}=\int_{\tau}^{1}\udc z\int_{\tau/z}^{1}\udc\xi\int\uddc\vec{}r_{\perp}\biggl[\frac{F^{(n)}_{s2ji}(z,\xi,\vec{}r_{\perp})-F^{(n)}_{s2ji}(z,1,\vec{}r_{\perp})}{1-\xi}+F^{(n)}_{n2ji}(z,\xi,\vec{}r_{\perp})\biggr]\\
+\int_{\tau}^{1}\udc z\int\uddc\vec{}r_{\perp}\biggl[F^{(n)}_{s2ji}(z,1,\vec{}r_{\perp})\ln\biggl(1-\frac{\tau}{z}\biggr)+F^{(n)}_{d2ji}(z,1,\vec{}r_{\perp})\biggr]\end{multline}\biggl(\frac{\udddc\sigma}{\udc Y\uddc\vec{p}_{\perp}}\biggr)_{2ji}^{(n)}=\int_{\tau}^{1}\udc z\int_{\tau/z}^{1}\udc\xi\int\uddc\vec{}r_{\perp}\biggl[\frac{F^{(n)}_{s2ji}(z,\xi,\vec{}r_{\perp})-F^{(n)}_{s2ji}(z,1,\vec{}r_{\perp})}{1-\xi}+F^{(n)}_{n2ji}(z,\xi,\vec{}r_{\perp})\biggr]\\
+\int_{\tau}^{1}\udc z\int\uddc\vec{}r_{\perp}\biggl[F^{(n)}_{s2ji}(z,1,\vec{}r_{\perp})\ln\biggl(1-\frac{\tau}{z}\biggr)+F^{(n)}_{d2ji}(z,1,\vec{}r_{\perp})\biggr]This is the integral actually performed by SOLO when this term is evaluated in position space. The functions $F^{(n)}_{s2ji}$, $F^{(n)}_{n2ji}$, and $F^{(n)}_{d2ji}$ can be picked out from the corresponding expression for $\mathcal{H}^{(n)}_{2ji}$ in reference~\cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{Chirilli:2012jd}{}{}]} using~\eqref{eq:masterformula2}, transcribed directly into the program, and then equation~\eqref{eq:dipolemasterformula} is implemented by a generic integration routine.
\par\par\lx@frontmatter@fallback\@@unnumbered@section{paragraph}{toc}{Radial position space}
It turns out that the non-Fourier part of each dipole-type term, $\hat{}\mathcal{H}^{(n)}_{2ji}$, is independent of the angle of $\vec{}r_{\perp}$.
This means we can integrate over the angle in equation~\eqref{eq:integrateddipolecontribution},
\begin{equation}\iint\frac{\uddc\vec{}r_{\perp}}{(2\pi)^{2}}\mathcal{S}(r_{\perp})e^{-iC_{0}\vec{}k_{\perp}\cdot\vec{}r_{\perp}}\hat{}\mathcal{H}^{(n)}_{2ji}(r_{\perp})=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{0}^{\infty}\udc r_{\perp}r_{\perp}\mathcal{S}(r_{\perp})J_{0}(C_{0}k_{\perp}r_{\perp})\hat{}\mathcal{H}^{(n)}_{2ji}(r_{\perp})\end{equation}and again, pick out the pieces of the term as
\lx@equationgroup@subnumbering@begin
\@@amsalign
\lx@equationgroup@subnumbering@end
The entire term, analogous to equation~\eqref{eq:dipolemasterformula}, will be
\begin{multline}\biggl(\frac{\udddc\sigma}{\udc Y\uddc p_{\perp}}\biggr)_{\text{dip}}=\int_{\tau}^{1}\udc z\int_{\tau/z}^{1}\udc\xi\int_{0}^{\infty}\udc r_{\perp}\biggl[\frac{F^{(n)}_{s2ji}(z,\xi,r_{\perp})-F^{(n)}_{s2ji}(z,1,r_{\perp})}{1-\xi}+F^{(n)}_{n2ji}(z,\xi,r_{\perp})\biggr]\\
+\int_{\tau}^{1}\udc z\int_{0}^{\infty}\udc r_{\perp}\biggl[F^{(n)}_{s2ji}(z,1,r_{\perp})\ln\biggl(1-\frac{\tau}{z}\biggr)+F^{(n)}_{d2ji}(z,1,r_{\perp})\biggr]\end{multline}\biggl(\frac{\udddc\sigma}{\udc Y\uddc p_{\perp}}\biggr)_{\text{dip}}=\int_{\tau}^{1}\udc z\int_{\tau/z}^{1}\udc\xi\int_{0}^{\infty}\udc r_{\perp}\biggl[\frac{F^{(n)}_{s2ji}(z,\xi,r_{\perp})-F^{(n)}_{s2ji}(z,1,r_{\perp})}{1-\xi}+F^{(n)}_{n2ji}(z,\xi,r_{\perp})\biggr]\\
+\int_{\tau}^{1}\udc z\int_{0}^{\infty}\udc r_{\perp}\biggl[F^{(n)}_{s2ji}(z,1,r_{\perp})\ln\biggl(1-\frac{\tau}{z}\biggr)+F^{(n)}_{d2ji}(z,1,r_{\perp})\biggr]\par Essentially, the change from Cartesian to radial coordinates, equation~\eqref{eq:radialdipolehardfactorintegral}, amounts to making the replacement
\begin{equation}e^{-iC_{0}\vec{}k_{\perp}\cdot\vec{}r_{\perp}}\to 2\pi r_{\perp}J_{0}(C_{0}k_{\perp}r_{\perp})\end{equation}and adjusting the variable of integration accordingly. In practice, this is the easiest way to determine the radial formulas: start with the Cartesian position-space expression from~\cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{Chirilli:2012jd}{}{}]}, replace the Fourier factors with Bessel functions \`{a} la~\eqref{eq:maptoradial}, and then break the formula down into $F^{(n)}_{s2ji}$, $F^{(n)}_{n2ji}$, and $F^{(n)}_{d2ji}$.
\par This formulation improves numerical accuracy compared to the Cartesian implementation, by virtue of requiring one fewer oscillatory integral.
\par\par\lx@frontmatter@fallback\@@unnumbered@section{paragraph}{toc}{Momentum space}
In some cases, we can express a contribution to the cross section in terms of the momentum space gluon distribution as defined in equation~\eqref{eq:dipoleFdefinition}. The mathematical procedure for doing this is fairly specific to each individual term, but it will work out to some convolution of $\mathcal{F}_{x_{g}}$ and the Fourier transform of the rest of the term, $\hat{}\mathcal{H}^{(n)}_{2ji}$:
\begin{equation}\tilde{}\mathcal{H}^{(n)}_{2ji}(k_{\perp})=\iint\frac{\uddc\vec{}r_{\perp}}{(2\pi)^{2}}e^{i\vec{}k_{\perp}\cdot\vec{}r_{\perp}}\hat{}\mathcal{H}^{(n)}_{2ji}(\vec{}r_{\perp})\end{equation}\par For example, suppose $\mathcal{S}=S_{x_{g}}^{(2)}$ in equation~\eqref{eq:radialdipolehardfactorintegral}. We can express $S_{x_{g}}^{(2)}$ as
\begin{equation}S_{x_{g}}^{(2)}(\vec{}r_{\perp})=\frac{1}{S_{\perp}}\iint\uddc\vec{}q_{\perp}e^{i\vec{}q_{\perp}\cdot\vec{}r_{\perp}}\mathcal{F}_{x_{g}}(\vec{}q_{\perp})\end{equation}and plug it in to the position integral of equation~\eqref{eq:integrateddipolecontribution} to get
\begin{equation}\iint\frac{\uddc\vec{}r_{\perp}}{(2\pi)^{2}}S_{x_{g}}^{(2)}(\vec{}r_{\perp})e^{-iC_{0}\vec{}k_{\perp}\cdot\vec{}r_{\perp}}\hat{}\mathcal{H}^{(n)}_{2ji}(r_{\perp})=\frac{1}{S_{\perp}}\iint\uddc\vec{}q_{\perp}\mathcal{F}_{x_{g}}(\vec{}q_{\perp})\tilde{}\mathcal{H}^{(n)}_{2ji}(\vec{}q_{\perp}-C_{0}\vec{}k_{\perp})\end{equation}Or if $\mathcal{S}=\bigl[S_{x_{g}}^{(2)}\bigl]^{2}$, we get
\begin{multline}\iint\frac{\uddc\vec{}r_{\perp}}{(2\pi)^{2}}\bigl[S_{x_{g}}^{(2)}(\vec{}r_{\perp})\bigr]^{2}e^{-iC_{0}\vec{}k_{\perp}\cdot\vec{}r_{\perp}}\hat{}\mathcal{H}^{(n)}_{2ji}(r_{\perp})\\
=\frac{1}{S_{\perp}^{2}}\iint\uddc\vec{}q_{1\perp}\mathcal{F}_{x_{g}}(\vec{}q_{1\perp})\iint\uddc\vec{}q_{2\perp}\mathcal{F}_{x_{g}}(\vec{}q_{2\perp})\tilde{}\mathcal{H}^{(n)}_{2ji}(C_{0}\vec{}k_{\perp}-\vec{}q_{1\perp}-\vec{}q_{2\perp})\end{multline}\iint\frac{\uddc\vec{}r_{\perp}}{(2\pi)^{2}}\bigl[S_{x_{g}}^{(2)}(\vec{}r_{\perp})\bigr]^{2}e^{-iC_{0}\vec{}k_{\perp}\cdot\vec{}r_{\perp}}\hat{}\mathcal{H}^{(n)}_{2ji}(r_{\perp})\\
=\frac{1}{S_{\perp}^{2}}\iint\uddc\vec{}q_{1\perp}\mathcal{F}_{x_{g}}(\vec{}q_{1\perp})\iint\uddc\vec{}q_{2\perp}\mathcal{F}_{x_{g}}(\vec{}q_{2\perp})\tilde{}\mathcal{H}^{(n)}_{2ji}(C_{0}\vec{}k_{\perp}-\vec{}q_{1\perp}-\vec{}q_{2\perp})or so on. In general, there will be as many factors of $\mathcal{F}_{x_{g}}$ as there were of $S_{x_{g}}^{(2)}$, and an extra 2D integration for every $\mathcal{F}_{x_{g}}$ beyond the first.
\par In the end, we'll get an expression for the contribution to the cross section of the form:
\begin{multline}\biggl(\frac{\udddc\sigma}{\udc Y\uddc\vec{}p_{\perp}}\biggr)_{\text{dip}}=\int_{\tau}^{1}\udc z\int_{\tau/z}^{1}\udc\xi\iint\uddc\vec{}q_{\perp}\biggl[\frac{F^{(n)}_{s2ji}(z,\xi,\vec{}q_{\perp})-F^{(n)}_{s2ji}(z,1,\vec{}q_{\perp})}{1-\xi}+F^{(n)}_{n2ji}(z,\xi,\vec{}q_{\perp})\biggr]\\
+\int_{\tau}^{1}\udc z\iint\uddc\vec{}q_{\perp}\biggl[F^{(n)}_{s2ji}(z,1,\vec{}q_{\perp})\ln\biggl(1-\frac{\tau}{z}\biggr)+F^{(n)}_{d2ji}(z,1,\vec{}q_{\perp})\biggr]\end{multline}\biggl(\frac{\udddc\sigma}{\udc Y\uddc\vec{}p_{\perp}}\biggr)_{\text{dip}}=\int_{\tau}^{1}\udc z\int_{\tau/z}^{1}\udc\xi\iint\uddc\vec{}q_{\perp}\biggl[\frac{F^{(n)}_{s2ji}(z,\xi,\vec{}q_{\perp})-F^{(n)}_{s2ji}(z,1,\vec{}q_{\perp})}{1-\xi}+F^{(n)}_{n2ji}(z,\xi,\vec{}q_{\perp})\biggr]\\
+\int_{\tau}^{1}\udc z\iint\uddc\vec{}q_{\perp}\biggl[F^{(n)}_{s2ji}(z,1,\vec{}q_{\perp})\ln\biggl(1-\frac{\tau}{z}\biggr)+F^{(n)}_{d2ji}(z,1,\vec{}q_{\perp})\biggr]where
\lx@equationgroup@subnumbering@begin
\@@amsalign
\lx@equationgroup@subnumbering@end
As mentioned, the number of momentum variables involved depends on how many factors of $S_{x_{g}}^{(2)}$ there are.
\par The advantage of this formulation is that, in momentum space, $F^{(n)}_{s2ji}$, $F^{(n)}_{n2ji}$, and $F^{(n)}_{d2ji}$ don't oscillate.
General-purpose integration algorithms can be wildly inaccurate when applied to oscillating functions.
Although specialized algorithms~\cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{Levin1996,Ooura1999,Inverarity2002}{}{}]} do exist for performing Fourier-type integrals, it's still easier and more accurate to use equation~\eqref{eq:dipolemasterformulamomentum} so we can work with a relatively smooth, non-oscillatory integrand.
\par However, it's not always possible to express a given term in momentum space. Unless the Fourier transform of $\hat{}\mathcal{H}^{(n)}_{2ji}$~\eqref{eq:hardfactorfouriertransform} can be computed symbolically, we won't have a momentum-space expression to use. One could compute that Fourier transform numerically, but that's no different from what we do in the radial position-space representation.
\par Furthermore, in deriving equation~\eqref{eq:masterformula2momentum} we haven't really \emph{eliminated} the Fourier factor; we've just ``shifted'' it into the Fourier transform of the dipole gluon distribution, equation~\eqref{eq:dipoledistributionfouriertransform}. We still need to compute $\mathcal{F}_{x_{g}}$ from $S_{x_{g}}^{(2)}$ before calculating the cross section with equation~\eqref{eq:dipolemasterformulamomentum}. Still, the Fourier transform of $S_{x_{g}}^{(2)}$ tends to be ``better behaved'' than the hard factor $\mathcal{H}^{(n)}_{2ji}$. $S_{x_{g}}^{(2)}$ is a relatively smooth function which generally takes a roughly Gaussian form, and in fact under the assumption of angular independence, we can perform one integration symbolically:
\begin{equation}\mathcal{F}_{x_{g}}(\vec{}k_{\perp})=S_{\perp}\iint\uddc\vec{}r_{\perp}e^{-i\vec{}k_{\perp}\cdot\vec{}r_{\perp}}S_{x_{g}}^{(2)}(\vec{}r_{\perp})=2\pi S_{\perp}\int_{0}^{\infty}\udc r_{\perp}r_{\perp}J_{0}(k_{\perp}r_{\perp})S_{x_{g}}^{(2)}(\vec{}r_{\perp})\end{equation}This is still an oscillatory integral, but a one-dimensional one, so standard methods are much better able to handle it than they would a more complicated multidimensional integral.
\par In fact, it's possible to compute the solution to the BK equation in momentum space directly. Theoretically we should be able to use that in equations~\eqref{eq:masterformula2momentum}, but as described in section~\ref {sec:bknumerics}, there are some difficulties ensuring that the solutions in momentum space and position space are consistent.
\par\par\lx@frontmatter@fallback\@@unnumbered@section{subsubsection}{toc}{Quadrupole-Type Terms}
The other terms in~\cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{Chirilli:2012jd}{}{}]} are quadrupole-type, which involve integrals over three transverse position coordinates, $\vec{}x_{\perp}$, $\vec{}b_{\perp}$, and $\vec{}y_{\perp}$. These terms can be written
\begin{multline}\biggl(\frac{\udddc\sigma}{\udc Y\uddc\vec{p}_{\perp}}\biggr)_{4ji}^{(n)}=\biggl(\frac{\alpha_{s}}{2\pi}\biggr)^{n}\int\frac{\udc z\udc\xi}{z^{2}}x_{p}f_{i}(x_{p},\mu)D_{\mathit{{h}{}_{\mspace{-3mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{-1mu}\scriptstyle{}}}/j}(z,\mu)\int\frac{\uddc\vec{}x_{\perp}\uddc\vec{}b_{\perp}\uddc\vec{}y_{\perp}}{(2\pi)^{4}}\\
\times\mathcal{S}(\vec{}x_{\perp},\vec{}b_{\perp},\vec{}y_{\perp})e^{-iC_{1}\vec{}k_{\perp}\cdot(\vec{}x_{\perp}-\vec{}b_{\perp})}e^{-iC_{2}\vec{}k_{\perp}\cdot(\vec{}y_{\perp}-\vec{}b_{\perp})}\hat{}\mathcal{H}^{(n)}_{4ji}(\vec{}x_{\perp},\vec{}b_{\perp},\vec{}y_{\perp})\end{multline}\biggl(\frac{\udddc\sigma}{\udc Y\uddc\vec{p}_{\perp}}\biggr)_{4ji}^{(n)}=\biggl(\frac{\alpha_{s}}{2\pi}\biggr)^{n}\int\frac{\udc z\udc\xi}{z^{2}}x_{p}f_{i}(x_{p},\mu)D_{\mathit{{h}{}_{\mspace{-3mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{-1mu}\scriptstyle{}}}/j}(z,\mu)\int\frac{\uddc\vec{}x_{\perp}\uddc\vec{}b_{\perp}\uddc\vec{}y_{\perp}}{(2\pi)^{4}}\\
\times\mathcal{S}(\vec{}x_{\perp},\vec{}b_{\perp},\vec{}y_{\perp})e^{-iC_{1}\vec{}k_{\perp}\cdot(\vec{}x_{\perp}-\vec{}b_{\perp})}e^{-iC_{2}\vec{}k_{\perp}\cdot(\vec{}y_{\perp}-\vec{}b_{\perp})}\hat{}\mathcal{H}^{(n)}_{4ji}(\vec{}x_{\perp},\vec{}b_{\perp},\vec{}y_{\perp})where $C_{1}$ and $C_{2}$ are, like $C_{0}$, coefficients independent of position and momentum.
For this type of term, the hard factor $\mathcal{H}^{(n)}_{4ji}$ as given in~\cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{Chirilli:2012jd}{}{}]} corresponds to the combination
\begin{equation}\mathcal{H}^{(n)}_{4ji}=e^{-iC_{1}\vec{}k_{\perp}\cdot(\vec{}x_{\perp}-\vec{}b_{\perp})}e^{-iC_{2}\vec{}k_{\perp}\cdot(\vec{}y_{\perp}-\vec{}b_{\perp})}\hat{}\mathcal{H}^{(n)}_{4ji}\end{equation}Handling these terms proceeds along the same general lines as the dipole-type terms, with a few differences to account for the additional coordinates. First we change variables to
\@@amsalign
and $\vec{}r_{\perp}=\vec{}s_{\perp}-\vec{}t_{\perp}$. As in the dipole case, $\hat{}\mathcal{H}^{(n)}_{4ji}$ is independent of $\vec{R}_{\perp}$ so we can integrate over that degree of freedom.
\begin{multline}\biggl(\frac{\udddc\sigma}{\udc Y\uddc\vec{p}_{\perp}}\biggr)_{4ji}^{(n)}=S_{\perp}\biggl(\frac{\alpha_{s}}{2\pi}\biggr)^{n}\int\frac{\udc z\udc\xi}{z^{2}}x_{p}f_{i}(x_{p},\mu)D_{\mathit{{h}{}_{\mspace{-3mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{-1mu}\scriptstyle{}}}/j}(z,\mu)\int\frac{\uddc\vec{}s_{\perp}\uddc\vec{}t_{\perp}}{(2\pi)^{4}}\\
\times\mathcal{S}(\vec{}s_{\perp},\vec{}t_{\perp})e^{-iC_{1}\vec{}k_{\perp}\cdot\vec{}s_{\perp}}e^{-iC_{2}\vec{}k_{\perp}\cdot\vec{}t_{\perp}}\hat{}\mathcal{H}^{(n)}_{4ji}(\vec{}s_{\perp},\vec{}t_{\perp})\end{multline}\biggl(\frac{\udddc\sigma}{\udc Y\uddc\vec{p}_{\perp}}\biggr)_{4ji}^{(n)}=S_{\perp}\biggl(\frac{\alpha_{s}}{2\pi}\biggr)^{n}\int\frac{\udc z\udc\xi}{z^{2}}x_{p}f_{i}(x_{p},\mu)D_{\mathit{{h}{}_{\mspace{-3mu}\scriptstyle{}}^{\mspace{-1mu}\scriptstyle{}}}/j}(z,\mu)\int\frac{\uddc\vec{}s_{\perp}\uddc\vec{}t_{\perp}}{(2\pi)^{4}}\\
\times\mathcal{S}(\vec{}s_{\perp},\vec{}t_{\perp})e^{-iC_{1}\vec{}k_{\perp}\cdot\vec{}s_{\perp}}e^{-iC_{2}\vec{}k_{\perp}\cdot\vec{}t_{\perp}}\hat{}\mathcal{H}^{(n)}_{4ji}(\vec{}s_{\perp},\vec{}t_{\perp})\par Now, $\mathcal{S}\hat{}\mathcal{H}^{(n)}_{4ji}$ is a scalar, so it depends on $\vec{}s_{\perp}$ and $\vec{}t_{\perp}$ through scalar functions of those variables: $s_{\perp}^{2}$, $t_{\perp}^{2}$, and $\vec{}s_{\perp}\cdot\vec{}t_{\perp}=\frac{1}{2}(s_{\perp}^{2}+t_{\perp}^{2}-r_{\perp}^{2})$. This means we can't integrate over the angular variables of both $\vec{}s_{\perp}$ and $\vec{}t_{\perp}$, for the following reason. In Cartesian position space, $\mathcal{S}\hat{}\mathcal{H}^{(n)}_{4ji}$ is written as a function of four variables, $s_{x\perp}$, $s_{y\perp}$, $t_{x\perp}$, and $t_{y\perp}$. We can express it as a function of three variables, $r_{\perp}$, $s_{\perp}$, and $t_{\perp}$, but not two. So we can only hope to integrate over one angle. In principle, we could do that integration, but we're not going to have a simple substitution rule to do so, as we did with the dipole-type terms (equation~\eqref{eq:maptoradial}).
\par In practice, all the quadrupole-type terms in~\cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{Chirilli:2012jd}{}{}]} can be expressed in momentum space, so it's not worth the effort to find the radial representation at all.\par\par\lx@frontmatter@fallback\@@unnumbered@section{paragraph}{toc}{Cartesian position space}
As with the dipole terms, we can practically copy the Cartesian position space formula from reference~\cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{Chirilli:2012jd}{}{}]}.
\lx@equationgroup@subnumbering@begin
\@@amsalign
\lx@equationgroup@subnumbering@end
and by analogy with equation~\eqref{eq:dipolemasterformula}, the term becomes
\begin{multline}\biggl(\frac{\udddc\sigma}{\udc Y\uddc\vec{p}_{\perp}}\biggr)_{4ji}^{(n)}=\int_{\tau}^{1}\udc z\int_{\tau/z}^{1}\udc\xi\int\uddc\vec{}s_{\perp}\uddc\vec{}t_{\perp}\\
\times\biggl[\frac{F^{(n)}_{s4ji}(z,\xi,\vec{}s_{\perp},\vec{}t_{\perp})-F^{(n)}_{s4ji}(z,1,\vec{}s_{\perp},\vec{}t_{\perp})}{1-\xi}+F^{(n)}_{n4ji}(z,\xi,\vec{}s_{\perp},\vec{}t_{\perp})\biggr]\\
+\int_{\tau}^{1}\udc z\int\uddc\vec{}s_{\perp}\uddc\vec{}t_{\perp}\biggl[F^{(n)}_{s4ji}(z,1,\vec{}s_{\perp},\vec{}t_{\perp})\ln\biggl(1-\frac{\tau}{z}\biggr)+F^{(n)}_{d4ji}(z,1,\vec{}s_{\perp},\vec{}t_{\perp})\biggr]\end{multline}\biggl(\frac{\udddc\sigma}{\udc Y\uddc\vec{p}_{\perp}}\biggr)_{4ji}^{(n)}=\int_{\tau}^{1}\udc z\int_{\tau/z}^{1}\udc\xi\int\uddc\vec{}s_{\perp}\uddc\vec{}t_{\perp}\\
\times\biggl[\frac{F^{(n)}_{s4ji}(z,\xi,\vec{}s_{\perp},\vec{}t_{\perp})-F^{(n)}_{s4ji}(z,1,\vec{}s_{\perp},\vec{}t_{\perp})}{1-\xi}+F^{(n)}_{n4ji}(z,\xi,\vec{}s_{\perp},\vec{}t_{\perp})\biggr]\\
+\int_{\tau}^{1}\udc z\int\uddc\vec{}s_{\perp}\uddc\vec{}t_{\perp}\biggl[F^{(n)}_{s4ji}(z,1,\vec{}s_{\perp},\vec{}t_{\perp})\ln\biggl(1-\frac{\tau}{z}\biggr)+F^{(n)}_{d4ji}(z,1,\vec{}s_{\perp},\vec{}t_{\perp})\biggr]\par\par\lx@frontmatter@fallback\@@unnumbered@section{paragraph}{toc}{Momentum space}
Also by analogy with the dipole terms, we can Fourier transform quadrupole-type terms into momentum space.
The resulting expression includes the momentum space hard factor:
\begin{equation}\tilde{}\mathcal{H}^{(n)}_{4ji}(k_{\perp})=\iint\frac{\uddc\vec{}s_{\perp}}{(2\pi)^{2}}\iint\frac{\uddc\vec{}t_{\perp}}{(2\pi)^{2}}e^{-i\vec{}k_{\perp}\cdot\vec{}s_{\perp}}e^{-i\vec{}k_{\perp}\cdot\vec{}t_{\perp}}\hat{}\mathcal{H}^{(n)}_{4ji}(\vec{}s_{\perp},\vec{}t_{\perp})\end{equation}and either $\mathcal{G}_{x_{g}}$ or factors of $\mathcal{F}_{x_{g}}$, depending on the gluon distribution in each individual term.
As a straightforward example, the structure
\begin{equation}\int\frac{\uddc\vec{}s_{\perp}}{(2\pi)^{2}}\frac{\uddc\vec{}t_{\perp}}{(2\pi)^{2}}S_{x_{g}}^{(4)}(\vec{}s_{\perp},\vec{}t_{\perp})e^{-iC_{1}\vec{}k_{\perp}\cdot\vec{}s_{\perp}}e^{-iC_{2}\vec{}k_{\perp}\cdot\vec{}t_{\perp}}\hat{}\mathcal{H}^{(n)}_{4ji}(\vec{}s_{\perp},\vec{}t_{\perp})\end{equation}becomes
\begin{equation}\int\frac{\uddc\vec{}q_{1\perp}}{(2\pi)^{2}}\frac{\uddc\vec{}q_{2\perp}}{(2\pi)^{2}}\mathcal{G}_{x_{g}}(\vec{}q_{1\perp},\vec{}q_{2\perp})\tilde{}\mathcal{H}^{(n)}_{4ji}(\vec{}q_{1\perp}-C_{1}\vec{}k_{\perp},\vec{}q_{2\perp}-C_{2}\vec{}k_{\perp})\end{equation}Of course the exact nature of the expression varies significantly from term to term.
\par In the end the contribution of a quadrupole-type term takes the form
\begin{multline}\biggl(\frac{\udddc\sigma}{\udc Y\uddc\vec{}p_{\perp}}\biggr)_{\text{quad}}=\int_{\tau}^{1}\udc z\int_{\tau/z}^{1}\udc\xi\int\uddc\vec{}q_{1\perp}\uddc\vec{}q_{2\perp}\\
\times\biggl[\frac{F^{(n)}_{s4ji}(z,\xi,\vec{}q_{1\perp},\vec{}q_{2\perp})-F^{(n)}_{s4ji}(z,1,\vec{}q_{1\perp},\vec{}q_{2\perp})}{1-\xi}+F^{(n)}_{n4ji}(z,\xi,\vec{}q_{1\perp},\vec{}q_{2\perp})\biggr]\\
+\int_{\tau}^{1}\udc z\int\uddc\vec{}q_{1\perp}\uddc\vec{}q_{2\perp}\biggl[F^{(n)}_{s4ji}(z,1,\vec{}q_{1\perp},\vec{}q_{2\perp})\ln\biggl(1-\frac{\tau}{z}\biggr)+F^{(n)}_{d4ji}(z,1,\vec{}q_{1\perp},\vec{}q_{2\perp})\biggr]\end{multline}\biggl(\frac{\udddc\sigma}{\udc Y\uddc\vec{}p_{\perp}}\biggr)_{\text{quad}}=\int_{\tau}^{1}\udc z\int_{\tau/z}^{1}\udc\xi\int\uddc\vec{}q_{1\perp}\uddc\vec{}q_{2\perp}\\
\times\biggl[\frac{F^{(n)}_{s4ji}(z,\xi,\vec{}q_{1\perp},\vec{}q_{2\perp})-F^{(n)}_{s4ji}(z,1,\vec{}q_{1\perp},\vec{}q_{2\perp})}{1-\xi}+F^{(n)}_{n4ji}(z,\xi,\vec{}q_{1\perp},\vec{}q_{2\perp})\biggr]\\
+\int_{\tau}^{1}\udc z\int\uddc\vec{}q_{1\perp}\uddc\vec{}q_{2\perp}\biggl[F^{(n)}_{s4ji}(z,1,\vec{}q_{1\perp},\vec{}q_{2\perp})\ln\biggl(1-\frac{\tau}{z}\biggr)+F^{(n)}_{d4ji}(z,1,\vec{}q_{1\perp},\vec{}q_{2\perp})\biggr]with
\lx@equationgroup@subnumbering@begin
\@@amsalign
\lx@equationgroup@subnumbering@end
As mentioned, the number of momentum variables involved depends on how many factors of $S_{x_{g}}^{(2)}$ there are.
\par\par\lx@frontmatter@fallback\@@unnumbered@section{section}{toc}{Summary of Terms}
\par The formulas in reference~\cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{Chirilli:2012jd}{}{}]} generically take the form
\begin{equation}\frac{\udddc\sigma}{\udc Y\uddc\vec{}p_{\perp}}=x_{p}f(x_{p},\mu)\otimes\mathcal{S}\otimes\mathcal{H}\otimes D(z,\mu^{2})\end{equation}where $\mathcal{S}$ is some combination of dipole or quadrupole gluon distributions, and $\mathcal{H}$ is a hard factor, representing the perturbative part of the calculation. It's straightforward to extract the Cartesian position space formulas from the expressions given in~\cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{Chirilli:2012jd}{}{}]}, but an accurate numerical implementation requires the radial or momentum space formulas, which need to be derived. The purpose of this section is to show those derivations.
\par Table~\ref {tbl:hardfactorformulas} summarizes which equations give the contribution corresponding to each hard factor in each implementation, for easy reference.
\par\begin{table}\begin{tabularx}{345.0pt}[]{lXXX}\hline\cr\hline\cr Term&Cartesian&Radial&Momentum\\
\hline\cr$\mathcal{H}^{(0)}_{2qq}$&\eqref{eq:h02qq:cartesian:xsec} with \eqref{eq:h02qq:cartesian:Fd}&&\eqref{eq:h02qq:momentum:xsec} with \eqref{eq:h02qq:momentum:Fd}\\
$\mathcal{H}^{(1)}_{2qq}$&\eqref{eq:h12qq:cartesian:xsec} with \eqref{eq:h12qq:cartesian:Fs}, \eqref{eq:h12qq:cartesian:Fd}&\eqref{eq:h12qq:radial:xsec} with \eqref{eq:h12qq:radial:Fs}, \eqref{eq:h12qq:radial:Fd}&\\
$\mathcal{H}^{(1)}_{4qq}$&\eqref{eq:h14qq:cartesian:xsec} \newline
with \eqref{eq:h14qq:cartesian:Fs}, \eqref{eq:h14qq:cartesian:Fd:quadrupole}, \eqref{eq:h14qq:cartesian:Fd:dipole}&&\eqref{eq:h14qq:momentum:xsec} \newline
with \eqref{eq:h14qq:momentum:Fs}, \eqref{eq:h14qq:momentum:Fd}\\
